Kubernetes and Cloud Native Associate (KCNA)

Complete Test Preparation — Pass the KCNA & Beyond. 200 MCQs covering all 4 KCNA domains. From Novice → Cloud Native Practitioner → Beyond. Built with anihpj/jobpost.

📝
200MCQs

🏠 Master SummaryOVERVIEW

🎯 What This Document Is

Your complete KCNA exam preparation system — 200 multiple-choice questions across all 4 CNCF domains, each with detailed explanations, ASCII diagrams, and real-world context from anihpj/jobpost (Django + PostgreSQL + Redis) running on your 6-cluster Kubernetes lab. Zero prior knowledge required.

📖 Start here: Document Overview → for study strategy, topic priorities, navigation guide, and exam facts.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "Why is my pod stuck in Pending?" — No idea where to look
  • ❌ "The Service works but returns 503" — Don't know about selectors
  • ❌ "I updated the ConfigMap but nothing changed" — Didn't know pods need restart
  • ❌ "kubectl commands take forever" — Never checked etcd or API server health
  • ❌ "The node is down but I can't drain it" — PDB concept unknown
  • ❌ "I ran commands on the wrong cluster" — No context discipline
  • ❌ "etcd backup? What's etcd?" — No disaster recovery plan

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Diagnose any pod failure in <2 min using systematic Tenet Flow
  • ✅ Fix Service/NetworkPolicy/DNS issues using endpoint & selector checks
  • ✅ Trigger rolling restarts, rollbacks, and canary deployments confidently
  • ✅ Monitor etcd health, compact & defrag, restore from snapshots
  • ✅ Cordon → Drain → Work → Uncordon with PDB awareness
  • ✅ Switch contexts safely and verify before every destructive command
  • ✅ Backup/restore etcd, renew TLS certs, upgrade clusters without downtime

⚔️ Exam Day Battle Plan — The First 5 Minutes & Beyond

⏱️ 0-2 min
Setup
alias k=kubectl
export KUBECONFIG=...
Verify context
⏱️ 2-5 min
Scan
Read ALL 17 tasks
Mark as Easy/Med/Hard
Identify highest-weight
⏱️ 5-90 min
Execute
Easy first (build confidence)
Then medium, then hard
Use --dry-run -o yaml
⏱️ 90-120 min
Verify
Re-check each answer
Test from debug pod
Leave nothing blank
🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The Exam
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl create ... --dry-run=client -o yaml to generate YAML in seconds — never type manifests from scratch.
  • ✅ DO: Run kubectl config use-context <given-context> as the FIRST command for every task. Wrong context = 0 points.
  • ✅ DO: Use kubectl explain <resource> --recursive when you forget a field name. It's faster than searching docs.
  • ✅ DO: Create a debug pod: kubectl run test --rm -it --image=busybox -- sh to test DNS, connectivity, and curl from inside the cluster.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 7 minutes on any single task. Flag it, move on, return with fresh eyes.
  • ❌ DON'T: Forget to set the namespace. Many tasks require -n flag. Use kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=<ns> to save typing.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any task blank. Even partial work (creating the namespace, writing partial YAML) may earn partial credit.
  • ❌ DON'T: Use kubectl delete pod on a static pod — you must delete the manifest file from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

KCNA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple-choice (60 questions) | Passing Score: 75% | Cost: $250 | Certification Validity: 3 years | Prerequisites: None — this is the entry-level CNCF certification!

4 Official Domains: Kubernetes Fundamentals (44%), Container Orchestration (28%), Cloud Native Application Delivery (16%), Cloud Native Architecture (12%). Kubernetes Fundamentals is the heaviest — focus your study time accordingly.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (5 min) → Answer easy questions first → Flag and return to hard ones → Use process of elimination on tough MCQs → Review flagged questions in the last 10 minutes. The KCNA tests conceptual understanding — not hands-on CLI skills.

Your 6-cluster lab (below) is for hands-on practice to reinforce concepts. Use ksw to switch between clusters.

📚 Pre-Learning: Cloud Native FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Cloud Native Mental Model

F.1 What Is Cloud Native & Why Does It Matter? — Traditional IT → Virtualization → Cloud → Cloud Native. The 5 Pillars: Containers, Orchestration, Microservices, Observability, CI/CD. Real-world: Netflix, Spotify, Uber.

F.2 Containers 101 — Docker & OCI — What IS a container? Dockerfile anatomy, image layers, registries. Multi-stage builds and distroless images for production.

F.3 Kubernetes Core Concepts — The "Big Picture" mental model: Cluster = Control Plane + Worker Nodes. Pods, declarative vs imperative, desired state reconciliation, kubectl basics.

F.4 The CNCF Landscape & Ecosystem — The 1,000+ project landscape map. Graduated (Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, Fluentd), Incubating (Argo, Cilium, Knative), Sandbox projects. The CNCF Trail Map.

F.5 The anihpj/jobpost Application Stack — Web tier (Django templates), API tier (Django REST Framework), Database tier (PostgreSQL 15), Redis (Celery + caching). Service discovery via DNS. Complete file tree and Dockerfile walkthrough.

If you're new to cloud native: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the MCQs. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example. Zero prior Kubernetes knowledge required!

🎯 How to Use This Guide — Study Strategy

📝 Per Question Method
1. Read the question carefully
2. Answer before seeing the solution
3. Toggle to reveal the explanation
4. Study the ASCII diagram — it encodes the concept visually
5. Note the KCNA key takeaway at the bottom
6. Connect to anihpj/jobpost context
📅 Daily Study Routine
Morning (30 min): 5-10 MCQs + review explanations
Afternoon (45 min): Review missed questions, study diagrams
Evening (60 min): 10 new MCQs, simulate exam conditions
Weekend (2-3 hrs): Timed mini-mock (50 Qs in 60 min)

🚀 Quick Reference — What You'll Learn

Cat 1 — K8s Fundamentals: Containers, Pods, Deployments, StatefulSets, ConfigMaps, Secrets, Scheduling, Cluster Architecture, kubeadm, kubectl, Namespaces, QoS
Cat 2 — Container Orchestration: Services, Ingress, NetworkPolicy, RBAC, Pod Security, TLS, Probes, Logging, PV/PVC, StorageClass, Service Mesh, Helm, Kustomize
Cat 3 — App Delivery: CI/CD, GitOps (Argo CD, Flux), Helm Charts, Kustomize Overlays, Deployment Strategies (Rolling, Blue/Green, Canary), Debugging
Cat 4 — Architecture: Prometheus, Grafana, Fluentd, OpenTelemetry, CNCF Ecosystem, 12-Factor App, Serverless, FinOps, KubeCon, Career Path

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Kubernetes Core Concepts, Administration, Scheduling & Containerization

This is the biggest category — 44% of the exam. It covers the four pillars: Kubernetes Core Concepts (Pods, Deployments, Services), Administration (kubeadm, cluster management, etcd backup/restore), Scheduling (labels, affinity, taints), and Containerization (OCI images, Dockerfiles, runtimes). For anihpj/jobpost, this means understanding how the Django API, PostgreSQL database, and Redis cache are packaged, deployed, and managed on Kubernetes.

1.1 Containers & Container ImagesQ1-Q10

K8s Fundamentals Q1 of 200
What is a container? Choose the most accurate description of how containers work in Linux.
A) A lightweight virtual machine that runs its own guest operating system kernel
B) A process isolated using Linux namespaces and constrained by cgroups — it shares the host kernel, NOT a mini VM
C) A compressed archive of application files that runs in a sandboxed interpreter
D) A hardware-level virtualization technology that partitions CPU and memory for each application
✅ Correct Answer: B

A container is a process — not a VM. It uses Linux namespaces to isolate what the process can see (PID, network, mount, UTS, IPC, user, cgroup namespaces) and cgroups (control groups) to limit what resources it can use (CPU, memory, disk I/O, PIDs).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container vs VM — Sharing the Kernel vs Running a Full OS
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     CONTAINER                           │
  │  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐                │
  │  │  App A  │  │  App B  │  │  App C  │                │
  │  │(anihpj) │  │(postgres)│  │ (redis) │                │
  │  ├─────────┤  ├─────────┤  ├─────────┤                │
  │  │ Bins/Libs│  │ Bins/Libs│  │ Bins/Libs│               │
  │  └─────────┘  └─────────┘  └─────────┘                │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │           CONTAINER RUNTIME (containerd)        │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │              HOST LINUX KERNEL                  │  │
  │  │     (shared by ALL containers — NO guest OS!)   │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │              HOST HARDWARE (CPU, RAM, DISK)     │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                   VIRTUAL MACHINE                       │
  │  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐                              │
  │  │  App A  │  │  App B  │                              │
  │  ├─────────┤  ├─────────┤                              │
  │  │ Bins/Libs│  │ Bins/Libs│                              │
  │  ├─────────┤  ├─────────┤                              │
  │  │ Guest OS│  │ Guest OS│  ← Each VM has its OWN kernel │
  │  ├─────────┤  ├─────────┤                              │
  │  │         HYPERVISOR         │                         │
  │  ├───────────────────────────┤                         │
  │  │      HOST HARDWARE        │                         │
  │  └───────────────────────────┘                         │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY DIFFERENCE: Containers share the host kernel (millisecond start,
  MBs of RAM). VMs each boot a full OS (seconds to start, GBs of RAM).

This is why containers start in milliseconds and use megabytes of RAM — there's no OS to boot. Docker, containerd, and CRI-O all use runc (the OCI reference runtime) under the hood, which creates the namespaces + cgroups for each container. For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API, PostgreSQL, and Redis each run in separate containers within their own namespaces, sharing the same Kubernetes node kernel.

K8s Fundamentals Q2 of 200
What is the OCI (Open Container Initiative) and what does it standardize?
A) A Linux kernel module that enables container isolation through namespaces and cgroups
B) A Kubernetes API group that defines how pods communicate with container runtimes
C) An open governance structure that maintains three specifications: image-spec (how images are built), runtime-spec (how containers run), and distribution-spec (how images are shared via registries)
D) A Docker-specific file format (.oci) used to define multi-container applications for deployment
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Open Container Initiative (OCI) is a Linux Foundation project that creates open industry standards for containers. It defines three specifications: image-spec (how container images are packaged — layers, manifests, configs), runtime-spec (how containers are executed — the lifecycle from creation to deletion, implemented by runc), and distribution-spec (how container images are pushed/pulled between registries — the HTTP API that Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, and Harbor all implement).

📝 Detailed Explanation
The Three OCI Specifications — Making Containers Portable
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    OCI (Open Container Initiative)               │
  │                        Linux Foundation Project                   │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐ │
  │  │   IMAGE-SPEC     │  │  RUNTIME-SPEC    │  │DISTRIBUTION-  │ │
  │  │                  │  │                  │  │    SPEC       │ │
  │  │ "What IS a       │  │ "How do you RUN  │  │ "How do you   │ │
│  │  container image?" │  │  a container?"   │  │ SHARE images?"│ │
  │  ├──────────────────┤  ├──────────────────┤  ├───────────────┤ │
  │  │ • Layer format   │  │ • Container      │  │ • Push/Pull   │ │
  │  │   (tar+gzip)     │  │   lifecycle      │  │   HTTP API    │ │
  │  │ • Manifest JSON  │  │   (create→start  │  │ • Registry    │ │
  │  │ • Config JSON    │  │    →stop→delete) │  │   protocol    │ │
  │  │ • Filesystem     │  │ • Namespaces     │  │ • Auth tokens │ │
  │  │   bundle         │  │ • Cgroups        │  │ • Tags/labels │ │
  │  │                  │  │ • Rootfs         │  │               │ │
  │  ├──────────────────┤  ├──────────────────┤  ├───────────────┤ │
  │  │ Implemented by:  │  │ Implemented by:  │  │ Implemented   │ │
  │  │ Docker, Podman,  │  │ runc, crun,      │  │ by: Docker    │ │
  │  │ Buildah, Kaniko  │  │ containerd,      │  │ Hub, ECR,     │ │
  │  │                  │  │ gVisor, Kata     │  │ GCR, Harbor   │ │
  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘  └───────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Before OCI, Docker was the de facto standard. When Kubernetes wanted to support other runtimes (like containerd and CRI-O), the industry needed a vendor-neutral standard. OCI was created in 2015 specifically so any OCI-compliant image runs on any OCI-compliant runtime. This is why you can docker build an image and run it with containerd on Kubernetes — they both follow OCI specs. For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API image built with Docker follows OCI image-spec, so it runs identically on containerd in your kubeadm cluster.

K8s Fundamentals Q3 of 200
How do Dockerfile layers work, and why does the order of instructions matter?
A) Each instruction (FROM, RUN, COPY, etc.) creates a new read-only layer stacked on top of the previous one. Layers are cached — if a layer hasn't changed, Docker reuses it. This is why COPY requirements.txt before COPY . speeds up builds: dependency installation is cached when only source code changes
B) All Dockerfile instructions are merged into a single compressed layer at build time; the order of instructions has no effect on caching or image size
C) Each instruction creates a separate writable layer that persists after the container stops, allowing changes to be saved back to the image automatically
D) Layers are created only by RUN instructions; COPY and ADD instructions modify the existing layer in-place without creating new layers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every instruction in a Dockerfile produces a new read-only layer. These layers are stacked using a Union Filesystem (OverlayFS). Docker caches each layer — if a layer and all layers above it haven't changed, Docker reuses them from cache, dramatically speeding up rebuilds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Dockerfile Layer Caching — Why Order Matters
  ❌ BAD ORDER (slow rebuilds — cache invalidated early):
  FROM python:3.12-slim          ← Layer 1 (rarely changes)
  COPY . /app                    ← Layer 2 ❌ changes every code edit!
  RUN pip install -r requirements.txt  ← Layer 3 MUST rebuild
  CMD ["gunicorn", "..."]        ← Layer 4
  → Every code change invalidates Layer 2, so Layer 3 ALWAYS reruns

  ✅ GOOD ORDER (fast rebuilds — cache preserved):
  FROM python:3.12-slim          ← Layer 1 (cached)
  COPY requirements.txt /app/    ← Layer 2 ✅ only changes when deps change
  RUN pip install -r /app/requirements.txt  ← Layer 3 ✅ cached!
  COPY . /app                    ← Layer 4 ← only THIS changes on code edits
  CMD ["gunicorn", "..."]        ← Layer 5 (cached)
  → Code edits only invalidate Layer 4. Layers 1-3 come from cache!

  LAYER CACHING RULES:
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. If a layer changes → ALL subsequent layers must rebuild │
  │ 2. Put SLOW-CHANGING instructions FIRST (OS, deps)         │
  │ 3. Put FAST-CHANGING instructions LAST (source code)       │
  │ 4. This is why COPY requirements.txt BEFORE COPY .         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the optimized Dockerfile puts COPY requirements.txt and RUN pip install before COPY . /app. When a developer changes the Django views but not the dependencies, only the final COPY layer rebuilds — saving 2-3 minutes per build. This is also why images share layers: if 10 microservices all use FROM python:3.12-slim, that base layer is stored ONCE on disk.

K8s Fundamentals Q4 of 200
What is a multi-stage Docker build and why is it used?
A) A technique where multiple Dockerfiles are combined at runtime to create a single running container
B) A Dockerfile with multiple FROM statements — each starts a new stage. The first stage(s) build/compile the application, and the final stage copies ONLY the needed artifacts, producing a minimal runtime image without build tools, compilers, or source code
C) A Docker Compose feature that orchestrates multiple containers (web, database, cache) in a single build pipeline
D) A CI/CD pattern where the same image is deployed to dev, staging, and production stages with different environment variables
✅ Correct Answer: B

A multi-stage build uses multiple FROM statements in a single Dockerfile. Each FROM begins a new stage with a fresh base image. The build stage(s) contain compilers, SDKs, and all dependencies needed to compile the app. The final runtime stage uses COPY --from=builder to grab only the compiled artifacts — resulting in a much smaller, more secure image.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Stage Build — anihpj/jobpost Django Application
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    MULTI-STAGE DOCKER BUILD                      │
  │                                                                 │
  │  STAGE 1: BUILDER (1.2 GB)              STAGE 2: RUNTIME (150 MB)│
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ FROM python:3.12-slim    │          │ FROM gcr.io/distroless│ │
  │  │                          │          │   /python3-debian12   │ │
  │  │ 🔧 Build tools:          │          │   :nonroot            │ │
  │  │  • pip, setuptools       │          │                        │ │
  │  │  • gcc, g++, make        │  COPY    │ ✅ Runtime only:       │ │
  │  │  • git (pull deps)       │  ────→   │  • Python libs        │ │
  │  │                          │  --from  │  • Django app code    │ │
  │  │ 📦 RUN pip install -r    │  =builder│  • gunicorn binary    │ │
  │  │    requirements.txt      │          │                        │ │
  │  │                          │          │ ❌ NOT included:       │ │
  │  │ 📁 COPY . /app           │          │  • gcc, make, git      │ │
  │  │                          │          │  • pip, setuptools     │ │
  │  │ ⚙️  RUN python manage.py │          │  • source code (.py)   │ │
  │  │    collectstatic         │          │  • shell (/bin/sh)     │ │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘          └──────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  RESULT: 1.2 GB → 150 MB (87% smaller! No compilers, no shell)  │
  │  CVEs:    ~120   → ~15    (87% fewer vulnerabilities!)          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the multi-stage build reduces the production image from ~1.2 GB to ~150 MB by using gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot as the final base. This eliminates build tools, compilers, and even the shell — meaning an attacker who compromises the container can't run apt-get, curl, or even /bin/sh. Multi-stage builds are a KCNA exam essential because they embody two cloud native principles: minimal attack surface and immutable infrastructure.

K8s Fundamentals Q5 of 200
What is a container registry and which operations does it support?
A) A Kubernetes Custom Resource that stores container runtime configuration for worker nodes
B) A local directory on each Kubernetes node where kubelet caches downloaded container images
C) A database that stores running container process IDs and their namespace assignments for the container runtime
D) A service that stores and distributes container images via the OCI distribution-spec API. It supports docker push (upload), docker pull (download), and docker tag (alias). Examples: Docker Hub, AWS ECR, GCR, ACR, Harbor
✅ Correct Answer: D

A container registry is the "GitHub for container images" — it stores, versions, and distributes OCI-compliant container images. Every registry implements the OCI distribution-spec HTTP API, which standardizes the push (upload layers + manifest), pull (download layers by digest), and tag (create a human-readable alias for a digest) operations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Registry — The Image Distribution Pipeline
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEVELOPER LAPTOP             REGISTRY               KUBERNETES │
  │  ┌──────────────┐       ┌──────────────┐       ┌─────────────┐ │
  │  │ docker build │       │ Docker Hub   │       │ kubelet on  │ │
  │  │ -t anihpj    │ push  │ anihpj/      │ pull  │ node "anih" │ │
  │  │   /api:v2.1  │──────▶│   api:v2.1   │──────▶│ containerd  │ │
  │  │              │       │              │       │   pulls     │ │
  │  │ Layers:      │       │ Stored:      │       │   image     │ │
  │  │ [a] python   │       │ [a] sha:abc  │       │             │ │
  │  │ [b] deps     │       │ [b] sha:def  │       │ Pod runs    │ │
  │  │ [c] app code │       │ [c] sha:ghi  │       │ anihpj/api  │ │
  │  └──────────────┘       └──────────────┘       └─────────────┘ │
  │                                                                 │
  │  POPULAR REGISTRIES:                                             │
  │  ┌────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ Docker Hub │ Public: docker.io/library/*  (default)         │ │
  │  │ AWS ECR    │ Private per AWS account (IAM auth)             │ │
  │  │ GCR/GAR    │ Google Cloud: gcr.io / artifact registry       │ │
  │  │ ACR        │ Azure: *.azurecr.io (Entra ID auth)            │ │
  │  │ Harbor     │ CNCF-graduated, self-hosted, vulnerability     │ │
  │  │            │ scanning + image signing built-in               │ │
  │  └────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When Kubernetes schedules an anihpj/jobpost pod, the kubelet on the assigned node tells containerd (via CRI) to pull the image from the registry. containerd downloads only the layers it doesn't already have cached locally — since layers are content-addressed by SHA256 hash, if 10 pods all use python:3.12-slim, that base layer is downloaded once and shared. If you use a private registry like ECR, you must create an ImagePullSecret in Kubernetes so the kubelet can authenticate.

K8s Fundamentals Q6 of 200
Why is the "latest" tag considered dangerous in production, and what should you use instead?
A) The latest tag automatically updates containers without restarting pods, causing unpredictable application behavior at runtime
B) The latest tag is deprecated by Docker and will be removed in future versions; it has no functional meaning
C) latest is a mutable floating tag — it points to whatever image was most recently pushed. Different nodes can pull different images at different times, causing version mismatch across replicas. Use semantic versioning (v1.2.3) or immutable content digests (sha256:abc...) instead
D) The latest tag causes images to be automatically deleted from the registry after 30 days, leading to ImagePullBackOff errors in production
✅ Correct Answer: C

The latest tag is a mutable pointer — it's just a default tag applied when no tag is specified. It always resolves to the most recently pushed image with that name, regardless of version. In a Kubernetes cluster, pod-1 on node-A might pull anihpj/api:latest at 10:00 AM (getting v1.2), while pod-2 on node-B pulls the same tag at 10:05 AM (getting v1.3) — resulting in two different versions running simultaneously with no way to roll back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Image Tags — Why latest Breaks Production
  TIME     REGISTRY                    KUBERNETES CLUSTER
  ────     ────────                    ──────────────────
  10:00    anihpj/api:latest → v1.2    Pod-1 pulls :latest → gets v1.2 ✅
  10:03                                Pod-2 pulls :latest → gets v1.2 ✅
  10:05    anihpj/api:latest → v1.3    (tag now points to v1.3!)
  10:07                                Pod-3 pulls :latest → gets v1.3 ❌
  10:10                                Pod-1 restarts, pulls :latest →
                                       gets v1.3 ❌
  RESULT: 3 pods running 2 different versions. No way to roll back.

  ✅ USE SEMANTIC VERSIONING (immutable):
  anihpj/api:v1.2.0  → always points to v1.2.0  (can't change)
  anihpj/api:v1.3.0  → always points to v1.3.0  (can't change)
  → kubectl set image deploy/api api=anihpj/api:v1.3.0
  → kubectl rollout undo deploy/api  (rolls back to v1.2.0!)

  ✅ USE DIGESTS (cryptographically immutable):
  anihpj/api@sha256:abc123def456...
  → Guarantees you get the EXACT same bytes every time
  → Immune to tag mutation, registry compromise, or MITM attacks

For anihpj/jobpost, we tag images as anihpj/api:v1.2.3 using semantic versioning. In the Kubernetes Deployment, we specify image: anihpj/api:v1.2.3. Rolling updates use kubectl set image to change to v1.3.0, and if something breaks, kubectl rollout undo instantly reverts. This is a KCNA exam fundamental — the CNCF recommends immutable tags for all production workloads.

K8s Fundamentals Q7 of 200
What are distroless container images, and why are they more secure than traditional base images?
A) Distroless images contain ONLY the application and its runtime dependencies — no shell, no package manager, no utilities. They have a minimal attack surface: fewer CVEs, no shell for attackers to exploit, and smaller size (often 10-20 MB vs 100+ MB for full OS images)
B) Distroless images run containers without any Linux kernel, using a custom hypervisor for complete isolation from the host operating system
C) Distroless is a Docker feature that automatically removes unused layers from an image to reduce its size during docker push operations
D) Distroless images are container images that don't include a CMD or ENTRYPOINT instruction, making them suitable only as base images for other Dockerfiles
✅ Correct Answer: A

Distroless images (pioneered by Google) contain only your application and its runtime dependencies. They exclude everything else: no shell (/bin/sh), no package manager (apt/yum/apk), no curl, no wget, no coreutils — just the bare minimum to run your app.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Distroless vs Traditional Image — Attack Surface Comparison
  ┌────────────────────────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────────────┐
  │      TRADITIONAL IMAGE             │ │    DISTROLESS IMAGE       │
  │      (debian:12-slim, ~80 MB)      │ │ (gcr.io/distroless, ~20MB)│
  │                                    │ │                          │
  │  ✅ Application (Django)           │ │ ✅ Application (Django)   │
  │  ✅ Python runtime                 │ │ ✅ Python runtime         │
  │  ✅ pip packages                   │ │ ✅ pip packages           │
  │  ────────────────────────────      │ │ ──────────────────────    │
  │  ❌ /bin/sh, /bin/bash             │ │                          │
  │  ❌ apt-get, dpkg                  │ │                          │
  │  ❌ curl, wget                     │ │                          │
  │  ❌ gcc, make, build-essential     │ │                          │
  │  ❌ ls, cat, grep, find            │ │                          │
  │  ❌ ssh, scp, nc (netcat)          │ │                          │
  │  ❌ cron, systemd                  │ │                          │
  │  ❌ perl, python2, ruby            │ │                          │
  │  ────────────────────────────      │ │ ──────────────────────    │
  │  CVEs: ~100-150                    │ │ CVEs: ~2-5               │
  │  Size: 80-150 MB                   │ │ Size: 15-30 MB           │
  │  Attacker gets shell: YES 🔴       │ │ Attacker gets shell: NO 🟢│
  └────────────────────────────────────┘ └──────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, we use gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot. If an attacker exploits a vulnerability in Django, they can't spawn a shell — there is no /bin/sh. They can't download tools — no curl or wget. They can't install packages — no apt. They can't even run ls or cat. The nonroot variant also runs as UID 65532, not root. This is defense in depth — a KCNA core concept.

K8s Fundamentals Q8 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between a container and a virtual machine (VM)?
A) Containers and VMs are functionally identical — the only difference is that containers use less disk space because they compress the application files
B) Containers share the host OS kernel and isolate processes using Linux namespaces + cgroups, while VMs each run their own complete guest OS kernel on top of a hypervisor. This means containers start in milliseconds and use MBs of RAM; VMs take seconds and use GBs
C) Containers run directly on hardware without any operating system layer, while VMs require a full operating system stack including kernel, drivers, and system services
D) Containers are designed only for stateless applications (web servers, APIs), while VMs are required for any application that needs persistent storage like databases
✅ Correct Answer: B

The key difference is kernel sharing. Containers run as isolated processes on the host's Linux kernel — no guest OS to boot. VMs each boot a complete guest operating system with its own kernel on top of a hypervisor. This has profound implications for speed, density, and security.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container vs VM — Resource Efficiency & Startup Time
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    CONTAINERS (shared kernel)                    │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj  │ │ anihpj  │ │ postgres│ │  redis  │ │ nginx   │  │
  │  │ api v1  │ │ api v2  │ │   15    │ │   7     │ │  1.27   │  │
  │  └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │              HOST LINUX KERNEL (shared)                  │   │
  │  │    One kernel serves ALL containers simultaneously       │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  • 5 containers on 1 host • Each: ~20 MB RAM • Start: <1 sec   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    VIRTUAL MACHINES (per-VM kernel)              │
  │                                                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐                   │
  │  │   anihpj API      │ │   PostgreSQL      │                   │
  │  │   ┌─────────────┐ │ │   ┌─────────────┐ │                   │
  │  │   │ Guest OS    │ │ │   │ Guest OS    │ │                   │
  │  │   │ (Ubuntu 22) │ │ │   │ (Ubuntu 22) │ │                   │
  │  │   │ ~1 GB RAM   │ │ │   │ ~1 GB RAM   │ │                   │
  │  │   └─────────────┘ │ │   └─────────────┘ │                   │
  │  └───────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                   HYPERVISOR                             │   │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │  • 2 VMs on 1 host • Each: ~1 GB RAM • Start: 30-60 sec        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

This is why Kubernetes can pack 50-100 containers on the same hardware that might only run 5-10 VMs — and why containers start in milliseconds instead of minutes. For anihpj/jobpost on your kubeadm cluster (node "anih"), the Django API, PostgreSQL, Redis, and nginx all share the same v1.32.13 kernel, each isolated in its own namespace with its own IP address.

K8s Fundamentals Q9 of 200
What is the relationship between Docker, containerd, and CRI-O in the Kubernetes ecosystem?
A) Docker, containerd, and CRI-O are three competing container runtimes that cannot coexist on the same Kubernetes node — you must choose exactly one
B) Docker is the only runtime that supports both Linux and Windows containers, while containerd and CRI-O are Linux-only alternatives with limited production use
C) containerd and CRI-O are plugins that run inside Docker to provide Kubernetes-specific features like pod networking and service discovery
D) Docker originally bundled containerd as its runtime component. Kubernetes deprecated direct Docker support in v1.20 (removed in v1.24) and now uses containerd or CRI-O directly via the CRI (Container Runtime Interface). Docker → dockerd → containerd → runc was the old chain; now it's kubelet → CRI → containerd/CRI-O → runc
✅ Correct Answer: D

This question tests one of the most misunderstood topics in Kubernetes. Docker was NEVER the container runtime — it was a higher-level tool that used containerd underneath. Kubernetes can (and now does) talk directly to containerd or CRI-O via the CRI, eliminating the Docker middleman.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Runtime Evolution — Before and After CRI
  BEFORE KUBERNETES v1.24 (dockershim removed):
  ┌─────────┐     ┌───────────┐     ┌────────────┐     ┌──────┐
  │ kubelet │────▶│ dockershim│────▶│  dockerd   │────▶│runc  │
  │         │     │(deprecated)│    │ (Docker    │     │(OCI) │
  │         │     │           │     │  daemon)   │     │      │
  └─────────┘     └───────────┘     └──────┬─────┘     └──────┘
                                           │
                                    ┌──────▼─────┐
                                    │ containerd │ (bundled inside)
                                    └────────────┘

  AFTER KUBERNETES v1.24 (direct CRI communication):
  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────┐
  │ kubelet │────▶│  containerd  │────▶│ runc │  ← SIMPLER, FASTER
  │         │     │  (via CRI)   │     │(OCI) │
  └─────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┘

  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────┐
  │ kubelet │────▶│   CRI-O      │────▶│ runc │  ← LIGHTER (K8s-only)
  │         │     │  (via CRI)   │     │(OCI) │
  └─────────┘     └──────────────┘     └──────┘

  KEY POINTS:
  • Docker (dockerd) = CLI + API + build + registry + containerd
  • containerd = CNCF-graduated runtime (used by Docker AND K8s)
  • CRI-O = lightest option, purpose-built ONLY for Kubernetes
  • runc = the low-level OCI runtime that ALL of them use
  • All three eventually call runc to create namespaces + cgroups

On your kubeadm cluster (node "anih"), kubelet talks directly to containerd via the CRI socket at /run/containerd/containerd.sock. There's no Docker daemon running. You can still use docker build on your laptop, push to a registry, and Kubernetes will pull and run the image with containerd — because both follow OCI standards. This decoupling is a KCNA exam essential.

K8s Fundamentals Q10 of 200
You're building the anihpj/jobpost Django application image. Which Dockerfile approach follows cloud native best practices?
A) Multi-stage build: Stage 1 uses python:3.12-slim to install dependencies and collect static files; Stage 2 uses gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot, copies only the needed artifacts from Stage 1, and runs as non-root user. Result: ~150 MB image with no shell, no compilers, minimal CVEs
B) Single-stage build using FROM python:3.12 (full image) with all dependencies installed, then using docker commit to save runtime changes back to the image after the container starts
C) Using the official Django Docker image (django:latest) as-is, without any custom Dockerfile, because official images are always optimized and secure by default
D) A single-stage build with FROM ubuntu:22.04, apt-get install python3, pip install django, and running the app as root for simplicity — since Kubernetes NetworkPolicies will handle security at the network layer
✅ Correct Answer: A

This question ties together everything from Q1-Q9 into a single practical example. The correct approach combines multi-stage builds (Q4), distroless images (Q7), immutable tags (Q6), OCI compliance (Q2), and proper layer ordering (Q3) — all applied to the anihpj/jobpost Django application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Complete Dockerfile Walkthrough
  # ============================================================
  # anihpj/jobpost — Production Dockerfile (Multi-Stage, Distroless)
  # ============================================================

  # ═══ STAGE 1: BUILDER ═══
  FROM python:3.12-slim@sha256:abc123... AS builder   ← Q6: digest, not :latest
  WORKDIR /app

  # Q3: COPY deps BEFORE source code (layer caching!)
  COPY requirements.txt .
  RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

  # Collect Django static files during build
  COPY . .
  RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

  # ═══ STAGE 2: RUNTIME ═══
  FROM gcr.io/distroless/python3-debian12:nonroot   ← Q7: distroless, no shell
  WORKDIR /app

  # Copy ONLY what's needed from builder (Q4: multi-stage)
  COPY --from=builder /usr/local/lib/python3.12/site-packages \
       /usr/local/lib/python3.12/site-packages
  COPY --from=builder /app /app

  USER 65532   ← Non-root user (defense in depth)
  EXPOSE 8000

  # gunicorn is copied from builder — no shell needed to start it
  CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]

  # ═══ BUILD & PUSH ═══
  # docker build -t anihpj/api:v1.2.3 .           ← Q6: semantic version
  # docker push anihpj/api:v1.2.3                  ← Q5: push to registry
  # docker tag anihpj/api:v1.2.3 anihpj/api:latest  ← optional alias

  # ═══ DEPLOY TO KUBERNETES ═══
  # kubectl set image deploy/anihpj-api api=anihpj/api:v1.2.3
  # → containerd pulls from registry (Q9: via CRI, no Docker needed)
  # → Pod starts in milliseconds (Q1: shared kernel, Q8: container ≠ VM)

This Dockerfile produces a ~150 MB image with approximately 5 known CVEs (vs ~800 MB and ~120 CVEs for a naive single-stage Ubuntu build). It runs as a non-root user with no shell — if compromised, an attacker can't install tools, spawn a reverse shell, or escalate privileges. Every concept from Q1-Q9 is applied here: namespace isolation (Q1), OCI compliance (Q2), layer caching (Q3), multi-stage build (Q4), registry distribution (Q5), immutable versioning (Q6), distroless security (Q7), and efficient container-over-VM architecture (Q8). This is the KCNA mindset: understand each piece individually, then combine them into a secure, efficient cloud native deployment.

1.2 Pods — The Fundamental UnitQ11-Q18

K8s Fundamentals Q11 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Pod?
A) A Kubernetes object that represents a single Docker container running in isolation on a worker node
B) The smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes — a group of one or more containers that share the same network namespace (IP address, localhost, ports), IPC namespace, and optionally shared volumes. Containers in a pod are always co-located on the same node
C) A logical grouping of multiple worker nodes that share the same storage and network configuration
D) A Kubernetes controller that manages the lifecycle of ReplicaSets and ensures the desired number of container instances are running
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Pod is the atomic unit of scheduling in Kubernetes — the smallest thing you can deploy. It wraps one or more containers that share: network (same IP, same localhost, same port space), IPC (inter-process communication), and optionally volumes. All containers in a pod are always scheduled to the same node and share the pod's lifecycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Anatomy — Shared Resources
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         POD "anihpj-api"                     │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │           SHARED NETWORK NAMESPACE (IP: 10.244.1.5)    │ │
  │  │                                                         │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐ │ │
  │  │  │ Container A  │  │ Container B  │  │  Sidecar     │ │ │
  │  │  │ Django       │  │ nginx proxy  │  │  Fluentd     │ │ │
  │  │  │ :8000        │  │ :8080        │  │  log fwd     │ │ │
  │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘ │ │
  │  │         │                  │                  │          │ │
  │  │         └──────────────────┴──────────────────┘          │ │
  │  │            Talk via localhost (SAME pod!)                │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │              SHARED VOLUMES (optional)                  │ │
  │  │    emptyDir, ConfigMap, Secret, PVC — all mountable     │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API container and a Fluentd log-forwarding sidecar share the same pod — they communicate via localhost and share the same IP address. If you need to scale, you scale the entire pod (not individual containers). This is why the Pod is the fundamental unit: you never deploy a bare container in Kubernetes — you always deploy a Pod.

K8s Fundamentals Q12 of 200
What are the phases in the Pod lifecycle, and why don't Pods automatically restart when they fail?
A) Pods cycle through: Created → Starting → Healthy → Degraded → Terminated. Pods auto-restart up to 5 times before being marked as Failed
B) The Pod lifecycle is: Scheduled → Initialized → ContainersReady → Ready → Completed. Pods themselves never restart — the kubelet restarts individual containers within the pod based on restartPolicy
C) Pod phases are: Pending → Running → Succeeded/Failed. Pods themselves do NOT restart — when a pod dies, it's gone forever. The ReplicaSet controller (if used) creates a NEW pod to replace it. This is why you use Deployments, not bare Pods, for production
D) Pods have two states: Alive and Dead. When a pod dies, Kubernetes automatically resurrects it on the same node with the same IP address to maintain service continuity
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Pod has exactly 5 phases: Pending (scheduling/ image pulling), Running (at least one container running), Succeeded (all containers exited with code 0), Failed (all containers terminated, at least one with non-zero exit), and Unknown (node lost contact). Once a pod reaches Succeeded or Failed, it is never restarted. A new pod must be created.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Lifecycle — You Can't Resurrect the Dead
  Pending ──▶ Running ──▶ Succeeded (exit 0)  ← Pod is DONE, gone forever
                │
                └──▶ Failed (non-zero exit)    ← Pod is DONE, gone forever

  🐄 "Pets vs Cattle" analogy:
  • Pod = Cattle (disposable — when it dies, get a NEW one)
  • Pod IP = dynamic (changes on restart)
  • Pod name = unique hash (anihpj-api-7d8f9-abcde)

  ✅ What RESTARTS: Containers within a pod (based on restartPolicy)
  ❌ What does NOT restart: The Pod itself (it's dead, Jim)

  This is why you NEVER create bare Pods for production:
  kubectl run nginx --image=nginx  ← Creates a bare Pod
  → Pod dies → GONE FOREVER 😱

  Instead, use a Deployment:
  kubectl create deploy nginx --image=nginx  ← Creates Deployment→ReplicaSet→Pod
  → Pod dies → ReplicaSet creates a NEW Pod ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API is deployed as a Deployment, not a bare Pod. If the anihpj-api pod crashes (OOMKilled, node failure), the ReplicaSet controller immediately creates a new pod — possibly on a different node with a different IP. The Service (anihpj-api) automatically updates its endpoints to point to the new pod. This self-healing is the core value proposition of Kubernetes.

K8s Fundamentals Q13 of 200
What are multi-container Pod design patterns, and when would you use them?
A) Multi-container pods are used to run multiple copies of the same application for high availability within a single pod
B) Kubernetes does not support multi-container pods — each pod can only contain exactly one container for security isolation
C) Multi-container pods are a deprecated feature replaced by Init Containers in Kubernetes v1.20; they should not be used in new deployments
D) Three common patterns: Sidecar (helper container alongside main app, e.g., log forwarder), Ambassador (proxy that abstracts external services), and Adapter (transforms main container's output). They share the pod's network/IPC namespaces, communicating via localhost
✅ Correct Answer: D

Multi-container pods exist because some problems are best solved by co-located helper containers that extend or support the main application. Since all containers in a pod share localhost and the same filesystem, they can communicate without network overhead.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Three Multi-Container Patterns
  SIDECAR: Helper container alongside main app
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌─────────┐      ┌──────────────┐   │
  │  │ Django  │──▶──▶│ Fluentd      │   │  Django writes to stdout
  │  │ :8000   │      │ log forwarder│   │  Fluentd reads + ships logs
  │  └─────────┘      └──────────────┘   │
  │        Shared emptyDir volume         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  AMBASSADOR: Proxy that abstracts external services
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌─────────┐      ┌──────────────┐   │
  │  │ Django  │──▶──▶│ Envoy proxy  │──▶│──▶ Redis cluster (external)
  │  │ :8000   │local- │ :6379        │   │  Ambassador handles sharding
  │  └─────────┘ host  └──────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

  ADAPTER: Transforms output to standard format
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌─────────┐      ┌──────────────┐   │
  │  │ Legacy  │──▶──▶│ Prometheus   │   │  Legacy app outputs custom metrics
  │  │ App     │      │ adapter      │   │  Adapter converts to /metrics format
  │  └─────────┘      └──────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API pod could include a Fluentd sidecar that tails the application logs and forwards them to Elasticsearch — no code changes needed in Django itself. The sidecar pattern is a KCNA favorite because it embodies the cloud native principle of separation of concerns.

K8s Fundamentals Q14 of 200
What are Init Containers and how do they differ from regular containers in a Pod?
A) Init containers run to completion BEFORE any regular containers start. They run sequentially (one after another) and must all succeed before the main containers begin. Used for setup tasks: database migrations, waiting for dependencies, file permissions
B) Init containers are identical to regular containers but are automatically restarted by kubelet if they exit with a non-zero status code
C) Init containers run in parallel with main containers and provide initialization data via shared environment variables rather than shared volumes
D) Init containers are a special type of container that runs once per node (not per pod) to initialize node-level resources like CNI plugins and storage drivers
✅ Correct Answer: A

Init containers run before the main application containers, sequentially (one finishes, then the next starts), and must all succeed (exit 0) before any regular container starts. They're perfect for setup tasks like running database migrations, waiting for dependent services, or setting file permissions on shared volumes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Init Containers — Sequential Setup Before App Start
  POD START
  │
  ├── Init Container 1: "Wait for PostgreSQL" (nc -z anihpj-db 5432)
  │   └── Loops until DB is ready, then exits 0 ✅
  │
  ├── Init Container 2: "Run Django migrations"
  │   └── python manage.py migrate → exits 0 ✅
  │
  └── Main Container: Django API starts
      └── gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --bind :8000

  KEY DIFFERENCES from regular containers:
  • Run SEQUENTIALLY (one at a time, in order)
  • Must ALL succeed (any failure = pod restart)
  • Can use DIFFERENT images (busybox for wait, Django for migrate)
  • Have their own resource limits (separate from main containers)
  • Can mount volumes to pass data to main containers

For anihpj/jobpost, an init container runs python manage.py migrate using the Django image before the API server starts. This ensures the database schema is always current before accepting traffic — a pattern used in production by thousands of Kubernetes deployments.

K8s Fundamentals Q15 of 200
How does networking work inside a Pod with multiple containers?
A) Each container in a pod gets its own unique IP address, and containers communicate through a virtual switch managed by kube-proxy
B) All containers in a pod share the same network namespace — they share the same IP address, the same localhost, and the same port space. Containers communicate via localhost and must not use conflicting ports
C) Containers in a pod communicate through a shared Docker bridge network; each container gets a unique port on the pod's IP address but shares the same MAC address
D) Multi-container pods use a NAT-based networking model where each container has an internal IP that is translated to the pod's external IP by the CNI plugin
✅ Correct Answer: B

All containers in a pod share a single network namespace. This means they all share one IP address, one localhost interface, and one port space. If container A listens on port 8000, container B in the same pod can reach it at localhost:8000 — but container B cannot also listen on port 8000 (port conflict within the shared namespace).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Networking — Shared localhost, Shared IP
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              POD: IP 10.244.1.5                              │
  │                                                              │
  │  Container A (Django)        Container B (nginx sidecar)     │
  │  Listens on :8000            Listens on :8080                │
  │       │                            │                         │
  │       └────────┬───────────────────┘                         │
  │                │                                             │
  │         localhost:8000 → Django                              │
  │         localhost:8080 → nginx                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ❌ Both CANNOT listen on :8000 (shared port space!)         │
  │  ✅ A can curl localhost:8080 → reaches B                    │
  │  ✅ B can curl localhost:8000 → reaches A                    │
  │  ✅ External traffic to 10.244.1.5:8000 → reaches A          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Pod-to-Pod communication (across pods):
  Pod A (10.244.1.5) → CNI → Pod B (10.244.2.8)  ← Uses pod IPs, not localhost

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django container and an nginx proxy share a pod, nginx can proxy to localhost:8000 (Django) with zero network overhead — it never leaves the pod. This is why the sidecar pattern is so efficient: no network hop between containers in the same pod.

K8s Fundamentals Q16 of 200
What are Pod resource requests and why are they essential for scheduling?
A) Resource requests are hard limits that the container cannot exceed; the scheduler ignores them and places pods randomly across available nodes
B) Resource requests are optional metadata fields used only for monitoring dashboards and cost allocation reports
C) Resource requests declare the MINIMUM CPU and memory a container needs. The kube-scheduler uses requests to find a node with sufficient available resources. Without requests, the scheduler can't make intelligent placement decisions, leading to overloaded nodes
D) Resource requests are automatically calculated by the metrics-server based on historical usage patterns and applied to pods at creation time
✅ Correct Answer: C

Resource requests tell the scheduler: "This container needs at least this much CPU and memory." The scheduler sums up requests of all containers in a pod and places it on a node that has enough free capacity. Without requests, pods might land on nodes that are already overcommitted.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Requests vs Limits — The Scheduler's Decision
  REQUESTS = "I need AT LEAST this" (scheduler uses this)
  LIMITS   = "I can use AT MOST this" (enforced by kernel/cgroups)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NODE: 4 CPU, 8 GB RAM                                     │
  │                                                             │
  │  Pod A: request 1 CPU, 2 GB  → Node has 3 CPU, 6 GB left   │
  │  Pod B: request 2 CPU, 3 GB  → Node has 1 CPU, 3 GB left   │
  │  Pod C: request 2 CPU, 2 GB  → ❌ NOT ENOUGH CPU! Pending   │
  │                                                             │
  │  Without requests, all 3 would be scheduled → OOM Killer!   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  anihpj/jobpost example:
  containers:
  - name: django-api
    resources:
      requests:
        cpu: "500m"      # 0.5 CPU core minimum
        memory: "512Mi"  # 512 MB RAM minimum
      limits:
        cpu: "1000m"     # Can burst to 1 full core
        memory: "1Gi"    # Hard cap at 1 GB (OOMKilled if exceeded)

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API requests 500m CPU and 512Mi memory. The scheduler ensures it only lands on a node with those resources available, preventing the "noisy neighbor" problem where one pod starves others. Always set requests — they're the foundation of reliable scheduling and a KCNA exam fundamental.

K8s Fundamentals Q17 of 200
What happens when a container exceeds its memory limit?
A) The container is throttled — it continues running but at reduced speed until memory usage drops below the limit
B) Kubernetes automatically increases the container's memory limit to accommodate the spike and sends a warning event
C) The excess memory is swapped to disk by the kernel, and the container continues running normally with degraded performance
D) The container is OOMKilled (Out Of Memory Killed) by the Linux kernel. The pod's restartPolicy determines what happens next — if set to Always (default), the container restarts. The termination reason will show "OOMKilled"
✅ Correct Answer: D

Memory is a non-compressible resource — unlike CPU (which can be throttled), you can't "slow down" memory usage. When a container's memory exceeds its limit, the Linux kernel's OOM (Out Of Memory) killer terminates the process with SIGKILL. The container runtime records exit code 137 (128 + 9 for SIGKILL).

📝 Detailed Explanation
CPU Throttling vs Memory OOMKill
  CPU (compressible resource):
  Container requests 500m, limit 1000m
  Usage hits 1000m → THROTTLED (slower, but ALIVE)
  Pod shows: "CPU throttling" in metrics

  MEMORY (non-compressible resource):
  Container requests 512Mi, limit 1Gi
  Usage hits 1Gi → OOMKilled (DEAD, exit code 137)
  Pod shows: "OOMKilled" in kubectl describe

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ OOM SCORE & QoS EVICTION ORDER (when NODE runs out):     │
  │                                                           │
  │  1. BestEffort pods (no requests/limits) — KILLED FIRST   │
  │  2. Burstable pods (requests < limits)                    │
  │  3. Guaranteed pods (requests == limits) — KILLED LAST    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django API has a memory leak and hits its 1Gi limit, it's killed and restarted. The key takeaway: set appropriate limits AND monitor memory trends. OOMKilled is a symptom — the fix is either fixing the leak or raising the limit (not both blindly).

K8s Fundamentals Q18 of 200
What does restartPolicy control and what are its three possible values?
A) restartPolicy controls what happens when ALL containers in a pod terminate. Values: Always (restart containers — default for Deployments), OnFailure (restart only on non-zero exit — for Jobs), Never (never restart — for one-shot init tasks)
B) restartPolicy controls how many times a pod can be rescheduled to different nodes after node failures, with values ranging from 1 to 10
C) restartPolicy determines whether a Deployment rolls back automatically when a new ReplicaSet fails its readiness checks
D) restartPolicy controls the maximum duration a pod can be in CrashLoopBackOff state before Kubernetes permanently deletes it, with values: Short, Medium, and Long
✅ Correct Answer: A

restartPolicy applies to all containers in a pod and determines what kubelet does when containers terminate. It has three values: Always (the default for pods created by Deployments/StatefulSets/DaemonSets — restart indefinitely), OnFailure (restart only if exit code ≠ 0 — used by Jobs), and Never (never restart — used for one-shot pods).

📝 Detailed Explanation
restartPolicy Decision Matrix
  ┌────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
  │ restartPolicy  │ Restarts on  │ Use Case                     │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Always         │ Any exit     │ Web servers, APIs (anihpj)   │
  │                │ (0 or ≠0)    │ Default for Deployments      │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ OnFailure      │ Only exit ≠0 │ Batch Jobs, DB migrations    │
  │                │              │ Default for Jobs              │
  ├────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
  │ Never          │ Never        │ One-shot init, data export   │
  │                │              │ Pod runs once, then stops     │
  └────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

  CrashLoopBackOff: When Always + repeated failures →
  kubelet adds exponential backoff (10s → 20s → 40s → ... max 5min)

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API uses restartPolicy: Always (via its Deployment). The database migration Job uses restartPolicy: OnFailure. A one-time data export pod might use restartPolicy: Never. This is a KCNA fundamental — know which policy applies to which workload type.

1.3 Workload Resources — Deployments, StatefulSets, DaemonSetsQ19-Q28

K8s Fundamentals Q19 of 200
What is the relationship between a Deployment, a ReplicaSet, and a Pod?
A) They are three independent Kubernetes resources that can be used interchangeably to run containers
B) A Deployment manages ReplicaSets, which manage Pods. Each layer adds features: Deployment adds rolling updates + rollbacks; ReplicaSet ensures N identical pods are running; Pod runs the containers. The full chain: Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod
C) A Deployment directly creates and manages Pods; ReplicaSets are only used internally by StatefulSets for stable network identity
D) A Deployment is just a YAML template — at runtime, it's converted into a ReplicaSet which is then converted into Pods by the kubelet
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod hierarchy is the most common workload pattern in Kubernetes. Each level adds capabilities while delegating lower-level concerns to the layer below.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod Hierarchy
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEPLOYMENT: anihpj-api                                 │
  │  • Rolling updates (gradual replacement of pods)        │
  │  • Rollback (undo to previous version)                  │
  │  • Pause/Resume (canary control)                        │
  │  • Revision history (keeps last 10 by default)          │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  REPLICASET: anihpj-api-7d8f9c4b6                │ │
  │  │  • Ensures N identical pods are running           │ │
  │  │  • Self-healing: if pod dies → creates replacement│ │
  │  │  • Selector-based pod management                  │ │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐       │ │
  │  │  │ Pod v1   │  │ Pod v1   │  │ Pod v1   │       │ │
  │  │  │ 10.244.1│  │ 10.244.2│  │ 10.244.1│       │ │
  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘       │ │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, kubectl apply -f django-api-deploy.yaml creates a Deployment. The Deployment creates a ReplicaSet (named anihpj-api-<hash>), which creates 3 Pods. When you update the image, the Deployment creates a NEW ReplicaSet and gradually shifts pods from old to new — this is the rolling update.

K8s Fundamentals Q20 of 200
What happens when a Pod managed by a ReplicaSet crashes or is deleted?
A) The ReplicaSet marks itself as degraded and sends an alert to the cluster administrator; manual intervention is required to recreate the pod
B) The ReplicaSet restarts the SAME pod on the same node with the same IP address, preserving all network connections and in-memory state
C) The ReplicaSet detects the discrepancy between desired count (e.g., 3) and actual count (2) and creates a NEW pod to restore the desired state. The new pod gets a new name and a new IP — this is Kubernetes self-healing
D) The ReplicaSet waits for the pod's restartPolicy timer to expire, then reattaches the pod's persistent volume to a newly created container on the same node
✅ Correct Answer: C

The ReplicaSet's reconciliation loop continuously compares spec.replicas (desired) with the actual count of matching pods. If a pod disappears, the ReplicaSet creates a brand new pod — new name, new UID, new IP. This is Kubernetes self-healing: no human intervention, no restoring state, just "make N pods exist."

📝 Detailed Explanation
ReplicaSet Self-Healing
  DESIRED: 3 pods                     ACTUAL: 3 pods
  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐                  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐
  │ P1│ │ P2│ │ P3│                  │ P1│ │ P2│ │ P3│  ✅ All good
  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘                  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘

  Node fails → P3 dies               ACTUAL: 2 pods
  ┌───┐ ┌───┐       ┌───┐            ┌───┐ ┌───┐
  │ P1│ │ P2│       │ P3│💀          │ P1│ │ P2│        ❌ 2 ≠ 3!
  └───┘ └───┘       └───┘            └───┘ └───┘
                                          │
  ReplicaSet detects diff ────────────────┘
  Creates NEW pod (P4, new IP)
                                          │
                                          ▼
                                      ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐
                                      │ P1│ │ P2│ │ P4│  ✅ 3 == 3
                                      └───┘ └───┘ └───┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if a node running the Django API pod crashes, the ReplicaSet creates a replacement pod on a healthy node within seconds. The Service (anihpj-api) automatically updates its endpoints to include the new pod's IP. Users experience at most a brief blip — this is the cloud native promise of resilience.

K8s Fundamentals Q21 of 200
How does a Kubernetes rolling update work?
A) The Deployment creates a new ReplicaSet and gradually scales it up while scaling down the old ReplicaSet, controlled by maxSurge (how many extra pods allowed) and maxUnavailable (how many pods can be down). This ensures zero downtime during updates
B) All existing pods are terminated simultaneously, then new pods with the updated image are created — causing a brief downtime of a few seconds
C) The Deployment patches each running pod in-place without creating new pods, preserving the pod's IP address and persistent volume mounts
D) Rolling updates require a service mesh (Istio/Linkerd) and cannot be performed with vanilla Kubernetes Deployments alone
✅ Correct Answer: A

A rolling update is the default Deployment strategy. The Deployment controller creates a new ReplicaSet and uses maxSurge and maxUnavailable to control the pace. With defaults (maxSurge=25%, maxUnavailable=25%), the update replaces pods one at a time while maintaining at least 75% availability — zero downtime for users.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rolling Update — Step by Step
  OLD RS (v1): 3 pods           NEW RS (v2): 0 pods
  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐
  │v1 │ │v1 │ │v1 │
  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘

  Step 1: maxSurge=1 → Create 1 v2 pod (total: 4 pods OK temporarily)
  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐
  │v1 │ │v1 │ │v1 │ │v2 │
  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘

  Step 2: maxUnavailable=1 → Kill 1 v1 pod
  ┌───┐ ┌───┐       ┌───┐
  │v1 │ │v1 │       │v2 │
  └───┘ └───┘       └───┘

  Repeat until all 3 are v2 — ZERO DOWNTIME!

For anihpj/jobpost, updating the Django API from v1.2.3 to v1.3.0: kubectl set image deploy/anihpj-api api=anihpj/api:v1.3.0. The Deployment gradually replaces pods. If health checks fail on v1.3.0, you can instantly kubectl rollout undo.

K8s Fundamentals Q22 of 200
What happens step-by-step when you run kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml with a new container image tag?
A) kubectl SSHs into each node, runs docker pull on the new image, then restarts the kubelet to pick up the changes
B) 1) kubectl sends the updated Deployment spec to the API Server, 2) API Server stores it in etcd, 3) Deployment controller detects the change, 4) creates a new ReplicaSet, 5) gradually scales up new RS while scaling down old RS, 6) old RS is retained for rollback
C) kubectl modifies the YAML file in-place on the node's filesystem, then sends a SIGHUP signal to the container runtime to reload its configuration
D) The Deployment controller restarts all pods simultaneously with the new image, then waits for them all to pass readiness checks before routing traffic
✅ Correct Answer: B

The full flow: kubectl → API Server (auth/authz/admission) → etcd (stored) → Deployment Controller (reconciliation loop detects diff) → creates new ReplicaSet → rolling update → old ReplicaSet retained. This is the reconciliation loop in action — the controller constantly compares desired state (etcd) with actual state (cluster) and takes action to converge.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl apply — The Full Chain
  kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml
  │
  ▼
  API SERVER: Auth → AuthZ → Admission ✓
  │
  ▼
  ETCD: /registry/deployments/anihpj/anihpj-api ← stored
  │
  ▼
  DEPLOYMENT CONTROLLER: Watches etcd → "New spec! Desired=3, Actual=0"
  │
  ▼
  NEW REPLICASET: anihpj-api-8f9d2c ← created
  │
  ▼
  ROLLING UPDATE: maxSurge=1, maxUnavailable=1 → Gradual replacement
  │
  ▼
  OLD REPLICASET RETAINED: anihpj-api-7d8f9 ← kept for rollback!

For anihpj/jobpost, kubectl apply -f django-api-deploy.yaml with a new image tag triggers this whole chain. The Deployment controller creates a new ReplicaSet and rolls it out. If the new version has bugs, kubectl rollout undo instantly switches back to the old ReplicaSet — which was kept specifically for this purpose.

K8s Fundamentals Q23 of 200
What do kubectl rollout status, history, and undo do?
A) These commands manage the Kubernetes cluster upgrade process — status checks node versions, history shows past upgrades, and undo reverts to a previous Kubernetes version
B) These are deprecated commands replaced by Helm's helm rollback feature in Kubernetes v1.25
C) rollout status watches a rolling update until complete (or fails), rollout history lists past revisions of a Deployment (default: 10), and rollout undo reverts to the previous revision — instantly restoring the old ReplicaSet. These are zero-downtime deployment controls
D) rollout status shows CPU/memory metrics during an update, history shows audit logs, and undo reverts etcd to a previous snapshot
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl rollout is your deployment control panel. status shows progress in real-time, history shows the last 10 revisions (each with a CHANGE-CAUSE if you use --record), and undo instantly switches back to the previous ReplicaSet — no new image build needed because the old pods are still there.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl rollout — Deployment Control Panel
  # Watch a rollout in real time
  kubectl rollout status deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj
  → Waiting for rollout to finish: 2 of 3 updated replicas available...

  # See what changed and when
  kubectl rollout history deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj
  REVISION  CHANGE-CAUSE
  1         Initial deploy
  2         Update image to v1.2.3
  3         Update image to v1.3.0  ← CURRENT

  # UNDO — instant rollback to revision 2!
  kubectl rollout undo deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj
  → Switches back to the v1.2.3 ReplicaSet (already running pods!)

For anihpj/jobpost, if v1.3.0 has a bug, kubectl rollout undo instantly restores v1.2.3 — the old ReplicaSet was kept around specifically for this. This is why Deployments retain old ReplicaSets: instant rollback.

K8s Fundamentals Q24 of 200
What is a StatefulSet and when should you use it instead of a Deployment?
A) A StatefulSet is identical to a Deployment but includes built-in autoscaling based on CPU and memory metrics
B) A StatefulSet provides: stable network identity (predictable pod names: app-0, app-1), stable persistent storage (each pod gets its own PVC that survives rescheduling), and ordered graceful deployment/scaling (0, then 1, then 2...). Used for stateful apps like databases where identity and data persistence matter
C) A StatefulSet is a Deployment variant that automatically creates cloud load balancers for each pod in AWS, GCP, and Azure
D) A StatefulSet is a deprecated resource replaced by Deployment with persistentVolumeClaim templates in Kubernetes v1.22
✅ Correct Answer: B

StatefulSets provide three guarantees that Deployments don't: stable identity (pod-0 is always pod-0, even after restart), stable storage (each pod gets its own PVC via volumeClaimTemplates that follows the pod), and ordered operations (scaling up goes 0→1→2, scaling down goes 2→1→0, gracefully).

📝 Detailed Explanation
StatefulSet vs Deployment
  DEPLOYMENT (stateless):          STATEFULSET (stateful):
  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐          ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │api-7│ │api-8│ │api-9│          │anihpj-db│ │anihpj-db│ │anihpj-db│
  │f8d9c│ │a1b2c│ │x9y0z│          │   -0    │ │   -1    │ │   -2    │
  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘          └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘
  Random names, random IPs              │            │            │
  Share PVC or no PVC              ┌────▼────┐  ┌────▼────┐  ┌────▼────┐
                                   │ PVC-0   │  │ PVC-1   │  │ PVC-2   │
                                   │ 10Gi    │  │ 10Gi    │  │ 10Gi    │
                                   └─────────┘  └─────────┘  └─────────┘
                                   Each pod has its OWN dedicated storage

For anihpj/jobpost, PostgreSQL runs as a StatefulSet because: (1) pod identity matters for replication (anihpj-db-0 is the primary), (2) each pod needs its own persistent data directory, and (3) the database needs ordered startup/shutdown. The Django API runs as a Deployment because it's stateless — any pod can handle any request.

K8s Fundamentals Q25 of 200
When should you use a StatefulSet vs a Deployment for the anihpj/jobpost application?
A) anihpj-db (PostgreSQL) = StatefulSet (needs stable identity + dedicated PVC per pod). anihpj-api (Django) = Deployment (stateless, any pod can serve). anihpj-redis = Deployment (cache is ephemeral). anihpj-web (nginx) = Deployment (stateless static file server)
B) All anihpj components should use StatefulSets because they provide better performance and reliability than Deployments
C) All anihpj components should use Deployments because StatefulSets are only needed for applications using cloud-specific storage drivers
D) anihpj-db should use a Deployment with a single PVC shared across replicas for better resource utilization
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is a practical decision you'll make for every Kubernetes application. StatefulSet → stateful (databases, message queues, anything where pod identity or dedicated storage matters). Deployment → stateless (APIs, web servers, anything where any pod can handle any request).

📝 Detailed Explanation
anihpj/jobpost — Workload Type Decisions
  ┌──────────────┬────────────┬──────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Component    │ Workload   │ Why                              │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ anihpj-db    │ StatefulSet│ PostgreSQL needs stable identity │
  │ (PostgreSQL) │            │ + dedicated PVC per replica      │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ anihpj-api   │ Deployment │ Django is stateless — any pod    │
  │ (Django)     │            │ can serve any API request        │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ anihpj-redis │ Deployment │ Cache data is ephemeral — if     │
  │ (Redis)      │            │ pod dies, just restart and refill│
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ anihpj-web   │ Deployment │ Nginx serves static files —      │
  │ (Nginx)      │            │ completely stateless             │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴──────────────────────────────────┘

The golden rule: if you care WHICH pod handles a request, use a StatefulSet. If ANY pod can handle it, use a Deployment. This is a KCNA fundamental — expect a question about workload type selection on the exam.

K8s Fundamentals Q26 of 200
What is a DaemonSet and what is it typically used for?
A) A DaemonSet is a Kubernetes security resource that runs antivirus scans on all container images in a registry
B) A DaemonSet runs exactly one pod in the entire cluster, typically used for singleton services like the Kubernetes dashboard
C) A DaemonSet ensures that a copy of a pod runs on EVERY node (or a subset via nodeSelector). Used for node-level agents: log collectors (Fluentd/Fluent Bit), monitoring agents (Prometheus node-exporter), CNI plugins, and storage daemons
D) A DaemonSet is a scheduling policy that ensures critical system pods are never evicted during resource pressure
✅ Correct Answer: C

A DaemonSet guarantees that exactly one pod runs on every node (or on specific nodes via nodeSelector). When a new node joins the cluster, the DaemonSet automatically schedules a pod on it. When a node leaves, the pod is garbage-collected. This is perfect for per-node infrastructure agents.

📝 Detailed Explanation
DaemonSet — One Pod Per Node
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              DAEMONSET: fluentd-log-collector               │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐     │
  │  │   NODE 1     │  │   NODE 2     │  │   NODE 3     │     │
  │  │ ┌──────────┐ │  │ ┌──────────┐ │  │ ┌──────────┐ │     │
  │  │ │ fluentd  │ │  │ │ fluentd  │ │  │ │ fluentd  │ │     │
  │  │ │ pod      │ │  │ │ pod      │ │  │ │ pod      │ │     │
  │  │ └──────────┘ │  │ └──────────┘ │  │ └──────────┘ │     │
  │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐│  │ ┌──┐┌──┐    │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐│   │
  │  │ │P1││P2││P3││  │ │P4││P5│    │  │ │P6││P7││P8││P9││   │
  │  │ └──┘└──┘└──┘│  │ └──┘└──┘    │  │ └──┘└──┘└──┘└──┘│   │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘     │
  │                                                             │
  │  Each fluentd pod tails /var/log/containers/ on its node    │
  │  and forwards logs to Elasticsearch                         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Common DaemonSet examples: Fluentd/Fluent Bit (log collection on every node), Prometheus node-exporter (hardware metrics per node), Calico/Cilium agents (CNI on every node), kube-proxy (runs as a DaemonSet in many clusters). On your kubeadm cluster, kube-proxy runs as a DaemonSet — one pod on node "anih".

K8s Fundamentals Q27 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Job and how does it differ from a Deployment?
A) A Job is identical to a Deployment but uses a simpler YAML format with fewer configuration options
B) A Job continuously runs pods and restarts them indefinitely, similar to a Deployment with replicas: 1
C) A Job is a deprecated API replaced by CronJob in Kubernetes v1.21
D) A Job creates one or more pods and ensures a specified number of them successfully complete (exit 0). Unlike Deployments which run indefinitely, Jobs run to COMPLETION. Used for batch tasks: database migrations, data processing, backups
✅ Correct Answer: D

Jobs are for finite tasks. Deployments run forever; Jobs run until completion. A Job with completions: 1 creates a pod and waits for it to exit 0. A Job with completions: 10 runs 10 pods sequentially or in parallel (controlled by parallelism).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Job vs Deployment — Finite vs Infinite
  DEPLOYMENT: Runs FOREVER                      JOB: Runs to COMPLETION
  ┌──────────────────────────┐                 ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ kubectl create deploy    │                 │ kubectl create job   │
  │ anihpj-api --image=...   │                 │ db-migrate --image=..│
  │                           │                 │                      │
  │ Pod starts → running ∞   │                 │ Pod starts           │
  │ If pod dies → restart    │                 │ → python manage.py   │
  │ If node dies → reschedule│                 │   migrate            │
  │ NEVER stops (unless you  │                 │ → exit 0             │
  │ kubectl delete)          │                 │ → Job: "Completed" ✅ │
  └──────────────────────────┘                 └──────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, database migrations run as a Job: kubectl create job db-migrate --image=anihpj/api:v1.2.3 -- python manage.py migrate. The pod runs the migration, exits, and the Job marks itself Complete. No need for the migration pod to keep running — it's a finite task.

K8s Fundamentals Q28 of 200
What is a CronJob and how does it relate to a Job?
A) A CronJob is a Kubernetes resource that replaces the Linux cron daemon on worker nodes for scheduling container restarts
B) A CronJob is an alternative to Deployment that schedules pod creation based on CPU and memory thresholds
C) A CronJob creates Jobs on a repeating schedule using cron syntax (e.g., "0 2 * * *" = every day at 2 AM). Each scheduled execution creates a new Job, which creates a pod. Used for recurring batch tasks: nightly backups, report generation, cache warming
D) A CronJob is a continuous loop that restarts failed pods with exponential backoff until they succeed
✅ Correct Answer: C

A CronJob is a Job scheduler. It uses standard cron syntax (5 fields: minute, hour, day-of-month, month, day-of-week) to create Jobs on a recurring schedule. Each scheduled run creates a new Job, which creates a pod. CronJobs maintain a history of the last 3 successful and 1 failed Job by default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CronJob — Scheduled Job Creation
  CronJob: anihpj-nightly-backup
  schedule: "0 2 * * *"  ← Every day at 2:00 AM
  │
  ├── Day 1 @ 2:00 AM → Job: anihpj-nightly-backup-28061500
  │   └── Pod runs pg_dump → exits 0 → Job Complete ✅
  │
  ├── Day 2 @ 2:00 AM → Job: anihpj-nightly-backup-28061501
  │   └── Pod runs pg_dump → exits 0 → Job Complete ✅
  │
  └── Day 3 @ 2:00 AM → Job: anihpj-nightly-backup-28061502
      └── Pod runs pg_dump → ...
