  Cron syntax: ┌─ minute (0-59)
               │ ┌─ hour (0-23)
               │ │ ┌─ day of month (1-31)
               │ │ │ ┌─ month (1-12)
               │ │ │ │ ┌─ day of week (0-6, Sunday=0)
               │ │ │ │ │
  "0 2 * * *"  → At 02:00 AM every day
  "*/5 * * * *"→ Every 5 minutes
  "0 0 1 * *"  → Midnight on the 1st of every month

For anihpj/jobpost, a CronJob runs pg_dump every night at 2 AM to back up the PostgreSQL database. Another CronJob could run every hour to clear expired session data from Redis. CronJobs embody the cloud native principle of automating operational tasks.

1.4 Configuration: ConfigMaps & SecretsQ29-Q34

K8s Fundamentals Q29 of 200
What is a ConfigMap and why is it a cloud native best practice?
A) A ConfigMap stores non-sensitive configuration data as key-value pairs, decoupled from the container image. This follows the 12-Factor App principle: "store config in the environment." You can update a ConfigMap without rebuilding the image
B) ConfigMap is a Kubernetes security resource that encrypts sensitive configuration data like API keys and database passwords at rest in etcd
C) ConfigMap is a GitOps tool that maps Git repository branches to Kubernetes namespaces for automated deployment
D) ConfigMap is a Docker Compose file that maps container ports to host ports for external access
✅ Correct Answer: A

ConfigMaps externalize configuration from container images. Instead of baking database URLs, feature flags, or environment names into the image, you store them in a ConfigMap and inject them at runtime. This means the same image can run in dev, staging, and prod — only the ConfigMap changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ConfigMap — Same Image, Different Config
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ONE IMAGE: anihpj/api:v1.2.3                              │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐             │
  │  │   DEV    │    │  STAGING │    │   PROD   │             │
  │  │ ConfigMap│    │ ConfigMap│    │ ConfigMap│             │
  │  ├──────────┤    ├──────────┤    ├──────────┤             │
  │  │DB: dev-db│    │DB: stg-db│    │DB: prod  │             │
  │  │DEBUG:true│    │DEBUG:true│    │DEBUG:false│            │
  │  │LOG:debug │    │LOG:info  │    │LOG:warn  │             │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘             │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the ConfigMap stores the database hostname, Redis URL, log level, and Django ALLOWED_HOSTS. When moving from staging to production, you only change the ConfigMap — the Docker image stays the same. This is 12-Factor App Factor III in practice.

K8s Fundamentals Q30 of 200
How do you create a ConfigMap in Kubernetes?
A) ConfigMaps can only be created by writing a YAML manifest file and applying it — there is no imperative way to create them
B) ConfigMaps are automatically generated by Kubernetes from annotations on Deployments; they cannot be created independently
C) ConfigMaps are created using docker config create and then pushed to the container registry alongside the image
D) Multiple ways: kubectl create configmap from --from-literal (key=value), --from-file (whole file), --from-env-file (env file), or declaratively via YAML with data: or binaryData: fields
✅ Correct Answer: D

ConfigMaps can be created imperatively (from literals, files, or env files) or declaratively (from YAML). The imperative approach is great for quick setups; the declarative approach is preferred for GitOps.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Creating ConfigMaps — Four Methods
  # Method 1: From literal key-value pairs (FASTEST)
  kubectl create configmap anihpj-config \
    --from-literal=DB_HOST=anihpj-db \
    --from-literal=REDIS_URL=redis://anihpj-redis:6379 \
    --from-literal=LOG_LEVEL=info -n anihpj

  # Method 2: From a file (entire file becomes one key)
  kubectl create configmap nginx-config \
    --from-file=nginx.conf -n anihpj

  # Method 3: From an env file
  kubectl create configmap app-env \
    --from-env-file=.env -n anihpj

  # Method 4: Declarative YAML (GitOps-friendly)
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ConfigMap
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-config
    namespace: anihpj
  data:
    DB_HOST: anihpj-db
    REDIS_URL: redis://anihpj-redis:6379
    LOG_LEVEL: info

For anihpj/jobpost, we use declarative YAML stored in Git — this enables GitOps workflows where Argo CD or Flux automatically syncs ConfigMap changes to the cluster. The --from-literal approach is handy during development and debugging.

K8s Fundamentals Q31 of 200
How can a Pod consume data from a ConfigMap?
A) Pods can only consume ConfigMap data by reading it directly from etcd using the Kubernetes API; they cannot access ConfigMaps through environment variables or files
B) Three ways: 1) envFrom to inject all keys as environment variables, 2) valueFrom.configMapKeyRef to inject a specific key as an env var, 3) Volume mount — the ConfigMap appears as files in the pod's filesystem (each key = one file). Volume mounts auto-update (with kubelet sync delay)
C) ConfigMaps are injected into pods through a special ConfigMap sidecar container that runs alongside the main application container
D) ConfigMaps are consumed by pods through Kubernetes Service endpoints; the pod makes an HTTP request to the ConfigMap service to retrieve configuration
✅ Correct Answer: B

ConfigMaps can be consumed as environment variables (envFrom for all keys, valueFrom for specific keys) or as files (volume mount — each key becomes a file). Volume-mounted ConfigMaps are automatically updated by the kubelet (with a ~60-second sync delay). Environment variables are NOT updated — you must restart the pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ConfigMap Consumption Methods
  # Create ConfigMap
  kubectl create configmap anihpj-config \
    --from-literal=DB_HOST=anihpj-db \
    --from-literal=LOG_LEVEL=debug

  # Method 1: envFrom (all keys → env vars)
  containers:
  - name: api
    envFrom:
    - configMapRef:
        name: anihpj-config
  → DB_HOST=anihpj-db  LOG_LEVEL=debug  (auto-created env vars)

  # Method 2: valueFrom (specific key → env var)
  containers:
  - name: api
    env:
    - name: DATABASE_URL
      valueFrom:
        configMapKeyRef:
          name: anihpj-config
          key: DB_HOST
  → DATABASE_URL=anihpj-db  (single env var)

  # Method 3: Volume mount (each key → file)
  containers:
  - name: api
    volumeMounts:
    - name: config
      mountPath: /etc/config
  volumes:
  - name: config
    configMap:
      name: anihpj-config
  → /etc/config/DB_HOST (file containing "anihpj-db")
  → /etc/config/LOG_LEVEL (file containing "debug")
  ⚡ Volume mounts auto-update! Env vars do NOT.

For anihpj/jobpost, we use volume mounts for the Django settings file so changes propagate without restarting all pods. We use env vars for the database URL since that rarely changes and requires a pod restart anyway.

K8s Fundamentals Q32 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Secret and how does it differ from a ConfigMap?
A) Secrets are automatically encrypted by Kubernetes and can only be accessed by pods running as root; ConfigMaps are unencrypted and accessible to any pod
B) Secrets and ConfigMaps are identical — the only difference is the API group they belong to (v1 vs apps/v1)
C) Secrets are designed for sensitive data (passwords, tokens, keys). Key differences from ConfigMaps: data is base64-encoded (NOT encrypted by default!), Secrets are only sent to nodes that need them, stored in tmpfs (not disk), and support specific types (Opaque, kubernetes.io/tls, kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson). Use Secrets for passwords, ConfigMaps for app settings
D) Secrets can only be created by cluster administrators using kubectl create secret; regular users cannot create or modify Secrets
✅ Correct Answer: C

⚠️ Critical KCNA fact: Secrets are NOT encrypted by default! They're base64-encoded (easily decoded — try echo <value> | base64 -d). To encrypt Secrets at rest, you MUST configure EncryptionConfiguration for etcd. Secrets ARE stored in tmpfs (RAM, not disk) on the node and are only sent to nodes that run pods needing them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets — Base64 ≠ Encryption
  ❌ COMMON MISCONCEPTION: "Secrets are encrypted"
  kubectl get secret db-pass -o yaml
  data:
    password: c2VjcmV0MTIz  ← Base64, NOT encrypted!
  
  echo "c2VjcmV0MTIz" | base64 -d
  → secret123  ← Anyone with kubectl access can decode this!

  ✅ TO ACTUALLY ENCRYPT SECRETS (etcd at rest):
  apiVersion: apiserver.config.k8s.io/v1
  kind: EncryptionConfiguration
  resources:
  - resources: ["secrets"]
    providers:
    - aescbc:
        keys:
        - name: key1
          secret: 

  SECRET TYPES:
  • Opaque (default) — arbitrary key-value pairs
  • kubernetes.io/tls — TLS cert + key
  • kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson — registry credentials (.docker/config.json)
  • kubernetes.io/basic-auth — username + password

For anihpj/jobpost, we store the PostgreSQL password and Django SECRET_KEY in Secrets, and database host/port/log level in ConfigMaps. Remember: base64 is encoding, not encryption. Anyone with kubectl access can decode Secrets.

K8s Fundamentals Q33 of 200
How do you create different types of Kubernetes Secrets?
A) All Secrets are created the same way using kubectl create configmap — Kubernetes automatically detects sensitive values and converts them to Secrets
B) Secrets can only be created through the Kubernetes dashboard UI; CLI creation is disabled for security reasons
C) Secrets must be manually encrypted before creation using gpg or openssl, then the encrypted blob is submitted to the API server
D) kubectl create secret generic for opaque key-value data, kubectl create secret tls for TLS certificate+key pairs, kubectl create secret docker-registry for container registry credentials. Each type validates the input format (e.g., tls requires valid PEM certificates)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Kubernetes provides three specialized Secret types with built-in validation. Each has a specific kubectl create secret subcommand that validates the input.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Creating Secrets — Three Types
  # Type 1: Opaque (generic key-value)
  kubectl create secret generic db-credentials \
    --from-literal=username=anihpj \
    --from-literal=password=secret123 \
    -n anihpj

  # Type 2: TLS (certificate + private key)
  kubectl create secret tls anihpj-tls \
    --cert=server.crt \
    --key=server.key \
    -n anihpj
  → Validates that cert and key are valid PEM

  # Type 3: Docker Registry credentials
  kubectl create secret docker-registry ecr-credentials \
    --docker-server=123456789.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com \
    --docker-username=AWS \
    --docker-password=$(aws ecr get-login-password) \
    -n anihpj
  → Used as imagePullSecrets in pod specs

For anihpj/jobpost, we create an Opaque Secret for database credentials, a TLS Secret for the Ingress HTTPS certificate, and a docker-registry Secret to pull from AWS ECR. Each Secret type enforces the correct data format.

K8s Fundamentals Q34 of 200
What does setting immutable: true on a ConfigMap or Secret do?
A) Once set, the resource cannot be modified — you must delete and recreate it. Benefits: prevents accidental changes to critical configuration, and significantly improves kubelet performance because it doesn't need to watch for updates (no periodic sync checks)
B) It encrypts the data at rest in etcd using the cluster's configured EncryptionConfiguration provider
C) It makes the ConfigMap or Secret read-only for all users except those with cluster-admin privileges
D) It automatically backs up the resource to an external database before any modification attempt, enabling point-in-time recovery
✅ Correct Answer: A

immutable: true is a performance and safety feature. The kubelet normally watches ConfigMaps/Secrets for changes and re-syncs mounted volumes (~60s delay). With immutable set, the kubelet skips these watches entirely — reducing API server load and kubelet CPU usage in large clusters.

📝 Detailed Explanation
immutable — Performance + Safety
  WITHOUT immutable:
  kubelet watches ConfigMap every 60s → checks for changes → resyncs volumes
  ↑ API server load scales with pod count × ConfigMap count

  WITH immutable: true:
  kubelet mounts once → NEVER watches again
  ↑ Zero watch overhead for this ConfigMap
  ↑ Cannot be accidentally modified (safety)

  USE: Production ConfigMaps/Secrets that change via CI/CD (new deployment)
  NOT: Dev ConfigMaps you tweak frequently during development

For anihpj/jobpost, production ConfigMaps should be immutable — changes go through CI/CD with a new Deployment. This follows the immutable infrastructure principle: never mutate, always replace.

1.5 Scheduling: Labels, Selectors, Affinity & TaintsQ35-Q42

K8s Fundamentals Q35 of 200
What are Kubernetes Labels and how are they used?
A) Labels are immutable metadata fields set at cluster creation time that define the cluster's region, zone, and provider
B) Labels are key-value pairs attached to Kubernetes objects (Pods, Services, Deployments, Nodes) used for organization, selection, and grouping. They enable selectors (Service → Pod), scheduling (nodeSelector), and operational queries (kubectl get pods -l app=api)
C) Labels are unique identifiers (like UUIDs) automatically assigned by Kubernetes to every resource for internal tracking purposes
D) Labels are security credentials that authenticate pods to the Kubernetes API server for RBAC authorization
✅ Correct Answer: B

Labels are the glue that connects Kubernetes resources. They're arbitrary key-value pairs that YOU define to organize your cluster. Services find pods via label selectors. Deployments find their pods via label selectors. kubectl queries use labels (-l app=api). Scheduling uses labels (nodeSelector, affinity).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Labels — The Glue Connecting Kubernetes Resources
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: anihpj-api-7d8f9                                    │
  │  labels:                                                   │
  │    app: anihpj-api        ← Service selector uses this!    │
  │    tier: backend                                          │
  │    version: v1.2.3        ← Deployment selector uses this  │
  │    env: production                                        │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
              │                           │
              ▼                           ▼
  ┌─────────────────────┐    ┌─────────────────────────────┐
  │ Service: anihpj-api │    │ Deployment: anihpj-api       │
  │ selector:           │    │ selector:                    │
  │   app: anihpj-api   │    │   app: anihpj-api            │
  │                     │    │   version: v1.2.3            │
  │ → Routes to pod ✅  │    │ → Manages pod ✅             │
  └─────────────────────┘    └─────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, every resource gets labels: app: anihpj-api, tier: backend, env: production. The Service uses app: anihpj-api to find pods. The Deployment uses app: anihpj-api, version: v1.2.3. Wrong label = broken Service = 503 errors. This is the #1 cause of "my Service doesn't work" in Kubernetes.

K8s Fundamentals Q36 of 200
What is the difference between equality-based and set-based label selectors?
A) Equality-based selectors support regex patterns; set-based selectors only support exact string matching
B) Equality-based: app=nginx, env!=prod (exact match or not-equal). Set-based: env in (staging,prod), tier notin (frontend), version (exists check). Set-based is more expressive — Services and Deployments support both, but some resources (like ReplicaSets) only support equality-based in their selector field
C) Equality-based selectors are used for Pod scheduling; set-based selectors are used exclusively for NetworkPolicy rules
D) There is no difference — they are two syntaxes for the same functionality and can be used interchangeably in all Kubernetes resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

Equality-based: app=nginx, env!=prod. Simple, exact match or not-equal. Set-based: env in (staging, prod), tier notin (frontend), version (exists check). Set-based is more powerful — it can express OR conditions and existence checks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Label Selectors — Equality vs Set-Based
  EQUALITY-BASED (simple, fast):
  kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj-api        ← exact match
  kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj-api,env=prod  ← AND: both must match
  kubectl get pods -l env!=prod             ← NOT equal

  SET-BASED (expressive, flexible):
  kubectl get pods -l 'env in (staging,prod)'     ← OR: staging OR prod
  kubectl get pods -l 'tier notin (frontend)'     ← NOT in set
  kubectl get pods -l 'version'                   ← EXISTS check (has label)

  ⚠️ LIMITATION: ReplicaSet .spec.selector.matchLabels
     only supports EQUALITY-based selectors!
     This is why Deployment selectors must be equality-based.

For anihpj/jobpost, the Service uses equality-based app: anihpj-api to find pods. NetworkPolicies can use set-based selectors for more complex rules like "allow from namespaces where env in (staging,prod)".

K8s Fundamentals Q37 of 200
What does nodeSelector do and how do you use it?
A) nodeSelector is the simplest scheduling constraint — it tells the scheduler to place a pod ONLY on nodes with specific labels. You first label the node (kubectl label node <name> disktype=ssd), then add nodeSelector: {disktype: ssd} to the pod spec
B) nodeSelector automatically selects the least-loaded node based on CPU and memory utilization metrics from the metrics-server
C) nodeSelector is a kubectl command that interactively prompts you to choose which node to deploy a pod to from a numbered list
D) nodeSelector randomly distributes pods across all available nodes to ensure even workload distribution
✅ Correct Answer: A

nodeSelector is the simplest form of pod-to-node assignment. It's a hard requirement: the node MUST have the specified labels or the pod stays Pending forever.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nodeSelector — Simple Node Assignment
  # Step 1: Label the node
  kubectl label node anih disktype=ssd

  # Step 2: Reference in pod spec
  spec:
    nodeSelector:
      disktype: ssd

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Scheduler: "Pod needs disktype=ssd"                   │
  │                                                         │
  │  NODE 1: labels={disktype: ssd}     ← ✅ MATCH!         │
  │  NODE 2: labels={disktype: hdd}     ← ❌ No match       │
  │  NODE 3: labels={}                  ← ❌ No match       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ⚠️ If no node has the label → Pod stays Pending FOREVER

For anihpj/jobpost, you could use nodeSelector to ensure PostgreSQL runs on a node with SSD storage: nodeSelector: {disktype: ssd}. On your kubeadm cluster, node "anih" would need the label disktype: ssd applied first.

K8s Fundamentals Q38 of 200
What is the difference between requiredDuringScheduling and preferredDuringScheduling in nodeAffinity?
A) requiredDuringScheduling means the pod must run on the specified node; preferredDuringScheduling means the pod may optionally run on other nodes at the scheduler's discretion
B) There is no difference — both are synonyms for the same scheduling behavior introduced in different Kubernetes API versions
C) requiredDuringScheduling is a HARD rule: pod will NOT schedule if no node matches. preferredDuringScheduling is a SOFT preference: scheduler tries to match but will schedule elsewhere if no match is found. The "required" gives you a scheduling guarantee; the "preferred" gives you best-effort placement
D) requiredDuringScheduling applies during initial scheduling only; preferredDuringScheduling also applies during pod eviction and rescheduling
✅ Correct Answer: C

nodeAffinity is the more expressive successor to nodeSelector. requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution = HARD rule (like nodeSelector but supports set-based expressions). preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution = SOFT preference with a weight (1-100). The scheduler adds the weight to its scoring if the preference matches.

📝 Detailed Explanation
nodeAffinity — Hard vs Soft Rules
  spec:
    affinity:
      nodeAffinity:
        requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:  ← HARD
          nodeSelectorTerms:
          - matchExpressions:
            - key: kubernetes.io/arch
              operator: In
              values: [amd64, arm64]
        preferredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution: ← SOFT
        - weight: 80
          preference:
            matchExpressions:
            - key: disktype
              operator: In
              values: [ssd]

  RESULT:
  • Node MUST be amd64 or arm64 (HARD — or pod stays Pending)
  • Scheduler PREFERS ssd nodes (SOFT — will use hdd if no ssd available)
  • weight: 80 means "strong preference, but not a requirement"

For anihpj/jobpost: requiredDuringScheduling: arch=amd64 (must be x86), preferredDuringScheduling: disktype=ssd weight=90 (prefer SSD but HDD is acceptable). This is more flexible than nodeSelector's all-or-nothing approach.

K8s Fundamentals Q39 of 200
What does podAffinity do?
A) podAffinity ensures pods are evenly distributed across all available nodes to maximize fault tolerance
B) podAffinity attracts pods to nodes where other specified pods are already running. Use case: "keep the Django API pod near the Redis cache pod for low-latency localhost communication" or co-locate related services. It uses label selectors to identify which pods to group with
C) podAffinity is a security feature that restricts which pods can communicate with each other based on their ServiceAccount tokens
D) podAffinity is the default Kubernetes scheduling behavior — it always places pods from the same Deployment on the same node
✅ Correct Answer: B

podAffinity says: "schedule me NEAR pods that match this label." It's a co-location constraint — useful when pods benefit from being on the same node or in the same zone (lowest latency). You specify a labelSelector and a topologyKey (e.g., kubernetes.io/hostname for same-node, or topology.kubernetes.io/zone for same-AZ).

📝 Detailed Explanation
podAffinity — "Keep Me Near You"
  spec:
    affinity:
      podAffinity:
        requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
        - labelSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: anihpj-redis    ← "Find pods with app=anihpj-redis"
          topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname  ← "On the SAME NODE"

  RESULT: Django API pod schedules on the same node as Redis
  → localhost communication = zero network latency!

  NODE 1: [anihpj-redis] ← Django pod schedules HERE
  NODE 2: [empty]        ← Not preferred (no Redis here)

For anihpj/jobpost, you could use podAffinity to put the Django API on the same node as Redis for ultra-low-latency cache access. However, this reduces fault tolerance — if that node fails, both go down. Affinity is a trade-off: latency vs resilience.

K8s Fundamentals Q40 of 200
What does podAntiAffinity do and when would you use it?
A) podAntiAffinity prevents pods from running on the same node as the Kubernetes control plane components
B) podAntiAffinity is a security policy that blocks pods from communicating with each other over the network
C) podAntiAffinity repels pods away from nodes where other specified pods are running. Use case: spread database replicas across different nodes/zones for high availability, or keep CPU-intensive pods apart to avoid resource contention. "Keep me AWAY from pods matching this label"
D) podAntiAffinity is automatically enabled for all StatefulSet pods to ensure they never share a node
✅ Correct Answer: C

podAntiAffinity is the inverse of podAffinity — it pushes pods APART. The most common use case is spreading replicas across nodes for high availability: "don't put two anihpj-db pods on the same node — if that node fails, we lose quorum."

📝 Detailed Explanation
podAntiAffinity — "Keep Me Away From You"
  spec:
    affinity:
      podAntiAffinity:
        requiredDuringSchedulingIgnoredDuringExecution:
        - labelSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: anihpj-db
          topologyKey: kubernetes.io/hostname

  RESULT: No two anihpj-db pods on the same node

  BEFORE:                  AFTER (with antiAffinity):
  NODE 1: [db-0, db-1] ❌  NODE 1: [db-0] ✅
  NODE 2: [db-2]           NODE 2: [db-1] ✅
                           NODE 3: [db-2] ✅

For anihpj/jobpost, podAntiAffinity on PostgreSQL replicas ensures that a single node failure doesn't take down the entire database cluster. Combined with podAffinity for low-latency pairs, you build a resilient, performant topology.

K8s Fundamentals Q41 of 200
What are Taints and Tolerations and how do they work together?
A) Taints are node-level firewall rules that block network traffic; Tolerations are pod-level rules that allow specific IP ranges through the firewall
B) Taints are applied to pods to mark them as "untrusted"; Tolerations are applied to nodes to accept untrusted pods in a sandboxed environment
C) Taints and Tolerations are a deprecated scheduling mechanism replaced by nodeAffinity in Kubernetes v1.19
D) Taints are applied to NODES to REPEL pods (unless tolerated). Tolerations are applied to PODS to allow them on tainted nodes. Think of it as: "This node is reserved for GPU workloads only" (taint) and "I'm a GPU workload, I can go there" (toleration). Taints have three effects: NoSchedule (hard), PreferNoSchedule (soft), NoExecute (evict existing pods)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Taints and Tolerations work like a VIP club: the node has a bouncer (taint) that says "no entry unless you're on the list." Pods need a VIP pass (toleration) to get in. Unlike nodeSelector/nodeAffinity which ATTRACT pods to nodes, taints REPEL pods — it's the inverse mechanism.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Taints & Tolerations — The VIP Club
  # TAINT A NODE: "Only GPU workloads allowed here"
  kubectl taint node gpu-node-1 gpu=true:NoSchedule

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NODE: gpu-node-1                                       │
  │  TAINT: gpu=true:NoSchedule                             │
  │                                                          │
  │  Pod A: No toleration → ❌ REJECTED (NoSchedule)         │
  │  Pod B: toleration gpu=true → ✅ ALLOWED                 │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  TAINT EFFECTS:
  • NoSchedule: Don't schedule NEW pods without toleration (HARD)
  • PreferNoSchedule: Try not to schedule (SOFT)
  • NoExecute: Evict EXISTING pods without toleration + block new

  # Remove a taint
  kubectl taint node gpu-node-1 gpu=true:NoSchedule-

  # Every control-plane node has this taint by default:
  node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule
  → Regular pods don't tolerate this → they stay off CP nodes

On your kubeadm cluster, node "anih" has the control-plane taint. Regular anihpj pods don't tolerate it, so they can't schedule there (keeping the control plane clean for system components). Taints REPEL, Tolerations ALLOW. This is a KCNA exam essential.

K8s Fundamentals Q42 of 200
What does topologySpreadConstraints do?
A) topologySpreadConstraints is a network policy that restricts pod-to-pod traffic based on network topology zones
B) topologySpreadConstraints evenly distributes pods across topology domains (zones, nodes, regions) based on a maxSkew value. Example: with maxSkew=1 and topologyKey=zone, pods are spread so no zone has more than 1 extra pod compared to others. Used for true high availability across failure domains
C) topologySpreadConstraints automatically creates pods in every availability zone regardless of the deployment's replica count
D) topologySpreadConstraints is a replacement for podAntiAffinity that provides the same functionality with a simpler YAML syntax
✅ Correct Answer: B

topologySpreadConstraints is the modern, flexible way to spread pods across failure domains. It's more powerful than podAntiAffinity because it works across multiple topology keys simultaneously with fine-grained maxSkew control.

📝 Detailed Explanation
topologySpreadConstraints — Zone-Aware Spreading
  WITHOUT constraints:
  Zone A: [P1][P2][P3][P4]  Zone B: [P5]  ← Uneven! Zone A failure = 4 pods lost

  WITH maxSkew=1, topologyKey=zone:
  Zone A: [P1][P2][P3]      Zone B: [P4][P5]  ← Balanced! max 1 extra per zone
  Skew = max(3,2) - min(3,2) = 1 ✅

  For anihpj/jobpost: spread Django API pods across nodes AND zones
  → Node failure = 1 pod lost, Zone failure = half the pods still running

For anihpj/jobpost, combining topologySpreadConstraints (spread across zones) with podAntiAffinity (no two DB pods on same node) gives the strongest HA configuration. This is the KCNA-level understanding of production-ready scheduling.

1.6 Kubernetes Architecture & ComponentsQ43-Q50

K8s Fundamentals Q43 of 200
What are the components of the Kubernetes Control Plane and what does each do?
A) The control plane consists of kubelet, kube-proxy, and the container runtime running on a dedicated master node
B) The control plane is a single binary called kube-apiserver that handles all cluster operations including scheduling, storage, and networking
C) The control plane runs only in managed cloud Kubernetes services (EKS, GKE, AKS) and is not available in self-hosted clusters
D) Four main components: API Server (front-end REST API, all communication goes through it), etcd (distributed key-value store for all cluster state), kube-scheduler (assigns pods to nodes), kube-controller-manager (runs reconciliation loops for nodes, replicas, endpoints, etc.)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The control plane is the "brain" of Kubernetes. Think of it as a company: API Server = receptionist (all requests go through it), etcd = filing cabinet (stores everything), Scheduler = office manager (assigns work to workers), Controller Manager = department heads (constantly checking things are as they should be).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Control Plane Components
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                   CONTROL PLANE                           │
  │                                                           │
  │  kubectl ──▶ ┌─────────────┐                              │
  │              │ API Server  │  "Front desk" — ALL requests │
  │              │   :6443     │   go through this            │
  │              └──────┬──────┘                              │
  │                     │                                     │
  │         ┌───────────┼───────────┐                        │
  │         ▼           ▼           ▼                        │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐                 │
  │  │  etcd    │ │Scheduler │ │Controller│                 │
  │  │  :2379   │ │          │ │ Manager  │                 │
  │  │ "Filing  │ │ "Office  │ │ "Dept    │                 │
  │  │ Cabinet" │ │ Manager" │ │ Heads"   │                 │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster (node "anih"), all four components run as static pods in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/. The API server listens on port 6443 — every kubectl command, every controller action, and every kubelet heartbeat goes through it. This centralized architecture is what makes Kubernetes auditable and secure.

K8s Fundamentals Q44 of 200
What is the role of the Kubernetes API Server?
A) The API Server is a load balancer that distributes incoming HTTP requests across all running pods in a Service
B) The API Server is the front-end to the control plane — ALL operations (kubectl, controllers, kubelets) go through it. It handles authentication (who are you?), authorization (are you allowed?), admission control (should this be allowed?), and is the ONLY component that reads/writes to etcd
C) The API Server is a monitoring component that collects metrics from all pods and nodes and exposes them through a Prometheus-compatible endpoint
D) The API Server is responsible for scheduling pods to nodes and managing the container lifecycle on worker nodes
✅ Correct Answer: B

The API Server is the single entry point for all cluster operations. Every kubectl command, every controller reconciliation check, every kubelet heartbeat — all go through the API Server. It's the only component with direct etcd access. This centralized design is what enables auditing, authentication, and authorization.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Server — The Front Desk
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    API SERVER (:6443)                       │
  │                                                             │
  │  REQUEST FLOW:                                              │
  │  kubectl apply → API Server                                 │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       ├── 1. Authentication: Who are you?                   │
  │       │   (cert, token, OIDC, webhook)                      │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       ├── 2. Authorization: Are you allowed?                │
  │       │   (RBAC, ABAC, Node, Webhook)                       │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       ├── 3. Admission Control: Should this happen?         │
  │       │   (Mutating → Validating webhooks)                  │
  │       │                                                     │
  │       └── 4. etcd: Store the desired state                  │
  │                                                             │
  │  KEY: API Server is the ONLY component writing to etcd      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster, the API Server runs as a static pod at /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml listening on port 6443. When you type kubectl get pods, kubeconfig points to https://192.168.x.x:6443 — the API Server.

K8s Fundamentals Q45 of 200
What is etcd and why is it critical to Kubernetes?
A) etcd is a distributed, consistent key-value store that holds ALL Kubernetes cluster state — every pod, service, deployment, secret, and config. It uses the RAFT consensus algorithm requiring a majority (quorum = N/2+1) of members to agree on changes. If etcd is lost without backup, the entire cluster state is gone
B) etcd is a logging daemon that collects and aggregates logs from all containers running on a Kubernetes node
C) etcd is a container network interface plugin that manages pod-to-pod communication and IP address allocation
D) etcd is a Kubernetes controller that manages the lifecycle of persistent volumes and storage classes
✅ Correct Answer: A

etcd is the "source of truth" for Kubernetes. Every kubectl create, every pod status update, every node heartbeat — all stored in etcd. It uses RAFT consensus: writes go to the leader, which replicates to followers. For a 3-node etcd cluster, you need 2 nodes up (quorum = 3/2+1 = 2). Back up etcd regularly — losing it means losing your entire cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd — The Brain's Memory
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    etcd CLUSTER                             │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐           │
  │  │  NODE 1  │◀───▶│  NODE 2  │◀───▶│  NODE 3  │           │
  │  │ LEADER   │ RAFT│ FOLLOWER │ RAFT│ FOLLOWER │           │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────────┘           │
  │                                                             │
  │  QUORUM = N/2 + 1 = 2 (need 2 nodes for consensus)         │
  │  If 2 nodes fail → etcd READ-ONLY (no quorum)              │
  │                                                             │
  │  STORES:                                                    │
  │  /registry/pods/anihpj/anihpj-api-7d8f9                    │
  │  /registry/services/anihpj/anihpj-api                      │
  │  /registry/deployments/anihpj/anihpj-api                   │
  │  /registry/secrets/anihpj/db-credentials                   │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster, etcd runs as a static pod. You can back it up with: ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd.db. The KCNA exam WILL test that you know etcd is the cluster's single source of truth.

K8s Fundamentals Q46 of 200
How does the kube-scheduler decide which node to place a pod on?
A) The scheduler randomly selects a node from the pool of available nodes to ensure even distribution
B) The scheduler uses a round-robin algorithm to distribute pods evenly across all worker nodes
C) Two-phase process: 1) FILTERING — removes nodes that don't meet the pod's requirements (resource capacity, nodeSelector, taints, port conflicts), 2) SCORING — ranks remaining nodes by fitness (spreading pods across nodes/zones, favoring least-requested nodes). The pod is bound to the highest-scoring node
D) The scheduler asks the API Server to randomly select a node, then verifies the selection against taints and tolerations
✅ Correct Answer: C

The scheduler runs a two-phase algorithm every time a new pod appears without a node assignment. Filtering eliminates nodes that can't run the pod. Scoring ranks the survivors and picks the best one.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scheduler — Filtering → Scoring → Binding
  NEW POD: anihpj-api-7d8f9 (request: 500m CPU, 512Mi RAM)
  │
  ├── PHASE 1: FILTERING (Can this node run the pod?)
  │   NODE 1: 1 CPU free → ❌ FAIL (needs 500m, only 1 CPU total)
  │   NODE 2: 2 CPU free → ✅ PASS → goes to scoring
  │   NODE 3: 3 CPU free → ✅ PASS → goes to scoring
  │
  └── PHASE 2: SCORING (Which node is BEST?)
      • NodeResourcesLeastAllocated: prefer nodes with most free resources
      • NodeResourcesBalancedAllocation: prefer balanced CPU/memory
      • ImageLocality: prefer nodes that already have the image cached
      • PodTopologySpread: spread pods across zones
      │
      NODE 2: Score 85   NODE 3: Score 92  ← WINNER!
      │
      └── BINDING: Pod bound to Node 3

For anihpj/jobpost, when deploying 3 API pods, the scheduler spreads them across nodes for high availability. If a node already has the Django image cached (ImageLocality plugin), it gets a scoring bonus — pods start faster.

K8s Fundamentals Q47 of 200
What does the kube-controller-manager do?
A) It manages the lifecycle of container images, pulling them from registries and caching them on worker nodes
B) It monitors cluster health and automatically replaces failed control plane components by restarting their static pods
C) It provides a web UI dashboard for managing Kubernetes resources through a graphical interface
D) It runs a collection of controllers, each implementing a reconciliation loop: observe current state (from API Server), compare to desired state (from etcd), take action to converge. Examples: Node controller (monitors node health), Replication controller (ensures N pods), Endpoints controller (populates Service endpoints), ServiceAccount controller (creates default SAs)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The controller-manager is actually many controllers bundled together, each watching a specific resource type. They all follow the same pattern: Observe → Diff → Act.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kube-controller-manager — All Controllers in One Binary
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              KUBE-CONTROLLER-MANAGER (single binary)       │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐    │
  │  │ Node Ctrl    │  │ReplicaSet Ctrl│  │ Endpoint Ctrl│    │
  │  │ Watches:     │  │ Watches:      │  │ Watches:     │    │
  │  │ node status  │  │ RS vs pods    │  │ Svc selector │    │
  │  │ → marks      │  │ → creates/    │  │ → populates  │    │
  │  │   NotReady   │  │   deletes pods│  │   Endpoints  │    │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘    │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐    │
  │  │ SA Ctrl     │  │Namespace Ctrl│  │ Deployment   │    │
  │  │ Creates      │  │ Garbage       │  │ Ctrl         │    │
  │  │ default SA   │  │ collects      │  │ Manages      │    │
  │  │ + token      │  │ empty NS      │  │ rollouts     │    │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ALL controllers share: Observer pattern → Reconciliation loop → etcd

For anihpj/jobpost, when you delete a pod, the ReplicaSet controller (running inside controller-manager) detects the gap and creates a replacement — all happening in the same binary on your kubeadm control plane.

K8s Fundamentals Q48 of 200
What are the components running on a Kubernetes Worker Node?
A) Three main components: kubelet (node agent — registers node, manages pod lifecycle, reports status), kube-proxy (network proxy — maintains iptables/IPVS rules so Services route to pods), and the Container Runtime (containerd or CRI-O — pulls images and runs containers via CRI)
B) Worker nodes only run the container runtime; all other components run on the control plane and SSH into workers to manage pods
C) Worker nodes run etcd, the API server, and the scheduler — all control plane components are distributed across workers for high availability
D) Worker nodes run kubelet and a lightweight API server proxy; the actual containers run on the control plane node for security isolation
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every worker node runs three essential components. kubelet is the node's "manager" — it registers the node, watches for assigned pods, and reports health. kube-proxy is the network "router" — it writes iptables/IPVS rules so Services can reach pods. The Container Runtime (containerd/CRI-O) does the actual container work — pull, create, start, stop.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Worker Node Components
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    WORKER NODE                            │
  │                                                           │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌───────────────┐  │
  │  │   kubelet    │  │  kube-proxy  │  │   Container   │  │
  │  │   :10250     │  │              │  │   Runtime     │  │
  │  │              │  │  iptables/   │  │               │  │
  │  │ "Node Agent" │  │  IPVS rules  │  │ containerd/   │  │
  │  │ • Register   │  │              │  │   CRI-O       │  │
  │  │ • Pod mgmt   │  │ "Network     │  │               │  │
  │  │ • Health     │  │  Proxy"      │  │ "Container    │  │
  │  │ • Reports    │  │              │  │  Runner"      │  │
  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └───────────────┘  │
  │                                                           │
  │  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐                          │
  │  │ P1│ │ P2│ │ P3│ │ P4│ │ P5│   ← Pods (user workloads) │
  │  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster, node "anih" acts as both control plane AND worker (single-node setup). The kubelet manages all pods including static pods for the control plane components. kubectl get nodes shows "anih" as Ready because kubelet is running and reporting health.

K8s Fundamentals Q49 of 200
What is the reconciliation loop, and why is it the heart of Kubernetes?
A) The reconciliation loop is a network protocol that synchronizes container images between the registry and worker nodes
B) Every controller continuously: 1) Observes current state (from API Server), 2) Compares to desired state (from etcd/spec), 3) Takes action to converge (create/update/delete resources). This loop runs FOREVER — if something drifts (pod dies, config changes), the controller fixes it automatically. This is Kubernetes self-healing
C) The reconciliation loop is a one-time process that runs during cluster bootstrap to ensure all nodes are properly configured
D) The reconciliation loop reconciles billing information between cloud providers and the Kubernetes cluster for cost allocation
✅ Correct Answer: B

The reconciliation loop is the core design pattern of Kubernetes. Every controller runs an infinite loop: check what IS (current state), check what SHOULD BE (desired state from spec), and take action to close the gap. This is why Kubernetes is declarative — you declare what you WANT, and controllers make it HAPPEN.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Reconciliation Loop — Observe → Diff → Act
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              RECONCILIATION LOOP (infinite)                 │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐           │
  │  │ OBSERVE  │────▶│  DIFF    │────▶│   ACT    │           │
  │  │ Current  │     │ Desired  │     │ Create/  │           │
  │  │ State    │     │ - Actual │     │ Update/  │           │
  │  │          │     │   = Gap  │     │ Delete   │           │
  │  └──────────┘     └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘           │
  │       ▲                                 │                 │
  │       └─────────────────────────────────┘                 │
  │                   Loop forever                            │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  EXAMPLE: ReplicaSet reconciler
  Desired: replicas=3    Actual: 2 pods running
  → GAP = 1 → Create new pod → Desired=3, Actual=3 ✅

This is the #1 concept to understand for KCNA. Kubernetes is NOT an imperative system (do this, then that). It's declarative (here's what I want) with controllers continuously reconciling to that desired state.

K8s Fundamentals Q50 of 200
What happens when you run kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml?
A) 1) kubectl reads kubeconfig, 2) sends HTTPS request to API Server (:6443), 3) API Server authenticates (cert), authorizes (RBAC), admission control (webhooks), 4) writes to etcd, 5) Deployment controller detects new desired state, 6) creates ReplicaSet, 7) ReplicaSet controller creates Pods, 8) Scheduler assigns pods to nodes, 9) Kubelet on assigned node starts containers
B) kubectl directly creates pods on the node by SSH-ing into each worker and running docker run commands
C) kubectl sends the YAML to etcd directly, bypassing the API Server for performance; controllers poll etcd for changes
D) kubectl broadcasts the deployment to all nodes via a gossip protocol; each node independently decides whether to run the pod
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is the complete request flow through Kubernetes. Every step is a specific component doing its job. Understanding this flow means you understand the entire architecture.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Complete Request Flow — kubectl apply to Running Pod
  kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml
  │
  ▼
  1. KUBECONFIG: reads current-context → https://192.168.x.x:6443
  │
  ▼
  2. API SERVER: Authentication (cert) → Authorization (RBAC) → Admission
  │
  ▼
  3. ETCD: stores Deployment object at /registry/deployments/anihpj/anihpj-api
  │
  ▼
  4. DEPLOYMENT CONTROLLER: WATCHES etcd → "New Deployment! Desired=3, Actual=0"
  │    → Creates ReplicaSet in etcd
  ▼
  5. REPLICASET CONTROLLER: "Desired=3, Actual=0" → Creates 3 Pods in etcd
  │
  ▼
  6. SCHEDULER: WATCHES for unassigned Pods → Filtering → Scoring → Bind to Node
  │
  ▼
  7. KUBELET (on assigned node): WATCHES for assigned Pods → CRI: pull image
  │    → create container → start → report status back to API Server
  ▼
  8. ✅ Pod Running! (kubectl get pods shows 1/1 Running)

For anihpj/jobpost on your kubeadm cluster: kubectl apply -f django-api-deploy.yaml triggers this entire chain. Every component plays its role. This flow ties together Q43-Q50 — if you understand it, you understand Kubernetes architecture.

K8s Fundamentals Q51 of 200
What does kubeadm init do step-by-step when bootstrapping a Kubernetes cluster?
A) kubeadm init downloads and starts all containers defined in a Docker Compose file, then registers them with the Kubernetes API
B) kubeadm init connects to a cloud provider and provisions virtual machines, installs an operating system, and configures networking automatically
C) kubeadm init performs sequential phases: preflight checks → generates CA + certs → creates kubeconfig files → generates static pod manifests (etcd, API server, controller-manager, scheduler) → starts control plane → uploads config to kubeadm-config ConfigMap → outputs join command for workers
D) kubeadm init clones a Git repository containing Kubernetes YAML files and applies them to create a cluster from declarative configuration
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubeadm init is the cluster bootstrap tool. It runs through well-defined phases: preflight checks (CPU, memory, kernel version), certificate generation (CA, API server, etcd certs), kubeconfig files (admin.conf, kubelet.conf), static pod manifests (etcd.yaml, kube-apiserver.yaml, kube-controller-manager.yaml, kube-scheduler.yaml) in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/, then waits for the control plane to be ready. Finally it outputs the kubeadm join command for worker nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeadm init — Phase by Phase
  kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr=10.244.0.0/16
  │
  ├── [preflight]    Check: 2+ CPU, 2GB+ RAM, kernel version
  ├── [certs]        Generate: ca.crt, apiserver.crt, etcd/ca.crt...
  ├── [kubeconfig]   Create: admin.conf, kubelet.conf, controller-manager.conf
  ├── [kubelet-start] Start kubelet (systemd service)
  ├── [control-plane] Write static pod manifests to /etc/kubernetes/manifests/
  │    ├── etcd.yaml
  │    ├── kube-apiserver.yaml
  │    ├── kube-controller-manager.yaml
  │    └── kube-scheduler.yaml
  ├── [etcd]         Wait for etcd to become healthy
  ├── [upload-config] Store cluster config in kubeadm-config ConfigMap
  ├── [mark-control-plane] Apply taint: node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane
  └── [done]         Output: kubeadm join <IP>:6443 --token <token> ...

On your kubeadm cluster, kubeadm init was run once on node "anih" to create the cluster. The static pod manifests in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ are what kubelet watches to run the control plane. This is a KCNA fundamental — you need to know what kubeadm does under the hood.

K8s Fundamentals Q52 of 200
How do worker nodes join a Kubernetes cluster using kubeadm?
A) kubeadm join uses a bootstrap token (generated by kubeadm init) and a CA certificate hash for secure discovery of the API server. The worker registers with the control plane, receives its client certificate, and the control plane begins scheduling pods to it
B) Workers join by running kubeadm connect and entering the cluster administrator's username and password for authentication
C) Workers automatically discover the control plane via mDNS (multicast DNS) and join without any explicit configuration
D) Workers join by sending an SSH public key to the control plane; the control plane then pushes its kubeconfig to the worker via SCP
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubeadm join uses two critical pieces of information: a bootstrap token (for initial authentication) and the CA certificate hash (--discovery-token-ca-cert-hash) to verify the API server's identity (preventing MITM attacks). Tokens expire after 24 hours by default; use kubeadm token create to generate new ones.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeadm join — Worker Node Joining
  WORKER NODE                            CONTROL PLANE
  ┌──────────────┐                      ┌──────────────┐
  │ kubeadm join │                      │ API Server   │
  │ <IP>:6443    │                      │   :6443      │
  │ --token xxx  │──── secure TLS ────▶│              │
  │ --discovery  │    (CA hash check)   │ Verify token │
  │ -token-ca    │                      │ Issue cert   │
  │ -cert-hash   │                      │ Register node│
  └──────┬───────┘                      └──────┬───────┘
         │                                     │
         │  Node registered!                    │
         │◀─────────────────────────────────────│
         │                                     │
  ┌──────▼───────┐                      ┌──────▼───────┐
  │ kubelet      │── heartbeat ───────▶│ etcd: node   │
  │ starts       │                      │   condition  │
  │ reporting    │                      │   = Ready    │
  └──────────────┘                      └──────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster, node "anih" is both control plane and worker (single-node). In a multi-node setup, worker nodes would run kubeadm join with the token shown after kubeadm init. KCNA tip: remember that tokens expire in 24h — you'll need to generate new ones for late-joining workers.

K8s Fundamentals Q53 of 200
What is the correct process for upgrading a kubeadm Kubernetes cluster?
A) Run kubeadm upgrade on all nodes simultaneously; Kubernetes handles the rolling upgrade automatically
B) On control plane: 1) apt-get install new kubeadm, 2) kubeadm upgrade plan, 3) kubeadm upgrade apply, 4) upgrade kubelet+kubectl. On each worker: 1) drain the worker, 2) upgrade kubeadm, 3) kubeadm upgrade node, 4) upgrade kubelet, 5) uncordon. Control plane upgrades FIRST, then workers
C) Delete all pods, run kubeadm reset, install new Kubernetes version, run kubeadm init again with the new version
D) Use kubectl upgrade cluster which automates the entire process including node draining and pod rescheduling
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cluster upgrades follow a strict order: control plane first, then workers. The version skew policy allows the kubelet to be at most one minor version behind the API server. Workers must be drained before upgrading to safely move pods to other nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeadm upgrade — Step by Step
  # ON CONTROL PLANE:
  apt-mark unhold kubeadm
  apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.x-*
  kubeadm upgrade plan       ← Verify what will happen
  kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.x
  apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.x-* kubectl=1.31.x-*
  systemctl restart kubelet

  # ON EACH WORKER:
  kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets   ← Move pods away
  apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.x-*
  kubeadm upgrade node
  apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.x-*
  systemctl restart kubelet
  kubectl uncordon worker-1                    ← Allow pods back

  ⚠️ VERSION SKEW: kubelet can be ±1 minor version from API server

For anihpj/jobpost, before upgrading the kubeadm cluster, you'd first ensure database backups are current. The KCNA exam expects you to know the upgrade ORDER: control plane → workers, and the drain → upgrade → uncordon workflow for each worker.

K8s Fundamentals Q54 of 200
Why is etcd backup critical and how do you perform it?
A) etcd automatically replicates data across nodes; manual backups are unnecessary because the RAFT consensus algorithm ensures data durability
B) etcd backup is performed by simply copying the /var/lib/etcd directory to another location
C) etcd backup is handled automatically by the kube-scheduler which periodically writes snapshots to a configured PersistentVolume
D) etcd contains ALL cluster state — losing it means losing your entire cluster. Backup: ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /backup/etcd.db with proper TLS certificates (--cacert, --cert, --key flags). This creates a point-in-time snapshot of the entire cluster state
✅ Correct Answer: D

etcd backup = cluster survival. If etcd data is corrupted or lost, every pod, service, deployment, secret, and config is gone. The snapshot captures the entire cluster state at a point in time. For anihpj/jobpost, this means regular etcd backups are as important as PostgreSQL backups — both are critical data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Backup — Full TLS Flags Required
  ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save /opt/etcd-backup.db \
    --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
    --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
    --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
    --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key

  # Verify the snapshot is valid
  ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot status /opt/etcd-backup.db

  # On your kubeadm cluster:
  # etcd runs as a static pod — the certs are in /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/
  # Schedule this command as a CronJob or systemd timer

KCNA exam essential: etcd backup is one of the most tested concepts. Remember the full TLS flags — you can't connect to etcd without them in a production cluster.

K8s Fundamentals Q55 of 200
How do you restore a Kubernetes cluster from an etcd snapshot?
A) Copy the snapshot file to /var/lib/etcd/ and restart the etcd pod — etcd automatically detects and applies snapshots
B) 1) etcdctl snapshot restore to a new data directory, 2) Update the etcd static pod manifest to point --data-dir to the new path and change --initial-cluster-token, 3) kubelet restarts etcd with restored data, 4) All control plane components reconnect and resume from the restored state
C) Run kubectl apply -f etcd-restore.yaml which triggers a Kubernetes Job that restores etcd from the specified snapshot
D) Delete the etcd pod and it will automatically pull the latest snapshot from a configured cloud storage bucket
✅ Correct Answer: B

Restoring etcd requires updating the static pod manifest AND changing the initial-cluster-token. Without the new token, etcd thinks it's the same cluster and may reject the restored data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
etcd Restore — The Critical Steps
  # 1. Restore snapshot to new directory
  ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot restore /backup/etcd.db \
    --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restore \
    --initial-cluster-token=etcd-restored-cluster  ← NEW token!

  # 2. Edit static pod manifest
  vi /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml
  # Change: --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd  →  /var/lib/etcd-restore

  # 3. kubelet detects manifest change → restarts etcd
  # 4. etcd starts with restored data + new cluster token
  # 5. API server reconnects → cluster state restored!

  ⚠️ Without --initial-cluster-token change → etcd rejects restored data

After restoration, all control plane components reconnect and resume from the restored state. For anihpj/jobpost, this means your deployments, services, and configs all come back from the snapshot.

K8s Fundamentals Q56 of 200
How do you check and renew Kubernetes certificates managed by kubeadm?
A) Kubernetes certificates are managed by cert-manager and automatically renewed 30 days before expiration
B) kubeadm certs check-expiration lists all certificates and their expiry dates. kubeadm certs renew all renews them. Certificates typically expire after 1 year — if API server certs expire, kubectl stops working with x509 errors. Renew BEFORE they expire
C) Certificates are stored in etcd and are valid for the lifetime of the cluster; manual renewal is never needed
D) Certificate renewal requires completely reinstalling the cluster with kubeadm reset && kubeadm init
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubeadm certificates expire after 1 year by default. If they expire, the API server can't establish TLS connections — kubectl fails with x509: certificate has expired.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Certificate Lifecycle in kubeadm
  kubeadm certs check-expiration
  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
  │ CERTIFICATE                  │ EXPIRES              │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┤
  │ admin.conf                   │ Jun 12, 2027 (363d)  │
  │ apiserver                    │ Jun 12, 2027 (363d)  │
  │ apiserver-kubelet-client     │ Jun 12, 2027 (363d)  │
  │ etcd-healthcheck-client      │ Jun 12, 2027 (363d)  │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  kubeadm certs renew all          ← Renew ALL certificates
  systemctl restart kubelet        ← Pick up new certs

  On your kubeadm cluster (node "anih"): check expiration regularly!

The KCNA exam expects you to know this lifecycle task. For anihpj/jobpost, expired certs = entire cluster inaccessible. Set a calendar reminder 30 days before expiration.

K8s Fundamentals Q57 of 200
What does the cordon → drain → uncordon workflow do for node maintenance?
A) Cordon restarts all pods on a node, drain deletes the node from the cluster, uncordon recreates the node from a backup
B) Cordon encrypts node communication, drain frees disk space, uncordon decrypts for normal operations
C) Cordon marks a node as unschedulable (no NEW pods), drain evicts EXISTING pods safely (respecting PDBs) to other nodes, uncordon marks the node schedulable again. Used for safe maintenance without downtime
D) This workflow only works in cloud-managed Kubernetes services (EKS, GKE, AKS) and is not available in kubeadm clusters
✅ Correct Answer: C

The cordon→drain→uncordon workflow is the standard node maintenance procedure. It ensures zero-downtime operations by safely moving all workloads off a node before maintenance, then allowing them back after.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cordon → Drain → Work → Uncordon
  kubectl cordon worker-1     ← "No new pods here"
  kubectl drain worker-1 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
  │                           ← "Move existing pods away"
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │  │worker-1  │  │worker-2  │
  │  │[P1][P2] │  │[P3]      │  BEFORE drain
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘
  │       │              │
  │       ▼              ▼
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
  │  │worker-1  │  │worker-2  │
  │  │[empty]   │  │[P3][P1][P2]│  AFTER drain
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘
  │
  [MAINTENANCE: upgrade kubelet, patch OS, replace hardware]
  │
  kubectl uncordon worker-1   ← "OK, new pods welcome again"

  ⚠️ drain respects PodDisruptionBudgets (PDBs) — won't evict if it
     would violate minAvailable

For anihpj/jobpost, before upgrading a node running PostgreSQL (StatefulSet), you'd drain with --delete-emptydir-data to clean up temporary volumes. The database pod safely moves to another node thanks to its PVC. This is a KCNA fundamental — drain before maintenance, uncordon after.

K8s Fundamentals Q58 of 200
What are static pods and how do they work?
A) Static pods are managed directly by kubelet (not the API server or controllers) from YAML files in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/. Kubelet watches this directory and creates/deletes pods based on file presence. Used for control plane components (etcd, API server, scheduler, controller-manager) in kubeadm clusters. You DELETE the FILE to remove the pod — kubectl delete won't work
B) Static pods are pods with fixed IP addresses that never change across restarts, used for stateful applications like databases
C) Static pods are deprecated pods created with kubectl run (the old imperative syntax) instead of kubectl apply (declarative)
D) Static pods are pods that cannot be updated or deleted once created — they're "static" and immutable like ConfigMaps with immutable: true
✅ Correct Answer: A

Static pods bypass the API server entirely. Kubelet watches /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ (configured via --pod-manifest-path). When a YAML file appears, kubelet creates the pod. When the file is deleted, kubelet removes the pod. You CANNOT delete static pods with kubectl delete — kubelet will recreate them. You MUST delete the manifest file.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Static Pods — Controlled by Kubelet, Not API Server
  /etc/kubernetes/manifests/
  ├── etcd.yaml                    ← kubelet watches this directory
  ├── kube-apiserver.yaml
  ├── kube-controller-manager.yaml
  └── kube-scheduler.yaml

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBELET (on control plane node)                          │
  │  --pod-manifest-path=/etc/kubernetes/manifests            │
  │                                                           │
  │  File created → kubelet creates pod (via CRI)             │
  │  File modified → kubelet updates pod                      │
  │  File deleted  → kubelet removes pod                      │
  │                                                           │
  │  ❌ kubectl delete pod etcd-anih → kubelet RECREATES it!  │
  │  ✅ rm /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml → pod deleted  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On your kubeadm cluster, the control plane runs as static pods. kubectl get pods -n kube-system shows them with node name suffixes (etcd-anih, kube-apiserver-anih). To restart the API server, you could move the YAML out and back — but on the CKA/CKAD exam, deleting static pods is a common task. KCNA key point: control plane = static pods in kubeadm.

K8s Fundamentals Q59 of 200
What does a ResourceQuota do in Kubernetes?
A) ResourceQuota automatically scales pods up or down based on CPU and memory usage metrics
B) ResourceQuota is a monitoring dashboard that displays resource usage across all namespaces in real-time
C) ResourceQuota sets default CPU and memory requests/limits for containers that don't specify them explicitly
D) ResourceQuota limits TOTAL resource consumption per namespace — CPU, memory, storage, and object counts (pods, services, secrets, PVCs, etc.). It prevents one team or application from consuming all cluster resources. kubectl describe quota shows current usage vs limits
✅ Correct Answer: D

ResourceQuota provides namespace-level resource governance. It can limit compute resources (CPU, memory) AND object counts (max 10 pods, max 5 PVCs, max 20 secrets). Without quotas, one namespace can consume everything and starve others — this is the "noisy neighbor" problem at the cluster level.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ResourceQuota — Namespace Resource Governance
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: ResourceQuota
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-quota
    namespace: anihpj
  spec:
    hard:
      requests.cpu: "10"         ← Total CPU requests across all pods
      requests.memory: "20Gi"    ← Total memory requests
      limits.cpu: "20"           ← Total CPU limits
      limits.memory: "40Gi"      ← Total memory limits
      persistentvolumeclaims: "5"← Max 5 PVCs
      pods: "20"                 ← Max 20 pods
      services: "10"             ← Max 10 Services
      secrets: "30"              ← Max 30 Secrets

  kubectl describe quota anihpj-quota -n anihpj
  Name:       anihpj-quota
  Resource    Used    Hard
  --------    ----    ----
  pods        12      20     ← Used 12/20 pods
  services    5       10     ← Used 5/10 services

For anihpj/jobpost, a ResourceQuota prevents the dev team from accidentally deploying 100 pods in the production namespace. Combined with LimitRange (Q60), it provides complete resource governance. KCNA key: Quota = namespace-level, LimitRange = pod-level.

K8s Fundamentals Q60 of 200
What does a LimitRange do and how is it different from a ResourceQuota?
A) LimitRange and ResourceQuota are identical — they both limit total resources per namespace
B) LimitRange sets default, minimum, and maximum resource requests/limits for INDIVIDUAL pods/containers within a namespace. ResourceQuota limits TOTAL resources across ALL pods. Use them together: LimitRange ensures each pod has reasonable defaults, ResourceQuota ensures the namespace doesn't exceed its budget
C) LimitRange applies cluster-wide; ResourceQuota applies per namespace
D) LimitRange controls network bandwidth; ResourceQuota controls CPU and memory
✅ Correct Answer: B

LimitRange = per-pod rules (what each pod can request). ResourceQuota = namespace budget (total across all pods). They work together: LimitRange sets defaults so pods can't be too big; ResourceQuota limits the sum so the namespace can't consume too much.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LimitRange + ResourceQuota — Defense in Depth
  apiVersion: v1
  kind: LimitRange
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-limits
    namespace: anihpj
  spec:
    limits:
    - type: Container
      default:
        cpu: "500m"
        memory: "512Mi"
      defaultRequest:
        cpu: "200m"
        memory: "256Mi"
      max:
        cpu: "2000m"      ← No pod can request more than 2 CPU
        memory: "4Gi"     ← No pod can request more than 4Gi RAM
      min:
        cpu: "100m"       ← Every pod must request at least 100m CPU
        memory: "128Mi"

  TOGETHER:
  LimitRange → "Each pod must be 100m-2000m CPU"
  ResourceQuota → "All pods combined: max 10 CPU total"
  → No single pod can be >2 CPU, and namespace can't exceed 10 CPU

For anihpj/jobpost: LimitRange ensures every pod has sensible defaults (no BestEffort pods slipping through). ResourceQuota ensures the production namespace stays within its allocated cluster share. Both are KCNA fundamentals for cluster governance.

1.8 kubectl & The Kubernetes APIQ61-Q70

K8s Fundamentals Q61 of 200
What are the essential kubectl commands every Kubernetes user must know?
A) kubectl only has three commands: run, stop, and status — everything else is handled by the Kubernetes dashboard
B) kubectl is only used by cluster administrators; application developers interact with Kubernetes exclusively through the dashboard UI
C) kubectl is a deprecated CLI tool replaced by kubeadm for all cluster operations in Kubernetes v1.28+
D) Core commands: get (list resources), describe (detailed info + events), create (imperative), apply (declarative), delete (remove), edit (modify live), explain (built-in docs), logs (container output), exec (run commands inside containers)
✅ Correct Answer: D

kubectl is your universal remote control for Kubernetes. The 9 core commands cover 90% of daily operations. get and describe are for inspection. create and apply are for creating resources. delete and edit are for modification. explain is your built-in reference. logs and exec are for debugging.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl — 9 Essential Commands
  INSPECTION:
  kubectl get pods -n anihpj           ← List resources
  kubectl describe pod anihpj-api-7d8  ← Detailed info + EVENTS

  CREATION:
  kubectl create deploy api --image=anihpj/api:v1  ← Imperative
  kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml                     ← Declarative

  MODIFICATION:
  kubectl delete pod anihpj-api-7d8    ← Remove resource
  kubectl edit deploy api              ← Modify live (opens $EDITOR)

  DOCUMENTATION:
  kubectl explain pod.spec.containers  ← Built-in reference!

  DEBUGGING:
  kubectl logs deploy/api --tail=20    ← View container output
  kubectl exec -it api-7d8 -- sh       ← Shell inside container

KCNA exam tip: Master kubectl explain — it's available offline and is faster than searching documentation during the exam.

K8s Fundamentals Q62 of 200
What is the difference between imperative and declarative Kubernetes commands?
A) Imperative commands are for production; declarative is only for development and testing
B) There is no difference — kubectl run and kubectl apply are aliases for the same operation
C) Imperative: "do this" (kubectl run, kubectl create, kubectl expose — you specify the action). Declarative: "here's what I want" (kubectl apply -f manifest.yaml — Kubernetes figures out how to reconcile). Declarative is preferred for production/GitOps because the YAML is your source of truth stored in Git
D) Imperative uses JSON; declarative uses YAML — they are different serialization formats for the same API calls
✅ Correct Answer: C

Imperative = tell Kubernetes HOW (create this pod!). Declarative = tell Kubernetes WHAT you WANT (I want 3 replicas of this image). Declarative is the cloud native way — you store the YAML in Git, and GitOps tools (Argo CD, Flux) apply it automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Imperative vs Declarative — When to Use Each
  IMPERATIVE ("do this"):
  kubectl run nginx --image=nginx           ← Quick test pod
  kubectl create deploy api --image=v1      ← Fast prototype
  kubectl expose deploy api --port=8000     ← Quick Service
  ✅ FAST for dev/debug    ❌ Not repeatable, not in Git

  DECLARATIVE ("here's what I want"):
  kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml              ← Production standard
  kubectl apply -f service.yaml
  kubectl apply -f k8s/                      ← Apply entire directory
  ✅ Git-friendly, repeatable, reviewable    ❌ More typing upfront

  KUBERNETES INTERNAL: Both go through API Server → etcd.
  Declarative adds diff detection: kubectl apply sends PATCH, not full PUT.

For anihpj/jobpost: use imperative kubectl run for quick debugging pods. Use declarative kubectl apply -f k8s/ for all production resources stored in Git. KCNA key: declarative = cloud native, imperative = convenience.

K8s Fundamentals Q63 of 200
What does kubectl --dry-run=client -o yaml do and why is it so useful?
A) It generates YAML from an imperative command WITHOUT actually creating the resource. Example: kubectl create deploy api --image=nginx --dry-run=client -o yaml outputs the YAML to stdout. This is the #1 exam speed hack — generate YAML in seconds instead of typing it from scratch
B) It creates the resource but marks it as "dry-run" so it's automatically deleted after 24 hours, useful for testing
C) It validates the YAML syntax without connecting to the cluster, similar to a linter for Kubernetes manifests
D) It runs the command on a simulated cluster with fake resources for training purposes
✅ Correct Answer: A

--dry-run=client -o yaml is the #1 productivity technique for Kubernetes. Instead of writing a 25-line Deployment YAML from memory, you type one imperative command and redirect the output to a file. It generates syntactically correct YAML instantly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
dry-run — Generate YAML Instantly
  # Generate a Deployment YAML from one command:
  kubectl create deploy anihpj-api \
    --image=anihpj/api:v1.2.3 \
    --replicas=3 \
    --port=8000 \
    --dry-run=client -o yaml > deploy.yaml

  # Output (25 lines of YAML, instant!):
  apiVersion: apps/v1
  kind: Deployment
  metadata:
    name: anihpj-api
  spec:
    replicas: 3
    selector:
      matchLabels:
        app: anihpj-api
    template:
      ...

  # Also works for: namespace, configmap, secret, service, job, etc.
  kubectl create namespace dev --dry-run=client -o yaml > ns.yaml

KCNA/CKA exam essential: On timed exams, this technique saves 30+ minutes. Generate the skeleton, then edit only the fields you need to change.

K8s Fundamentals Q64 of 200
Why is kubectl describe the #1 debugging command in Kubernetes?
A) kubectl describe opens an interactive debugger that allows you to step through container code line by line
B) kubectl describe shows only CPU and memory usage statistics for performance optimization
C) kubectl describe shows the Events section at the bottom — a chronological log of everything that happened to the resource. For pods, it shows scheduling attempts, image pull status, container starts/stops, probe failures, and warnings. Always check Events first when debugging
D) kubectl describe is only available for nodes and deployments; pods must be debugged using kubectl logs
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl describe is the first command you should run when something breaks. The Events section tells the story: "Why is my pod Pending?" → Events: "0/3 nodes available: insufficient cpu." "Why did my container restart?" → Events: "Liveness probe failed." Always scroll to Events first.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl describe — The Events Story
  kubectl describe pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 -n anihpj

  Events:
    Type     Reason            Age   Message
    ────     ──────            ───   ───────
    Normal   Scheduled         5m    Successfully assigned to worker-2
    Normal   Pulling           5m    Pulling image "anihpj/api:v1.2.3"
    Normal   Pulled            4m    Successfully pulled image
    Normal   Created           4m    Created container api
    Normal   Started           4m    Started container api
    Warning  Unhealthy         1m    Liveness probe failed: HTTP 500
    Normal   Killing           1m    Container api failed liveness probe

  ↑ The Events tell you exactly what happened in chronological order
  This is the #1 debugging pattern in Kubernetes

For anihpj/jobpost debugging: kubectl describe pod shows if the Django pod failed due to OOMKilled, liveness probe timeout, or image pull error. KCNA mantra: when in doubt, kubectl describe.

K8s Fundamentals Q65 of 200
What does kubectl logs --previous do?
A) It shows logs from the PREVIOUS (crashed) container instance — essential for debugging CrashLoopBackOff. Combined with --tail=N and --timestamps for targeted investigation. Without --previous, you only see logs from the current (likely new) container, missing the crash evidence
B) It shows logs from the pod that previously ran on this node before it was rescheduled
C) It reverts the pod's log output to a previous configuration version stored in the Deployment's revision history
D) It shows logs from the previous Kubernetes cluster context before kubectl config use-context was run
✅ Correct Answer: A

When a container crashes and restarts, kubectl logs shows only the NEW container's output. Adding --previous retrieves the CRASHED container's logs — where the error evidence lives.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl logs — Essential Debugging Flags
  kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj
  → Shows CURRENT container logs (probably fine after restart)

  kubectl logs deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj --previous
  → Shows PREVIOUS (CRASHED) container logs ← WHERE THE ERROR IS!

  COMBINED DEBUG COMBO:
  kubectl logs pod/anihpj-api-7d8f9 --previous --tail=100 --timestamps
  → Last 100 lines from crashed container, with timestamps

  OTHER KEY FLAGS:
  --tail=N        Last N lines
  --since=10m     Logs from last 10 minutes
  --timestamps    Show when each line was logged
  --follow (-f)   Stream logs in real-time
  -c <container>   Specific container in multi-container pods

For anihpj/jobpost CrashLoopBackOff debugging: always start with kubectl logs --previous. The crash evidence is in the PREVIOUS container's logs, not the current one.

K8s Fundamentals Q66 of 200
How do you execute commands inside a running container for debugging?
A) You must SSH into the worker node, then use docker exec on the host to access the container
B) Attach a debug sidecar container to the pod and use kubectl port-forward to access it
C) Restart the container with an interactive shell flag, then reconnect once the shell is available
D) kubectl exec -it <pod> -- <command> runs a command inside a running container. kubectl exec -it pod -- sh opens a shell. Use -c <container> for multi-container pods. kubectl exec is read-only by default — no need to restart or modify anything
✅ Correct Answer: D

kubectl exec is your direct line into a running container.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl exec — Inside the Container
  # Interactive shell (if container has /bin/sh)
  kubectl exec -it deploy/anihpj-api -n anihpj -- sh

  # Run a single command
