K8s Fundamentals Q68 of 200
What are Kubernetes contexts and why are they critical for safe operations?
A) Contexts are a feature of the Kubernetes dashboard that saves UI layout preferences for different users
B) A context is a triple: cluster (which API server), user (which credentials), namespace (default namespace). kubectl config use-context switches between them. Contexts prevent the #1 operational disaster: running commands on the wrong cluster. Always verify context before any destructive command
C) Contexts are labels applied to pods to determine which geographic region they run in for latency optimization
D) Contexts determine which container runtime (containerd vs CRI-O) is used for pods scheduled to a particular node
✅ Correct Answer: B

Contexts are your safety mechanism for multi-cluster operations. On your 6-cluster lab with ksw, each cluster has its own context. Running kubectl delete namespace production on the wrong cluster is catastrophic — contexts prevent this. Always run kubectl config current-context before destructive commands.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubeconfig — Contexts, Clusters, Users
  kubeconfig (~/.kube/config):
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  clusters:                                                  │
  │  - name: kubeadm                                            │
  │    server: https://192.168.1.100:6443                      │
  │  - name: kind-cka-lab                                       │
  │    server: https://127.0.0.1:xxxxx                         │
  │                                                             │
  │  users:                                                     │
  │  - name: admin@kubeadm                                      │
  │    client-certificate: /path/to/admin.crt                   │
  │                                                             │
  │  contexts: ← TRIPLE: cluster + user + namespace            │
  │  - name: kubeadm-admin                                      │
  │    cluster: kubeadm                                         │
  │    user: admin@kubeadm                                      │
  │    namespace: default                                       │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  kubectl config use-context kubeadm-admin  ← Switch context
  kubectl config current-context            ← Verify! ALWAYS!

On your lab: ksw switches contexts between the 6 clusters. KCNA safety rule: verify context before every kubectl delete. One wrong command on production is all it takes.

K8s Fundamentals Q69 of 200
How do you extract specific fields using kubectl JSONPath?
A) kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}' extracts pod names. JSONPath uses dot notation: .status.podIP for a single item, .items[*] to iterate arrays, {range}{end} for loops. Custom columns: -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,IP:.status.podIP
B) JSONPath requires installing the jq command-line tool; kubectl does not support it natively
C) kubectl get pods --fields=name,ip returns the same information without needing JSONPath syntax
D) JSONPath only works with kubectl get -o yaml output piped through grep
✅ Correct Answer: A

JSONPath extracts exactly what you need from kubectl output in scripts and automation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
JSONPath & Custom Columns — Precise Data Extraction
  # JSONPath examples:
  kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{.items[*].metadata.name}'
  → anihpj-api-7d8f9 anihpj-api-8f9d2 anihpj-db-0

  kubectl get nodes -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.addresses[?(@.type=="InternalIP")].address}'
  → 192.168.1.100

  # Custom columns (more readable):
  kubectl get pods -o custom-columns=\
  NAME:.metadata.name,\
  NODE:.spec.nodeName,\
  IP:.status.podIP
  NAME               NODE       IP
  anihpj-api-7d8f9   worker-1   10.244.1.5
  anihpj-api-8f9d2   worker-2   10.244.2.8

  # Range loop:
  kubectl get pods -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{"\t"}{.status.podIP}{"\n"}{end}'

For anihpj/jobpost automation: extract pod IPs and node assignments for health check scripts. JSONPath is essential for the KCNA exam and real-world Kubernetes operations.

K8s Fundamentals Q70 of 200
What does kubectl api-resources show and why is it useful?
A) It lists ALL available API resources in your cluster with their short names, API group, and whether they're namespaced. Use it to discover what resources exist and their kubectl shortcuts (e.g., svc for services, deploy for deployments). Essential for exploring custom resources (CRDs) installed by operators
B) It shows the CPU, memory, and storage usage of all API resources currently running in the cluster
C) It lists the API server's internal endpoints and their health status for cluster diagnostics
D) It shows the RBAC permissions of the currently authenticated user across all API groups
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubectl api-resources is your cluster map showing every available resource type.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl api-resources — Discover What's Available
  kubectl api-resources
  NAME         SHORTNAMES   APIVERSION   NAMESPACED   KIND
  pods         po           v1           true         Pod
  services     svc          v1           true         Service
  deployments  deploy       apps/v1      true         Deployment
  ingresses    ing          networking   true         Ingress
  nodes        no           v1           false        Node
  namespaces   ns           v1           false        Namespace

  KEY: NAMESPACED=true → -n flag works. NAMESPACED=false → cluster-wide.
  Custom resources (CRDs) installed by operators also appear here!

1.9 Namespaces, QoS & API BasicsQ71-Q80

K8s Fundamentals Q71 of 200
What are Kubernetes Namespaces and why are they used?
A) Namespaces are a security feature that encrypts data at rest within etcd for sensitive workloads
B) Namespaces are physical partitions of a cluster that run on separate hardware for complete isolation
C) Namespaces are virtual clusters within a physical cluster — they provide logical separation of resources (dev/staging/prod), scope for names (two pods named "api" can exist in different namespaces), and boundaries for RBAC, quotas, and network policies
D) Namespaces are automatically created for each user who authenticates to the cluster, providing personal workspaces
✅ Correct Answer: C

Namespaces partition a cluster into virtual sub-clusters. Resources in different namespaces can have the same name. RBAC, ResourceQuotas, and NetworkPolicies can be scoped to namespaces. The anihpj/jobpost app runs in the anihpj namespace, isolated from other applications.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Namespaces — Virtual Clusters Within a Cluster
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    KUBERNETES CLUSTER                       │
  │                                                             │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌────────────┐ │
  │  │ NAMESPACE: dev  │  │ NAMESPACE: stag │  │ NS: prod   │ │
  │  │ anihpj-api v2   │  │ anihpj-api v2   │  │ anihpj-api │ │
  │  │ anihpj-db (dev) │  │ anihpj-db (stg) │  │ anihpj-db  │ │
  │  │ ResourceQuota:  │  │ ResourceQuota:  │  │ RQ: 20 CPU │ │
  │  │   2 CPU, 4Gi    │  │   4 CPU, 8Gi    │  │   40Gi RAM │ │
  │  └─────────────────┘  └─────────────────┘  └────────────┘ │
  │                                                             │
  │  • Same pod names in different NS → NO conflict             │
  │  • RBAC: dev team can only access "dev" namespace           │
  │  • NetworkPolicy: prod can't talk to dev                    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

KCNA tip: system namespaces: kube-system (control plane), kube-public (public config), kube-node-lease (node heartbeats), default (if you don't specify one). Never run your apps in these system namespaces.

K8s Fundamentals Q72 of 200
How do you permanently set a default namespace for kubectl commands?
A) Export KUBE_NAMESPACE=anihpj in your shell profile; kubectl reads this environment variable
B) kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj permanently sets the namespace for the current context. All subsequent commands default to that namespace. Use -n flag to override per-command. Verify with kubectl config view --minify
C) Create a file at ~/.kube/namespace containing the desired namespace name
D) Namespace can only be set per-command with -n; there is no way to set a default namespace
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj saves the namespace in your kubeconfig.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Setting Default Namespace
  # Set default namespace for current context
  kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj

  # Verify
  kubectl config view --minify | grep namespace
      namespace: anihpj   ← Now all commands default to anihpj

  # Now these are equivalent:
  kubectl get pods                      ← Uses anihpj namespace
  kubectl get pods -n anihpj            ← Same result

  # Override per-command (still works):
  kubectl get pods -n kube-system       ← Temporarily use different NS

  # For your 6-cluster lab:
  ksw → choose cluster → kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=anihpj

KCNA time-saver: Set your namespace once per context. On your kubeadm cluster, this avoids typing -n anihpj for every command.

K8s Fundamentals Q73 of 200
What are QoS (Quality of Service) classes in Kubernetes?
A) QoS classes determine network bandwidth allocation between pods using tc (traffic control) rules
B) QoS classes are user-defined labels (gold/silver/bronze) that determine pod scheduling priority
C) QoS classes control which storage class (fast-ssd vs slow-hdd) is used for a pod's persistent volumes
D) Three classes determined by resource requests/limits: Guaranteed (request==limit for all containers, lowest eviction risk), Burstable (at least one container has request
✅ Correct Answer: D

QoS classes determine who gets killed first when a node runs out of memory. BestEffort pods die first, then Burstable, then Guaranteed. This is why production pods should always be Guaranteed or Burstable — never BestEffort.

📝 Detailed Explanation
QoS Classes — Eviction Priority
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NODE UNDER MEMORY PRESSURE — WHO DIES FIRST?              │
  │                                                             │
  │  1. BESTEFFORT (no requests/limits)     ← KILLED FIRST     │
  │     • No resource guarantees                              │
  │     • OOM score: highest                                  │
  │                                                             │
  │  2. BURSTABLE (request < limit)         ← KILLED SECOND    │
  │     • Has requests but can burst higher                    │
  │     • anihpj-api: request 512Mi, limit 1Gi                │
  │                                                             │
  │  3. GUARANTEED (request == limit)       ← KILLED LAST      │
  │     • Full resource guarantee                              │
  │     • anihpj-db: request 2Gi, limit 2Gi                   │
  │     • Most protected — ideal for databases                │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: PostgreSQL should be Guaranteed (critical data). Django API can be Burstable (can tolerate brief restart). Never run anything as BestEffort in production. KCNA key: QoS = eviction order.

K8s Fundamentals Q74 of 200
What causes an OOMKilled event and how do you investigate it?
A) OOMKilled means the node ran out of disk space — check /var/log for disk usage
B) OOMKilled is a normal Kubernetes operation that balances memory across nodes; no investigation needed
C) Container exceeded its memory limit → Linux OOM killer sends SIGKILL (exit code 137). Investigate: kubectl describe pod (look for OOMKilled reason), kubectl logs --previous (see what was happening), check memory limits (limits.memory may be too low). Fix: either fix the memory leak or increase the limit
D) OOMKilled means the container runtime crashed; restart the kubelet to recover
✅ Correct Answer: C

OOMKilled = container hit its memory limit. Exit code 137 = 128 + 9 (SIGKILL).

📝 Detailed Explanation
OOMKilled — Diagnosis & Fix
  kubectl describe pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 -n anihpj | grep -A5 "State:"
  State:          Running
  Last State:     Terminated
    Reason:       OOMKilled
    Exit Code:    137       ← 128 + 9 = SIGKILL from OOM killer

  # Check memory limits
  kubectl get pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 -o yaml | grep -A2 limits
    limits:
      memory: "512Mi"       ← Too low? Application needs more?

  # Check memory usage trends
  kubectl top pod anihpj-api-7d8f9 -n anihpj
  NAME               CPU   MEMORY
  anihpj-api-7d8f9   200m  498Mi    ← Close to 512Mi limit!

  FIX: Increase limits.memory OR investigate memory leak

For anihpj/jobpost: if the Django API is OOMKilled, check kubectl logs --previous for large queries, then either fix the query or increase limits.memory. Never just blindly increase limits — find the root cause first.

K8s Fundamentals Q75 of 200
What are API groups and versions in Kubernetes?
A) API groups organize resources by domain: core ('' or v1 — pods, services), apps (apps/v1 — deployments, statefulsets), networking.k8s.io ( networking.k8s.io/v1 — ingresses, networkpolicies). The version indicates stability: v1 (stable), v1beta1 (beta, may change), v1alpha1 (alpha, experimental)
B) API groups are user-defined collections of resources for RBAC; versions indicate the resource's creation timestamp
C) All Kubernetes resources are in a single flat API group called "v1" for backward compatibility
D) API groups correspond to different Kubernetes distributions (kubeadm, EKS, GKE, AKS); versions correspond to Kubernetes releases
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every YAML starts with apiVersion: <group>/<version>.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Groups — Organization by Domain
  ┌─────────────────────┬──────────────────┬─────────────────────────┐
  │ API GROUP           │ apiVersion       │ RESOURCES               │
  ├─────────────────────┼──────────────────┼─────────────────────────┤
  │ Core (legacy)       │ v1               │ Pod, Service, Node, NS  │
  │ apps                │ apps/v1          │ Deployment, StatefulSet │
  │ networking.k8s.io   │ networking/v1    │ Ingress, NetworkPolicy  │
  │ batch               │ batch/v1         │ Job, CronJob            │
  │ rbac.authorization  │ rbac/v1          │ Role, RoleBinding       │
  └─────────────────────┴──────────────────┴─────────────────────────┘

  STABILITY LEVELS:
  v1        = Stable/GA (use in production)
  v1beta1   = Beta (may change, 9-month deprecation notice)
  v1alpha1  = Alpha (experimental, can be removed anytime)

For anihpj/jobpost YAML manifests, always use stable API versions (apps/v1 for Deployments, v1 for Services). KCNA tip: know the major API groups — they're tested on the exam.

K8s Fundamentals Q76 of 200
What do apiVersion and kind mean in every Kubernetes YAML?
A) apiVersion specifies the YAML format version; kind specifies whether it's JSON or YAML
B) apiVersion is the application's version number; kind is the environment (dev/staging/prod)
C) apiVersion is the kubectl client version; kind is the resource's unique identifier in etcd
D) apiVersion defines which API group and version to use (e.g., apps/v1). kind defines the resource type (e.g., Deployment, Service, Pod). These two fields tell Kubernetes exactly what you're creating. Every valid Kubernetes manifest MUST start with these two fields
✅ Correct Answer: D

apiVersion and kind are the required first two fields of every Kubernetes YAML manifest.

📝 Detailed Explanation
apiVersion + kind — Every YAML Starts Here
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ apiVersion: apps/v1       ← WHICH API group and version     │
  │ kind: Deployment           ← WHAT type of resource           │
  │ metadata:                  ← WHO (name, labels, namespace)   │
  │   name: anihpj-api                                         │
  │ spec:                      ← WHAT you want (desired state)  │
  │   replicas: 3                                               │
  │   ...                                                       │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  VALID COMBINATIONS:
  apiVersion: v1, kind: Pod
  apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment
  apiVersion: v1, kind: Service
  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1, kind: Ingress

  ❌ apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Service → REJECTED (wrong group!)

Together they uniquely identify what you're creating. The API server validates the combination — wrong group+kind is rejected immediately.

B) It queries the API Server's REST API directly and shows the raw JSON response — the same response kubectl normally parses for you. kubectl get --raw /api returns available API groups. kubectl get --raw /api/v1/pods returns all pods in JSON. This bypasses kubectl's formatting and shows the raw API
C) It shows the raw etcd database contents in hexadecimal format for forensic analysis

K8s Fundamentals Q78 of 200
What does kubectl api-versions show?
A) It lists all API versions available in the cluster — e.g., v1, apps/v1, networking.k8s.io/v1, batch/v1. This helps you know which apiVersion to use in your YAML manifests. If a version isn't listed, you can't use it
B) It shows the version history of the Kubernetes cluster including all past upgrades
C) It displays the kubectl client version and checks if it's compatible with the server version
D) It lists the version of every container image currently running in the cluster

K8s Fundamentals Q79 of 200
What is the Kubernetes API deprecation policy?
A) APIs are deprecated immediately when a new version is released; old versions stop working without warning
B) Deprecated APIs continue working indefinitely for backward compatibility
C) APIs follow a structured deprecation: GA (v1) APIs are supported for 12 months after deprecation announcement. Beta APIs (v1beta1) are supported for 9 months. Alpha APIs (v1alpha1) can be removed anytime. Example: networking.k8s.io/v1beta1 Ingress was deprecated in v1.19 and removed in v1.22
D) API deprecation only applies to cloud-managed Kubernetes services; self-hosted clusters can use any API version indefinitely
✅ Correct Answer: C

Kubernetes never removes APIs without warning.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API Deprecation Timeline
  ┌──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ VERSION  │ DEPRECATION POLICY                           │
  ├──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ v1 (GA)  │ 12 months notice before removal              │
  │ v1beta1  │ 9 months notice, 3 releases supported        │
  │ v1alpha1 │ Can be removed anytime without notice        │
  └──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Example: networking.k8s.io/v1beta1 Ingress
  • Deprecated in v1.19 (announcement)
  • Removed in v1.22 (3 releases later)

  kubectl convert -f old-ingress.yaml --output-version networking/v1
  ↑ Convert deprecated manifests to current API version

For anihpj/jobpost: always use stable v1 API versions. Before upgrading Kubernetes, check kubectl api-versions and migrate any deprecated resources. Never use alpha APIs in production.

K8s Fundamentals Q80 of 200
What is the difference between containerd, CRI-O, gVisor, and Kata Containers?
A) They are all identical — just different names for the same container runtime technology
B) containerd (CNCF graduated, default in most clusters — uses runc for standard Linux isolation). CRI-O (lightweight, Kubernetes-only, no Docker heritage). gVisor (application kernel in userspace — intercepts syscalls for stronger isolation). Kata Containers (dedicated lightweight VM per container — strongest isolation, hardware-backed)
C) containerd runs on Linux, CRI-O on Windows, gVisor on macOS, and Kata on ARM processors
D) containerd is for production, CRI-O for development, gVisor for testing, Kata for CI/CD pipelines
✅ Correct Answer: B

The runtime choice is about the isolation vs performance trade-off.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Container Runtime Spectrum
  ┌──────────────┬────────────┬────────────┬──────────────┐
  │ containerd   │ CRI-O      │ gVisor     │ Kata         │
  │ (CNCF grad)  │ (K8s-only) │(syscall    │(per-pod VM)  │
  │              │            │ filter)    │              │
  ├──────────────┼────────────┼────────────┼──────────────┤
  │ Shares host  │ Shares host│ Intercepts │ Dedicated    │
  │ kernel       │ kernel     │ syscalls   │ guest kernel │
  │ FASTEST      │ FAST       │ SLOWER     │ SLOWEST      │
  │ Default K8s  │ Alt to     │ Multi-     │ Highest      │
  │ runtime      │containerd  │ tenant     │ security     │
  └──────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴──────────────┘

  Your kubeadm cluster: containerd (the CNCF default)
  Use RuntimeClass to select per-pod: runc vs gVisor vs Kata

For anihpj/jobpost: Django API uses containerd (fast, trusted), a tenant workload might use gVisor (syscall filtering), and a highly sensitive workload could use Kata (VM-level isolation).

1.10 Kubernetes Core Concepts — Integration & ReviewQ81-Q88

K8s Fundamentals Q81 of 200
How do Pods, Deployments, Services, and Ingress work together in an end-to-end request flow?
A) Ingress (L7 routing, TLS) → Service (stable endpoint, load balancing) → Pod (runs containers). The Ingress routes by host/path to the Service, the Service routes to healthy Pods via selector labels, and Pods run the application code. Each layer adds a specific capability: routing, stability, execution
B) These four resources are alternative ways to expose an application — you choose one based on your networking requirements
C) Pods and Deployments are the same thing; Services and Ingress are the same thing; they're just different API versions of the same functionality
D) The flow is Pod → Deployment → Service → Ingress; each resource wraps the previous one in a nested hierarchy
✅ Correct Answer: A

This is the complete request flow for anihpj/jobpost. User types https://api.jobpost.com/jobs → Ingress terminates TLS and routes to anihpj-api Service → Service load balances to a healthy pod → Pod's Django container processes the request. Each component adds exactly one responsibility.

📝 Detailed Explanation
End-to-End Request Flow — anihpj/jobpost
  USER: https://api.jobpost.com/jobs
  │
  ▼
  INGRESS (TLS termination, path routing)
  │  host: api.jobpost.com → backend: anihpj-api Service
  │
  ▼
  SERVICE: anihpj-api (ClusterIP 10.96.x.x:8000)
  │  selector: app=anihpj-api → Endpoints: [10.244.1.5:8000, 10.244.2.8:8000]
  │
  ▼
  POD: anihpj-api-7d8f9 (IP: 10.244.1.5)
  │  Container: Django + gunicorn → processes /jobs request
  │  → SELECT * FROM jobs → Service: anihpj-db:5432 → PostgreSQL pod
  │
  ▼
  RESPONSE: JSON array of job listings returned to user

KCNA integration question: This ties together Pods (Q11), Deployments (Q19), Services (will be Q89+), Ingress (Q95+), and DNS (Q99). If you understand this flow, you understand how Kubernetes serves applications.

K8s Fundamentals Q82 of 200
What is the fundamental principle of the Kubernetes networking model?
A) Pods communicate through a central network gateway that performs NAT and port mapping
B) Every Pod gets its own unique, routable IP address. Pods can communicate with all other pods on any node without NAT. This "IP-per-pod" model simplifies networking: no port conflicts, no address translation, pods are first-class network citizens
C) Only pods in the same namespace can communicate directly; cross-namespace traffic requires an Ingress
D) Pods share the node's IP address using port mapping, similar to Docker's -p flag
✅ Correct Answer: B

The IP-per-pod model is the foundational networking principle. Each pod gets a unique IP from the pod CIDR range.

📝 Detailed Explanation
IP-per-Pod Model — No NAT, No Port Conflicts
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod A (10.244.1.5)          Pod B (10.244.2.8)           │
  │  Container: Django :8000     Container: Django :8000      │
  │  Container: nginx  :8080     Container: nginx  :8080      │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       │                              │
       │  Direct pod-to-pod:          │
       │  10.244.1.5:8000 → 10.244.2.8:8000  ← NO NAT, NO port mapping
       │                              │
       └──────────────────────────────┘

  WHY THIS MATTERS:
  • No port conflicts: 100 pods can all listen on :8000
  • No NAT between pods: source IP preserved for NetworkPolicy
  • Simple: every pod is a first-class IP citizen

This is why Kubernetes doesn't have Docker's -p 8080:80 port mapping — every pod gets its own IP, so every pod can use port 80.

K8s Fundamentals Q83 of 200
Quick review: When would you use ClusterIP vs NodePort vs LoadBalancer vs ExternalName?
A) All four types are interchangeable — use whichever is easiest to configure
B) ClusterIP for databases, NodePort for APIs, LoadBalancer for web servers, ExternalName for microservices
C) ClusterIP is deprecated; NodePort is the recommended default for all services
D) ClusterIP: internal-only communication (anihpj-api → anihpj-db). NodePort: quick external access for dev/testing (port 30000-32767). LoadBalancer: production external access with cloud LB. ExternalName: CNAME alias to external services like AWS RDS (no cluster-internal proxy)
✅ Correct Answer: D

Service type is a decision based on access scope.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Type Decision Matrix for anihpj/jobpost
  COMPONENT       SERVICE TYPE    REASON
  ─────────       ────────────    ──────
  PostgreSQL      ClusterIP       Internal only, never exposed
  Redis           ClusterIP       Internal cache
  Django API      LoadBalancer    External access via AWS NLB
  Django Web      LoadBalancer    External access via AWS ALB
  AWS RDS (ext)   ExternalName    CNAME alias, bypass cluster
  Dev API test    NodePort        Quick dev access without LB

Each type solves a different access pattern. For the KCNA exam, you need to know which type to use based on the scenario description.

K8s Fundamentals Q84 of 200
How does Ingress differ from a Service?
A) Service = L4 (TCP/UDP) load balancing with a stable IP. Ingress = L7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing with host/path rules, TLS, and URL rewriting. Ingress sits in FRONT of Services — it routes to Services, not directly to pods. You need BOTH: Ingress for routing, Services for pod discovery
B) Ingress replaces Services entirely — you use one or the other, not both
C) Service and Ingress are the same resource with different names in different API versions
D) Service handles L7 routing; Ingress handles L4 load balancing — they complement each other
✅ Correct Answer: A

Ingress and Services work together, not as alternatives.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress + Service — Complementary Layers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  L7: INGRESS                                         │
  │  • Host routing: api.jobpost.com vs jobpost.com      │
  │  • Path routing: /api → api-svc, / → web-svc         │
  │  • TLS termination: HTTPS at edge                    │
  │  • URL rewriting                                     │
  │         │                                             │
  │         ▼                                             │
  │  L4: SERVICE                                          │
  │  • Stable ClusterIP                                  │
  │  • Load balancing to healthy pods                    │
  │  • Service discovery via DNS                         │
  │         │                                             │
  │         ▼                                             │
  │  PODS: Django API containers                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The flow: Internet → Ingress (L7 routing + TLS) → Service (L4 stable endpoint) → Pod (runs app). You can't use Ingress without a Service — Ingress routes TO a Service.

K8s Fundamentals Q85 of 200
How does Kubernetes DNS work for Service discovery?
A) Kubernetes uses multicast DNS (mDNS) to broadcast service names across the pod network
B) DNS is an optional addon; pods discover services through environment variables injected at startup
C) Every Service gets a DNS A record: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local → ClusterIP. For anihpj: anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local resolves to the API Service's ClusterIP. CoreDNS handles this automatically — pods use it by default via /etc/resolv.conf
D) DNS records are created manually using kubectl create dns-record for each service
✅ Correct Answer: C

DNS is automatic — create a Service, get a DNS record.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes DNS — Automatic Service Discovery
  Service: anihpj-api  (ClusterIP: 10.96.100.50)
  │
  ▼
  CoreDNS automatically creates:
  anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local → 10.96.100.50  (FQDN)
  anihpj-api.anihpj.svc                → 10.96.100.50
  anihpj-api.anihpj                    → 10.96.100.50
  anihpj-api                           → 10.96.100.50  (short name, same NS)

  Pod DNS config (/etc/resolv.conf):
  nameserver 10.96.0.10       ← CoreDNS ClusterIP
  search anihpj.svc.cluster.local svc.cluster.local cluster.local
  ndots:5

For anihpj/jobpost: Django connects to anihpj-db:5432 — DNS resolves this to the PostgreSQL Service's ClusterIP. No hardcoded IPs. This is cloud native service discovery.

K8s Fundamentals Q86 of 200
What does HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) do?
A) HPA automatically increases the CPU and memory limits of running pods when they approach their resource caps
B) HPA provisions new worker nodes when the cluster runs out of capacity to schedule pending pods
C) HPA automatically scales the number of pod replicas based on observed metrics (CPU, memory, or custom). Formula: desiredReplicas = ceil[currentReplicas × (currentMetric / desiredMetric)]. Requires metrics-server and resource requests to be set
D) HPA monitors application logs and automatically rolls back deployments when it detects an increased error rate
✅ Correct Answer: C

HPA = automatic scaling based on demand. If the Django API pods are at 80% CPU, HPA adds replicas. If they drop to 10%, HPA scales down (respecting minReplicas). The formula is: desired = ceil[ current × (actual / target) ]. For anihpj/jobpost, HPA on the API deployment scales from 2 to 10 replicas based on CPU at 70% target.

📝 Detailed Explanation
HPA — Scale Pods Based on Demand
  HPA: anihpj-api (min=2, max=10, target CPU=70%)

  8:00 AM — Low traffic:    2 pods @ 10% CPU  → HPA: keep 2
  12:00 PM — Lunch rush:    2 pods @ 85% CPU  → HPA: scale to ceil(2×85/70)=3
  12:05 PM — Still hot:     3 pods @ 80% CPU  → HPA: scale to ceil(3×80/70)=4
  6:00 PM — Traffic drops:  4 pods @ 15% CPU  → HPA: scale to ceil(4×15/70)=1
                                                     → but minReplicas=2, stay at 2

  REQUIREMENTS:
  • metrics-server running in kube-system
  • Pods must have resource requests set (CPU/memory)
  • Without requests, HPA can't calculate utilization %

KCNA key: HPA scales pods (horizontal), VPA scales resources (vertical), Cluster Autoscaler scales nodes. Know which does what.

K8s Fundamentals Q87 of 200
What does the Cluster Autoscaler do and how is it different from HPA?
A) Cluster Autoscaler and HPA are the same — both scale pods horizontally based on CPU metrics
B) Cluster Autoscaler adds/removes NODES when pods can't be scheduled (Pending due to insufficient resources) or nodes are underutilized. HPA scales PODS (horizontal). Cluster Autoscaler scales NODES (infrastructure). They work together: HPA creates more pods → pods go Pending → Cluster Autoscaler adds nodes
C) Cluster Autoscaler adjusts individual pod resource limits; HPA adjusts pod replica counts
D) Cluster Autoscaler works on-premises; HPA only works in cloud environments
✅ Correct Answer: B

HPA scales pods, Cluster Autoscaler scales nodes. They form a chain of automatic scaling.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scaling Chain — HPA → Cluster Autoscaler
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ TRAFFIC INCREASES:                                         │
  │ 1. HPA: CPU > 70% → adds pods (3 → 5)                     │
  │ 2. New pods Pending: "0/3 nodes available: insufficient cpu"│
  │ 3. Cluster Autoscaler: detects Pending pods → adds node    │
  │ 4. New node Ready → pods schedule → traffic served         │
  │                                                             │
  │ TRAFFIC DECREASES:                                         │
  │ 1. HPA: CPU < 30% → removes pods (5 → 3)                  │
  │ 2. Node becomes underutilized (low pod count)              │
  │ 3. Cluster Autoscaler: drains + removes underutilized node │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on AWS: HPA scales Django API pods, Cluster Autoscaler provisions new EC2 instances when needed. Together they provide full-stack automatic scaling.

K8s Fundamentals Q88 of 200
How does the 12-Factor App methodology apply to anihpj/jobpost on Kubernetes?
A) The 12-Factor App methodology is only applicable to Heroku and cannot be applied to Kubernetes workloads
B) The 12-Factor App is a security standard that defines encryption requirements for applications running in containers
C) The 12-Factor App is a set of network configuration rules that optimize pod-to-pod communication latency
D) Key factors applied to anihpj: I. Codebase (one repo, many deploys via GitOps), III. Config (ConfigMaps/Secrets, not in code), IV. Backing Services (PostgreSQL/Redis as attached resources via Services), VI. Processes (stateless Django pods), VIII. Concurrency (scale horizontally via HPA), X. Dev/Prod Parity (same containers in all environments)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The 12-Factor App methodology is the blueprint for cloud native applications. Kubernetes naturally implements many of the factors: ConfigMaps/Secrets for config, Services for backing services, Deployments for stateless processes, HPA for concurrency scaling. anihpj/jobpost is designed as a 12-Factor app from the ground up.

📝 Detailed Explanation
12-Factor App — anihpj/jobpost on Kubernetes
  ┌────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ FACTOR     │ HOW anihpj/jobpost IMPLEMENTS IT              │
  ├────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ I. Codebase│ One Git repo → deployed to dev/staging/prod  │
  │ III. Config│ DB URL, Redis URL in ConfigMap NOT in image   │
  │ IV. Backing│ PostgreSQL + Redis = attached resources       │
  │    Services│ accessed via Kubernetes Services              │
  │ VI. Process│ Django = stateless pods (any pod, any request)│
  │ VII. Port  │ gunicorn binds to $PORT (8000) — declared    │
  │    Binding │ in Dockerfile EXPOSE                          │
  │ VIII.      │ HPA scales pods horizontally (2-10 replicas) │
  │    Concur- │ not vertically (bigger pods)                  │
  │ X. Dev/Prod│ Same Docker image in dev/staging/prod, only   │
  │    Parity  │ ConfigMap differs between environments        │
  │ XI. Logs   │ All output to stdout → Fluentd sidecar collects│
  └────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────┘

KCNA capstone: The 12-Factor App methodology is the theoretical foundation of cloud native. Q88 ties together everything from Q1-Q87 — containers, pods, deployments, config, services, HPA, and the KCNA mindset. This is the final question of Part 1, Category 1.

2.1 Kubernetes NetworkingQ89-Q100

Container Orchestration Q89 of 200
What are the four Kubernetes Service types and when do you use each?
A) TCP, UDP, HTTP, and HTTPS — chosen based on the application protocol
B) Internal, External, Hybrid, and Global — chosen based on geographic distribution requirements
C) Pod, Node, Cluster, and Cloud — corresponding to the four levels of the Kubernetes networking model
D) ClusterIP (internal-only, default), NodePort (opens port 30000-32767 on every node), LoadBalancer (cloud LB provisions external IP), ExternalName (DNS CNAME alias for external services like RDS). Choose based on access scope: internal → ClusterIP, external dev → NodePort, external prod → LoadBalancer
✅ Correct Answer: D

Services provide stable endpoints for pods that come and go. Each type solves a different access pattern.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Types — Decision Guide
  ┌──────────────┬──────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ TYPE         │ ACCESS   │ USE CASE                             │
  ├──────────────┼──────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ClusterIP    │ Internal │ anihpj-api → anihpj-db (default)     │
  │ NodePort     │ External │ Dev testing: curl node-ip:30080/jobs │
  │ LoadBalancer │ External │ Production: AWS NLB → public DNS     │
  │ ExternalName │ External │ CNAME → AWS RDS (bypass cluster)     │
  └──────────────┴──────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  For anihpj/jobpost:
  • PostgreSQL: ClusterIP (never exposed externally)
  • Django API dev: NodePort (quick test access)
  • Django API prod: LoadBalancer (AWS NLB with TLS)
  • External RDS: ExternalName (rds.amazonaws.com)

KCNA key: Know which Service type for which access pattern. ClusterIP is the default and most common — it's used for all internal service-to-service communication.

Container Orchestration Q90 of 200
How does a ClusterIP Service route traffic to Pods?
A) kube-proxy watches the API server for new Services and creates iptables/IPVS rules on every node. When a pod sends traffic to the ClusterIP, the node's network stack DNATs (rewrites destination) to a random healthy pod IP. The Service itself has no process — it's purely a set of iptables rules
B) The ClusterIP Service runs as a proxy pod that receives all traffic and round-robins it to backend pods
C) ClusterIP uses DNS round-robin — each DNS lookup returns a different pod IP, and the client connects directly
D) ClusterIP is a virtual IP assigned by the CNI plugin that acts as a virtual switch between pods
✅ Correct Answer: A

The ClusterIP is virtual — there's no process, no interface, no pod. kube-proxy programs iptables rules so that any traffic to the ClusterIP is DNAT'd to a backend pod IP. In IPVS mode (more efficient for large clusters), the ClusterIP becomes a virtual server in the Linux kernel's IPVS load balancer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ClusterIP — Virtual IP, iptables Magic
  CLIENT POD (10.244.1.5) wants to reach anihpj-api Service
  │
  │  DNS: anihpj-api → 10.96.100.50 (ClusterIP)
  │
  ▼
  iptables rules (written by kube-proxy):
  -A KUBE-SERVICES -d 10.96.100.50/32 -p tcp --dport 8000 \
     -j KUBE-SVC-XXXX
  -A KUBE-SVC-XXXX -m statistic --mode random --probability 0.33 \
     -j KUBE-SEP-AAAA  →  DNAT to pod 10.244.1.5:8000
  -A KUBE-SVC-XXXX -m statistic --mode random --probability 0.50 \
     -j KUBE-SEP-BBBB  →  DNAT to pod 10.244.2.8:8000
  -A KUBE-SVC-XXXX -j KUBE-SEP-CCCC  →  DNAT to pod 10.244.3.2:8000

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API connects to anihpj-db:5432. DNS resolves this to the ClusterIP, and iptables routes it to the PostgreSQL pod — transparent to the application.

Container Orchestration Q91 of 200
What is a NodePort Service and when would you use it?
A) NodePort is the default Service type; it's automatically created for every pod in the cluster
B) NodePort provides a cloud load balancer with automatic DNS and TLS certificate provisioning
C) NodePort exposes the Service on a static port (30000-32767) on EVERY node's IP. Access: <any-node-ip>:<nodePort>. Simple external access without a cloud LB — good for dev/testing or when you control firewall rules. Not recommended for production (no load balancing across nodes, port range is limited)
D) NodePort is a deprecated Service type replaced by LoadBalancer in Kubernetes v1.25
✅ Correct Answer: C

NodePort opens the same port on every node — hitting any node's IP on that port reaches the Service.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NodePort — Simple External Access
  NodePort 30080 on EVERY node:
  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐
  │  worker-1   │  │  worker-2   │  │  worker-3   │
  │ IP: .1.101  │  │ IP: .1.102  │  │ IP: .1.103  │
  │ :30080→Pod  │  │ :30080→Pod  │  │ :30080→Pod  │
  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘
       ▲               ▲               ▲
       └───────────────┴───────────────┘
         curl 192.168.1.101:30080  ← ANY node works!

  PROS: Simple, no cloud LB needed
  CONS: Port range 30000-32767 only, no automatic LB across nodes
  USE: Dev/testing, not production

For anihpj/jobpost dev testing: kubectl expose deploy anihpj-api --type=NodePort --port=8000 makes the API accessible at <node-ip>:<assigned-port>.

Container Orchestration Q92 of 200
How does a LoadBalancer Service work in cloud environments?
A) The cloud-controller-manager detects the LoadBalancer Service and provisions an external cloud load balancer (AWS NLB, GCP TCP LB, Azure LB). The cloud LB gets a public IP and forwards traffic to NodePorts on all cluster nodes. You get: external IP → cloud LB → NodePort → ClusterIP → Pod
B) LoadBalancer creates a pod that runs HAProxy and distributes traffic to backend pods
C) LoadBalancer is identical to NodePort but uses ports 80 and 443 instead of 30000-32767
D) LoadBalancer requires MetalLB or a similar on-premises solution; it doesn't work with cloud providers
✅ Correct Answer: A

LoadBalancer is the production external access pattern.

📝 Detailed Explanation
LoadBalancer — Cloud-Native External Access
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Internet → Cloud LB (public IP: 54.123.45.67)           │
  │       │                                                   │
  │       ▼                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  Cloud LB → NodePort :30080 on EVERY node         │    │
  │  │  worker-1:30080  worker-2:30080  worker-3:30080  │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │       │                                                   │
  │       ▼                                                   │
  │  ClusterIP: anihpj-api (10.96.x.x:8000) → Pods           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Cloud-specific: AWS → NLB, GCP → TCP LB, Azure → LoadBalancer

For anihpj/jobpost on AWS: creating a LoadBalancer Service provisions an AWS NLB with a public DNS name. Traffic flows: Internet → NLB → NodePort → ClusterIP → Django pod.

Container Orchestration Q93 of 200
How does a Service find which Pods to route traffic to?
A) Services use pod IP addresses hardcoded in their spec; pods must be manually registered with the Service
B) Services use label selectors to find pods. The Endpoints controller watches for pods matching the selector and populates the Endpoints object with their IPs. If the selector doesn't match any pods → Service has no endpoints → 503 errors. This is the #1 cause of "my Service doesn't work"
C) Services broadcast a discovery packet on the pod network; pods respond with their IPs and the Service builds its routing table
D) Services are hardcoded to route to pods created by the same Deployment, using the Deployment's internal pod registry
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Endpoints controller continuously watches for pods matching the Service's selector. When matching pods appear, their IPs are added to the Endpoints object. When they disappear, IPs are removed. kubectl get endpoints shows the current list. If it's empty, your Service is not matching any pods — check your labels!

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Selector → Endpoints — The Critical Link
  Service spec:                    Pod labels:
  selector:                        app: anihpj-api
    app: anihpj-api                tier: backend

         ┌─────────────┐
         │  MATCH! ✅   │
         └──────┬───────┘
                ▼
  Endpoints: anihpj-api
  - 10.244.1.5:8000   ← Pod 1 is healthy
  - 10.244.2.8:8000   ← Pod 2 is healthy
  - 10.244.3.2:8000   ← Pod 3 is healthy

  DEBUGGING: kubectl get endpoints anihpj-api -n anihpj
  If empty → selector mismatch! kubectl get pods --show-labels

KCNA essential: Selector mismatch is the #1 cause of Service failures. Always verify with kubectl get endpoints and compare labels with kubectl get pods --show-labels.

Container Orchestration Q94 of 200
What are Endpoints and why are they essential for debugging Services?
A) Endpoints are API server health check endpoints used for cluster monitoring
B) Endpoints are the external URLs that users access to reach applications running in the cluster
C) Endpoints are the load balancer IPs assigned by cloud providers to LoadBalancer Services
D) Endpoints list the actual pod IP:port pairs a Service routes to. kubectl get endpoints shows which pods are receiving traffic. If empty → Service selector doesn't match any pods → 503 errors. This is the #1 Service debugging command
✅ Correct Answer: D

kubectl get endpoints is the fastest way to verify a Service is working.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Endpoints — The Service-to-Pod Connection
  kubectl get endpoints anihpj-api -n anihpj
  NAME         ENDPOINTS                                      AGE
  anihpj-api   10.244.1.5:8000,10.244.2.8:8000,10.244.3.2:8000   5d
                    ▲                  ▲                  ▲
                    Pod 1              Pod 2              Pod 3

  HEALTHY: Endpoints populated → Service routes to pods ✅
  BROKEN:  Endpoints:      → Service broken! ❌

  WHY EMPTY?
  • Selector doesn't match any pods (check labels!)

For anihpj/jobpost debugging: always start with kubectl get endpoints when a Service returns 503. Empty Endpoints = selector doesn't match = #1 cause of Service failures.

Container Orchestration Q95 of 200
What is a Kubernetes Ingress and what does it provide that a Service alone cannot?
A) Ingress is deprecated in favor of Gateway API; it provided pod-to-pod encryption before mTLS was available
B) Ingress provides load-balanced external access to pods by provisioning a cloud load balancer, same as a LoadBalancer Service
C) Ingress provides L7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing: host-based routing (api.jobpost.com vs web.jobpost.com), path-based routing (/api vs /), TLS termination, and URL rewriting. Services alone only provide L4 (TCP) load balancing — Ingress adds the application-layer intelligence
D) Ingress is a security resource that controls which IP ranges can access Services, similar to a firewall
✅ Correct Answer: C

Ingress is L7 routing for HTTP/HTTPS. Services give you L4 (TCP) load balancing — one port, one backend. Ingress adds: routing by domain name (api.jobpost.com → API Service, jobpost.com → Web Service), routing by URL path (/api → API, /admin → Admin), TLS termination with automatic cert management (cert-manager), and URL rewriting.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress — L7 Routing for anihpj/jobpost
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    INGRESS CONTROLLER                       │
  │                    (nginx-ingress, :443)                    │
  │                                                             │
  │  apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1                          │
  │  kind: Ingress                                             │
  │  spec:                                                     │
  │    tls:                                                    │
  │    - hosts: [api.jobpost.com, jobpost.com]                 │
  │      secretName: anihpj-tls                               │
  │    rules:                                                  │
  │    - host: api.jobpost.com                                 │
  │      http:                                                 │
  │        paths:                                              │
  │        - path: /                                           │
  │          backend: {service: anihpj-api, port: 8000}       │
  │    - host: jobpost.com                                     │
  │      http:                                                 │
  │        paths:                                              │
  │        - path: /                                           │
  │          backend: {service: anihpj-web, port: 80}         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Ingress routes api.jobpost.com to the Django API and jobpost.com to the nginx web frontend — with TLS encryption on both. KCNA key: Ingress = L7 routing, Service = L4 load balancing.

Container Orchestration Q96 of 200
How does Ingress TLS termination work?
A) You create a TLS Secret containing the certificate and private key, reference it in the Ingress spec's tls section with the hostname(s). The Ingress controller terminates TLS at the edge — HTTPS between client and cluster, HTTP between Ingress and backend Service. cert-manager can automate this with Let's Encrypt
B) TLS is handled automatically by Kubernetes; every Ingress gets a self-signed certificate without configuration
C) TLS termination happens at the pod level — each pod must have its own certificate mounted as a Secret volume
D) TLS is configured globally in the kube-apiserver configuration and applies to all Ingress resources
✅ Correct Answer: A

TLS Secrets contain tls.crt and tls.key. The Ingress controller uses these to terminate HTTPS at the edge.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Ingress TLS — HTTPS at the Edge
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Client ──HTTPS──▶ Ingress Controller (TLS termination)  │
  │                         │                                 │
  │  TLS Secret:             │  HTTP (inside cluster)         │
  │  anihpj-tls              ▼                                │
  │  ├── tls.crt            Service: anihpj-api               │
  │  └── tls.key                 │                            │
  │                               ▼                            │
  │                          Pod: Django :8000                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  # Create TLS Secret
  kubectl create secret tls anihpj-tls \
    --cert=server.crt --key=server.key -n anihpj

  # Auto-renew with cert-manager + Let's Encrypt (production)
  # No manual certificate rotation needed!

KCNA tip: cert-manager automates TLS with Let's Encrypt. The Ingress references the Secret by name, and cert-manager keeps it renewed automatically.

Container Orchestration Q97 of 200
What is a NetworkPolicy and why does it require CNI support?
A) NetworkPolicy is a Kubernetes-native firewall that works with any CNI plugin without additional configuration
B) NetworkPolicy controls which users can access the Kubernetes API server based on their network source IP
C) NetworkPolicy is an alternative to Services that provides direct pod-to-pod communication without kube-proxy
D) NetworkPolicy defines firewall rules for pod-to-pod traffic (Ingress/Egress). It requires a CNI that supports it (Calico, Cilium, Weave). If your CNI doesn't implement NetworkPolicy (e.g., Flannel without Calico), the policies are stored in etcd but NEVER enforced — pods can still communicate freely
✅ Correct Answer: D

NetworkPolicy is metadata only — it defines what you WANT, but the CNI plugin must ENFORCE it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NetworkPolicy — Metadata vs Enforcement
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  YOU CREATE: NetworkPolicy (stored in etcd)               │
  │  spec:                                                     │
  │    podSelector: {app: anihpj-db}                          │
  │    ingress:                                                │
  │    - from: [{podSelector: {app: anihpj-api}}]             │
  │    - ports: [{port: 5432}]                                │
  │                                                             │
  │  CNI PLUGIN MUST ENFORCE THIS:                              │
  │  ✅ Calico: translates to iptables/eBPF rules → ENFORCED   │
  │  ✅ Cilium: translates to eBPF programs → ENFORCED          │
  │  ❌ Flannel (default): IGNORES NetworkPolicy → NOT enforced │
  │                                                             │
  │  ALWAYS VERIFY: Create policy → test with busybox → confirm │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: a NetworkPolicy allowing only anihpj-api pods to reach anihpj-db on port 5432 works with Calico/Cilium, but does NOTHING with plain Flannel.

Container Orchestration Q98 of 200
What is a CNI plugin and why must you install one after kubeadm init?
A) CNI is optional — Kubernetes works without it using the built-in host-network mode
B) CNI is a monitoring plugin that collects network metrics from all pods for Prometheus
C) CNI (Container Network Interface) is the plugin system for pod networking. Without it, pods get no IP addresses and CoreDNS stays Pending. After kubeadm init, you must install a CNI (Calico, Cilium, Flannel) to enable pod-to-pod communication. kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr reserves the IP range the CNI will use
D) CNI is automatically installed by kubeadm init; no manual step is needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

After kubeadm init, kubectl get nodes shows the control plane as NotReady because no CNI is installed.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CNI — Why You Must Install It After kubeadm init
  kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr=10.244.0.0/16
  │
  ▼
  kubectl get nodes
  NAME   STATUS     ROLES           AGE   VERSION
  anih   NotReady   control-plane   2m    v1.32.13   ← NotReady!

  kubectl get pods -n kube-system
  coredns-xxx   0/1   Pending   ← Can't start without CNI!

  # INSTALL CNI (choose one):
  kubectl apply -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/flannel-io/flannel/.../kube-flannel.yml
  # OR
  kubectl apply -f https://docs.projectcalico.org/manifests/calico.yaml

  # After CNI install:
  kubectl get nodes
  NAME   STATUS   ROLES           AGE   VERSION
  anih   Ready    control-plane   5m    v1.32.13   ← Ready!

On your kubeadm cluster, a CNI (Flannel or Calico) was installed right after init. Without it, pods get no IPs and CoreDNS stays Pending — the cluster is effectively unusable.

Container Orchestration Q99 of 200
What is CoreDNS and how does Kubernetes DNS work?
A) CoreDNS is the default cluster DNS server (CNCF graduated). It runs as a Deployment and resolves Service names to ClusterIPs automatically: anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local → 10.96.x.x. It also handles external DNS forwarding and supports custom DNS configuration via the Corefile ConfigMap
B) CoreDNS is a monitoring tool that collects DNS query metrics from all pods and exports them to Prometheus
C) CoreDNS is a Kubernetes controller that automatically registers pods with external DNS providers like Route53
D) CoreDNS is a network plugin that replaces kube-proxy for Service-to-pod routing using DNS-based load balancing
✅ Correct Answer: A

CoreDNS provides automatic DNS for Services. Every Service gets a DNS record automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CoreDNS — Cluster DNS Architecture
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: anihpj-api                                          │
  │  nslookup anihpj-db                                       │
  │       │                                                    │
  │       ▼                                                    │
  │  /etc/resolv.conf:                                        │
  │  nameserver 10.96.0.10  ← CoreDNS Service ClusterIP       │
  │  search anihpj.svc.cluster.local                          │
  │       │                                                    │
  │       ▼                                                    │
  │  COREDNS (Deployment, 2 replicas)                          │
  │  • Resolves K8s Services: svc.ns.svc.cluster.local        │
  │  • Forwards external queries to upstream DNS (/etc/resolv) │
  │  • Configurable via Corefile ConfigMap                     │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: when Django connects to anihpj-db, CoreDNS resolves it to the PostgreSQL Service's ClusterIP — fully automatic, no configuration needed.

Container Orchestration Q100 of 200
What is the difference between kube-proxy's iptables and IPVS modes?
A) iptables mode is for Linux, IPVS mode is for Windows nodes
B) iptables mode uses sequential rule chains (O(n) lookup, slower with many services). IPVS mode uses a kernel hash table (O(1) lookup, faster at scale). IPVS also supports multiple load-balancing algorithms (rr, lc, sh, dh). For clusters with 1000+ services, IPVS is significantly faster
C) There is no difference — they are two names for the same kernel subsystem
D) iptables mode handles L4 (TCP/UDP), IPVS mode handles L7 (HTTP) load balancing
✅ Correct Answer: B

iptables = sequential rules, IPVS = hash table.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kube-proxy — iptables vs IPVS
  ┌──────────────┬─────────────────────┬────────────────────────┐
  │ MODE         │ LOOKUP              │ PERFORMANCE             │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ iptables     │ O(n) sequential     │ Slower with many Svc    │
  │              │ Traverses rules     │ (1000+ services = lag)  │
  ├──────────────┼─────────────────────┼────────────────────────┤
  │ IPVS          │ O(1) hash table     │ Fast regardless of     │
  │              │ Direct lookup        │ service count          │
  └──────────────┴─────────────────────┴────────────────────────┘

  IPVS SCHEDULERS: rr (round-robin), lc (least connection),
                   sh (source hash), dh (destination hash)

  On your kubeadm cluster: kube-proxy runs in iptables mode by default

For clusters with 1000+ services, IPVS mode is significantly faster. For anihpj/jobpost (small cluster), iptables is fine. KCNA key: know that IPVS = hash table = O(1), better for large clusters.

🔐 Section 2.2: Kubernetes Security FundamentalsQ101-Q110

🔵 Authentication, RBAC, Pod Security, TLS & Secrets

Domain: Container Orchestration (28%) — the 3 A's of Kubernetes security (Authentication, Authorization, Admission Control) and how to secure your cluster and workloads.

Container Orchestration Q101 of 200
What are the "Three A's" of Kubernetes security and what does each one control?
A) Applications, APIs, Admission — they control what apps can run, which APIs are exposed, and who gets admitted
B) AuthConfig, AuthPolicy, AuthWebhook — three CRDs that define security policies
C) Authorization, Automation, Auditing — they control access, orchestration, and logging
D) Authentication (who are you?), Authorization (what can you do?), Admission Control (should this request be allowed?) — three sequential gates every API request passes through
✅ Correct Answer: D

Every API request to the Kubernetes API server passes through three sequential security gates: Authentication (verifying identity via certificates, bearer tokens, OIDC, or webhook), Authorization (checking if the authenticated identity has permission via RBAC, ABAC, Node, or Webhook modes), and Admission Control (mutating or validating the request via built-in admission controllers like NamespaceLifecycle, LimitRanger, and external webhooks like OPA/Gatekeeper).

📝 Detailed Explanation
The 3 A's — Sequential Security Gates for Every API Request
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    KUBERNETES API SERVER                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl ───▶  ┌──────────────────┐                             │
  │                │ 1. AUTHENTICATION │  "WHO are you?"            │
  │                │ ┌──────────────┐  │                             │
  │                │ │ X.509 Certs  │  │  kubeconfig certificate    │
  │                │ │ Bearer Token │  │  ServiceAccount JWT token  │
  │                │ │ OIDC (SSO)   │  │  Google/GitHub/Azure AD    │
  │                │ │ Webhook      │  │  External auth service     │
  │                │ └──────────────┘  │                             │
  │                └────────┬─────────┘                             │
  │                         │ ✅ Authenticated                       │
  │                         ▼                                        │
  │                ┌──────────────────┐                             │
  │                │ 2. AUTHORIZATION  │  "WHAT can you do?"         │
  │                │ ┌──────────────┐  │                             │
  │                │ │ RBAC (Role/   │  │  Most common mode          │
  │                │ │  ClusterRole) │  │  "Can user X list pods?"   │
  │                │ │ Node          │  │  kubelet authorization     │
  │                │ │ ABAC          │  │  Attribute-based (legacy)  │
  │                │ │ Webhook       │  │  External authz service    │
  │                │ └──────────────┘  │                             │
  │                └────────┬─────────┘                             │
  │                         │ ✅ Authorized                          │
  │                         ▼                                        │
  │                ┌──────────────────┐                             │
  │                │ 3. ADMISSION      │  "SHOULD this be allowed?"  │
  │                │ ┌──────────────┐  │                             │
  │                │ │ Mutating     │  │  Inject sidecars, default   │
  │                │ │ Webhooks     │  │  storage classes, labels    │
  │                │ ├──────────────┤  │                             │
  │                │ │ Validating   │  │  Enforce policies: no       │
  │                │ │ Webhooks     │  │  privileged pods, PSS check │
  │                │ └──────────────┘  │                             │
  │                └────────┬─────────┘                             │
  │                         │ ✅ Admitted                            │
  │                         ▼                                        │
  │                   ┌──────────┐                                  │
  │                   │  ETCD    │  Object persisted                 │
  │                   └──────────┘                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  MEMORY AID: AuthN = "Who?" → AuthZ = "What?" → Admission = "Should?"
              Identity   → Permission  → Policy Check

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django pod uses a ServiceAccount with a token (Authentication), bound to a Role via RoleBinding (Authorization) that grants only the permissions it needs (e.g., read ConfigMaps, not delete pods). Admission controllers ensure no privileged containers sneak in. KCNA key: know the order — AuthN first, AuthZ second, Admission third.

Container Orchestration Q102 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between a Role and a ClusterRole in Kubernetes RBAC?
A) Role is namespace-scoped (applies within one namespace), ClusterRole is cluster-scoped (applies across all namespaces or to non-namespaced resources like nodes)
B) Role is for users, ClusterRole is for service accounts only
C) There is no difference — ClusterRole is just a Role with a different YAML kind field
D) Role is for pods, ClusterRole is for the control plane components
✅ Correct Answer: A

Role is namespace-scoped — it only grants permissions to resources within a specific namespace (e.g., "can get pods in the jobpost namespace"). ClusterRole is cluster-scoped — it can grant permissions to namespace-scoped resources across ALL namespaces, plus non-namespaced cluster resources like nodes, persistentvolumes, namespaces, and clusterroles themselves.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Role vs ClusterRole — Namespace Scope vs Cluster Scope
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        CLUSTER                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │              ClusterRole: "node-reader"                   │    │
  │  │  resources: ["nodes", "persistentvolumes"]              │    │
  │  │  verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                        │    │
  │  │  SCOPE: Entire cluster (no namespace needed!)           │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── ns: jobpost ─────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  Role: "pod-reader"                                      │    │
  │  │  resources: ["pods"]                                     │    │
  │  │  verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]                         │    │
  │  │  SCOPE: ONLY namespace "jobpost"                         │    │
  │  │                                                          │    │
  │  │  ✅ Can read pods in jobpost namespace                   │    │
  │  │  ❌ Cannot read pods in other namespaces                 │    │
  │  │  ❌ Cannot read nodes (needs ClusterRole)                │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── ns: staging ──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Role: "pod-reader"  ← same name, DIFFERENT namespace    │   │
  │  │  SCOPE: ONLY namespace "staging"                         │   │
  │  │  (Roles are namespace-isolated — they don't collide!)    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  KEY RULE: Use ClusterRole for cluster-wide resources (nodes, PVs, NS)
            or when you need the SAME permissions across ALL namespaces.
            Use Role for namespace-specific permissions (pods in 'jobpost').

In the anihpj/jobpost setup, a Role is used to grant the Django service account permissions to read ConfigMaps and Secrets only in the jobpost namespace — it doesn't need cluster-wide access. A ClusterRole would be used for a monitoring agent that needs to read pod metrics across all namespaces.

Container Orchestration Q103 of 200
You have a ClusterRole named "view-pods" that allows listing pods cluster-wide. You want to grant this permission to user "alice" ONLY in the "dev" namespace. Which binding should you use?
A) ClusterRoleBinding — because the ClusterRole is cluster-scoped, you must use a ClusterRoleBinding
B) RoleBinding referencing a Role — create a namespace-scoped Role that duplicates the ClusterRole permissions
C) RoleBinding referencing the ClusterRole — a RoleBinding can reference a ClusterRole but limits its scope to the RoleBinding's namespace
D) This is not possible — ClusterRole permissions always apply cluster-wide regardless of the binding type
✅ Correct Answer: C

This is a common KCNA trick question! A RoleBinding CAN reference a ClusterRole. When you create a RoleBinding (namespace-scoped) that references a ClusterRole, the permissions from the ClusterRole are scoped down to that namespace only. This is the "reuse ClusterRole, limit to namespace" pattern — you don't need to duplicate the ClusterRole as a Role.

📝 Detailed Explanation
RoleBinding + ClusterRole = Namespace-Scoped Permissions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ClusterRole: "view-pods"                                        │
  │  rules: [{apiGroups: [""], resources: ["pods"], verbs: ["list"]}]│
  │  SCOPE: Cluster-wide by default                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              │
                ┌─────────────┼─────────────┐
                │             │             │
                ▼             ▼             ▼
  ┌───────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────┐
  │ RoleBinding       │ │ ClusterRoleBinding│ │ RoleBinding       │
  │ ns: dev           │ │ (cluster-wide)    │ │ ns: staging       │
  │ subjects: [alice] │ │ subjects: [bob]   │ │ subjects: [carol] │
  │ roleRef:          │ │ roleRef:          │ │ roleRef:          │
  │   kind: ClusterRole│ │   kind: ClusterRole│ │   kind: ClusterRole│
  │   name: view-pods │ │   name: view-pods │ │   name: view-pods │
  │                   │ │                   │ │                   │
  │ EFFECT: alice can │ │ EFFECT: bob can   │ │ EFFECT: carol can │
  │ list pods ONLY in │ │ list pods in ALL  │ │ list pods ONLY in │
  │ the "dev" NS ✅   │ │ namespaces ✅     │ │ the "staging" NS ✅│
  └───────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘

  PATTERN: "Define Once (ClusterRole), Bind Many Times (RoleBinding
           or ClusterRoleBinding), Scope Appropriately"

For anihpj/jobpost, the CI/CD pipeline might have a ClusterRole "deployer" with pod update permissions, but each developer only gets a RoleBinding scoped to their own namespace (e.g., jobpost-dev), not cluster-wide. This is the principle of least privilege in action.

Container Orchestration Q104 of 200
What is a ServiceAccount in Kubernetes and what happens to pods that don't specify one?
A) A ServiceAccount is a special type of user account for the cloud provider's IAM — pods without one cannot run
B) A ServiceAccount provides a pod-level identity for API server authentication — every pod NOT specifying a SA gets the "default" ServiceAccount in its namespace
C) A ServiceAccount is only used by Ingress controllers — pods don't use them directly
D) A ServiceAccount is a CRD that manages external service credentials like AWS IAM roles
✅ Correct Answer: B

ServiceAccounts are Kubernetes-native identities for pods (not humans). Every namespace has a "default" ServiceAccount automatically created. When a pod is created without specifying serviceAccountName, it uses the "default" SA. The SA's JWT token is automatically mounted into each pod at /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token, allowing the pod's processes to authenticate to the API server.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ServiceAccount — Pod Identity in Kubernetes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  NAMESPACE: jobpost                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  ServiceAccount: "default" (auto-created)                │    │
  │  │  ServiceAccount: "django-api" (custom SA)                │    │
  │  │    └─ annotations: {                                     │    │
  │  │         "eks.amazonaws.com/role-arn": "arn:aws:iam::..."  │    │
  │  │       }                                                  │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐                    │
  │  │ Pod: django-app │     │ Pod: worker     │                    │
  │  │ serviceAccount- │     │ (no SA specified)│                    │
  │  │  Name: django-  │     │ → uses "default"│                    │
  │  │  api            │     │   SA            │                    │
  │  │                 │     │                 │                    │
  │  │ Token mounted at│     │ Token mounted at│                    │
  │  │ /var/run/.../   │     │ /var/run/.../   │                    │
  │  │ token           │     │ token           │                    │
  │  └─────────────────┘     └─────────────────┘                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Each SA token is a JWT signed by the kube-controller-manager    │
  │  with a projected volume (non-expiring by default in 1.24+       │
  │  with TokenRequest API — rotated automatically).                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API pod uses a custom ServiceAccount "django-api" bound to a Role that grants read access to ConfigMaps and Secrets. This follows the principle of least privilege — the "default" SA should have zero RBAC permissions in production.

Container Orchestration Q105 of 200
Your pod runs a static web server that never needs to talk to the Kubernetes API. What should you set to improve security?
A) automountServiceAccountToken: false — prevents the SA token from being mounted into the pod's filesystem
B) serviceAccountName: "" — sets an empty service account
C) disableRBAC: true — disables RBAC for this pod only
D) tokenExpiration: 0 — makes the token never expire, improving reliability
✅ Correct Answer: A

Setting automountServiceAccountToken: false in the pod spec (or on the ServiceAccount itself) prevents Kubernetes from mounting the SA token into the pod. If an attacker compromises the container, they cannot use the mounted token to call the Kubernetes API. This is a critical defense-in-depth measure — most web servers, workers, and stateless apps don't need API access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
automountServiceAccountToken — Defense in Depth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT automountServiceAccountToken: false                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Pod: nginx                                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/          │    │
  │  │  ├── token       ← JWT with API access 🔴 SECURITY RISK │    │
  │  │  ├── ca.crt      ← Cluster CA certificate               │    │
  │  │  └── namespace   ← Namespace name                       │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │  Attacker who gains shell → curl -k https://kubernetes/api/v1/   │
  │  pods --header "Authorization: Bearer $(cat token)" → LEAK!     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITH automountServiceAccountToken: false                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Pod: nginx                                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/          │    │
  │  │  (DIRECTORY DOES NOT EXIST!) 🟢 SAFE                    │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │  Attacker who gains shell → No token → Cannot access API → SAFE │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Pod spec:
    automountServiceAccountToken: false   ← KCNA EXAM: set this
    containers:                             for ANY pod that doesn't
    - name: nginx                           need to talk to the API

For anihpj/jobpost, the Nginx frontend pod serving static files should have automountServiceAccountToken: false because it never calls the API. The Django API pod does need a token (to read ConfigMaps), so it keeps the mount. KCNA exam tip: if a pod doesn't need API access, disable the token mount.

Container Orchestration Q106 of 200
What are the three Pod Security Standards (PSS) levels in Kubernetes and what does each allow?
A) Low, Medium, High — they define the sensitivity level of data a pod can access
B) Development, Staging, Production — they map to deployment environments
C) Restricted, Limited, Open — they control network egress policies
D) Privileged (unrestricted, like root containers), Baseline (prevents known privilege escalations, minimally restrictive), Restricted (heavily restricted, follows pod hardening best practices like non-root, read-only root FS, seccomp)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The Pod Security Standards (PSS) define three policy levels that control pod security posture. Privileged is completely unrestricted (root containers, hostNetwork, hostPID, privileged mode — use for system pods like CNI or kube-proxy). Baseline prevents known privilege escalations (no hostPath volumes, no privileged containers, no hostNetwork) — it's the minimum bar for most workloads. Restricted enforces pod hardening best practices (must run as non-root, read-only root filesystem, seccomp profile, drop ALL capabilities).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Security Standards — Three Escalating Levels
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     POD SECURITY STANDARDS                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  PRIVILEGED (least restrictive)                           │   │
  │  │  ✅ Root containers allowed                               │   │
  │  │  ✅ Privileged: true                                      │   │
  │  │  ✅ hostNetwork, hostPID, hostIPC                         │   │
  │  │  ✅ ALL Linux capabilities                                │   │
  │  │  USE: system pods (kube-proxy, CNI, CSI drivers)          │   │
  │  │  🟢 KCNA: "Totally unrestricted — rarely appropriate"     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  BASELINE (moderate — minimum bar)                        │   │
  │  │  ❌ hostProcess, hostNetwork, hostPID, hostIPC            │   │
  │  │  ❌ privileged containers                                 │   │
  │  │  ❌ hostPath volumes (except specific paths)              │   │
  │  │  ❌ CAP_SYS_ADMIN, CAP_NET_RAW                            │   │
  │  │  USE: most application workloads                          │   │
  │  │  🟡 KCNA: "Prevents known privilege escalations"         │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                          │                                       │
  │                          ▼                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  RESTRICTED (most restrictive — best practice)            │   │
  │  │  ❌ ALL capabilities dropped (except NET_BIND_SERVICE)    │   │
  │  │  ❌ Must run as non-root (runAsNonRoot: true)             │   │
  │  │  ❌ Read-only root filesystem (readOnlyRootFilesystem)    │   │
  │  │  ❌ Seccomp profile required (RuntimeDefault or localhost)│   │
  │  │  ❌ Privileged escalation disabled (allowPrivilegeEsc..)  │   │
  │  │  USE: production workloads with strong security needs     │   │
  │  │  🔴 KCNA: "Hardening best practices — most secure"        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API and Redis pods run at the Restricted level — non-root, read-only root FS, seccomp enforced. The PostgreSQL StatefulSet runs at Baseline because it needs a writable volume. KCNA key: know the three levels and what each blocks.

Container Orchestration Q107 of 200
You want to enforce the Restricted Pod Security Standard on the "production" namespace but only generate warnings (not block) for the "staging" namespace. Which admission controller and modes should you use?
A) OPA Gatekeeper with a ConstraintTemplate — PSS cannot be enforced natively in Kubernetes
