B) Pod Security Admission (PSA) controller with labels: pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted on production, pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted on staging
C) RBAC with a ClusterRole — denying pod creation based on security context
D) NetworkPolicy with egress rules — blocking pods that violate security standards
✅ Correct Answer: B

Pod Security Admission (PSA) is a built-in admission controller (GA since Kubernetes 1.25) that enforces Pod Security Standards at the namespace level using labels. Three modes are available: enforce (reject non-compliant pods), audit (allow but log an audit event), and warn (allow but show a warning to the user). You apply them by labeling namespaces with pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce, pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit, and pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Security Admission — Namespace-Labeled Enforcement
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PSA: BUILT-IN admission controller (replaces PodSecurityPolicy) │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── namespace: production ────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  labels:                                                  │   │
  │  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted        │   │
  │  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: v1.32     │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  kubectl run bad-pod --image=nginx  →  ❌ REJECTED        │   │
  │  │  "violates PodSecurity 'restricted:latest': runAsNonRoot" │   │
  │  │  (pod doesn't set securityContext.runAsNonRoot: true)     │   │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─── namespace: staging ───────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  labels:                                                  │   │
  │  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted           │   │
  │  │    pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn-version: v1.32        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  kubectl run bad-pod --image=nginx  →  ✅ CREATED         │   │
  │  │  WARNING: "would violate PodSecurity 'restricted:latest'" │   │
  │  │  (pod runs, but user sees a warning)                      │   │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  THREE PSA MODES:                                                │
  │    enforce → Reject (block)    🔴 Hard block                    │
  │    audit   → Allow + log       🟡 Observability                 │
  │    warn    → Allow + message   🟠 User feedback                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the production namespace uses enforce: restricted to block any pod that runs as root or without seccomp. The staging namespace uses warn: restricted so developers see warnings during testing without breaking their workflow. KCNA exam tip: PSA replaced PodSecurityPolicy (PSP) in 1.25 — PSP is DEPRECATED.

Container Orchestration Q108 of 200
Which Kubernetes components use TLS certificates for communication, and what happens when a certificate expires?
A) API server, kubelet, etcd, scheduler, controller-manager all use TLS — expired certs on kubelet or etcd cause nodes to go NotReady or the cluster to become unavailable
B) Only the API server uses TLS — other components communicate over plain HTTP internally
C) TLS is optional — Kubernetes can run without any certificates for development clusters
D) Only etcd uses TLS — the rest of the control plane uses shared secrets instead
✅ Correct Answer: A

Virtually ALL Kubernetes control plane communications use mutual TLS (mTLS). The API server has a serving certificate (clients verify the API server), kubelet has a serving certificate (API server verifies the kubelet), etcd uses peer and client certificates, and each component (scheduler, controller-manager) uses a client certificate to authenticate to the API server. Expired certificates are a common production outage cause — an expired kubelet cert makes nodes go NotReady.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TLS in Kubernetes — Every Component Uses Certificates
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    TLS CERTIFICATE MAP                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │         ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐                 │
  │         │         API SERVER (port 6443)       │                 │
  │         │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐    │                 │
  │         │  │ Serving cert: apiserver.crt │    │  ← Clients verify │
  │         │  │ CA: ca.crt                  │    │    this           │
  │         │  └─────────────────────────────┘    │                 │
  │         └──────────┬──────────────────────────┘                 │
  │                    │ mTLS (both sides verify)                     │
  │     ┌──────────────┼──────────────┬──────────────┐              │
  │     │              │              │              │              │
  │     ▼              ▼              ▼              ▼              │
  │  ┌────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐         │
  │  │ KUBELET│  │SCHEDULER │  │CONTROLLER│  │   ETCD   │         │
  │  │        │  │          │  │ MANAGER  │  │          │         │
  │  │Serving │  │Client    │  │Client    │  │Peer cert │         │
  │  │cert    │  │cert      │  │cert      │  │Client    │         │
  │  │(kubelet│  │(scheduler│  │(controller│  │cert      │         │
  │  │ .crt)  │  │.crt)     │  │-manager  │  │(etcd.crt)│         │
  │  │        │  │          │  │.crt)     │  │          │         │
  │  └────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CERTIFICATE LIFECYCLE (kubeadm):                                │
  │    Default validity: 1 year                                      │
  │    Renewal: kubeadm certs renew all                              │
  │    Check: kubeadm certs check-expiration                         │
  │    Auto-renewal on control plane upgrade (kubeadm 1.22+)         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On the anih kubeadm cluster (v1.32.13), you can check cert expiration with kubeadm certs check-expiration. If the kubelet cert expires, the node becomes NotReady because the API server can't verify the kubelet's identity. KCNA key: know that ALL control plane components use TLS, and cert expiry = outage.

Container Orchestration Q109 of 200
Secrets in etcd are stored base64-encoded by default. How do you enable encryption of Secret data at rest in etcd?
A) Set secret.encryption: true in the kubelet configuration — this encrypts secrets automatically
B) Use sealed-secrets or external-secrets-operator — Kubernetes has no built-in encryption for etcd
C) Create an EncryptionConfiguration manifest and pass --encryption-provider-config to the API server — this enables envelope encryption using providers like aesgcm, aescbc, or secretbox
D) Base64 encoding IS encryption — no additional configuration is needed for production security
✅ Correct Answer: C

By default, Secrets in etcd are only base64-encoded — NOT encrypted. Anyone with etcd access can decode them. To encrypt Secrets at rest, you create an EncryptionConfiguration YAML file specifying a provider (like aescbc, secretbox, or aesgcm) and pass it to the API server via --encryption-provider-config. The API server then encrypts data before writing to etcd and decrypts it when reading — this is called envelope encryption.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secret Encryption at Rest — Envelope Encryption Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT EncryptionConfiguration (DEFAULT — INSECURE)            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl create secret generic db-pass --from-literal=pw=hunter2 │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  API Server → etcd: "cGFzc3dvcmQ6IGh1bnRlcjI="  ← base64 only!  │
  │  $ echo "cGFzc3dvcmQ6IGh1bnRlcjI=" | base64 -d → "password: h2" │
  │  🔴 Anyone with etcdctl access can READ ALL SECRETS              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITH EncryptionConfiguration (PRODUCTION REQUIREMENT)            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  EncryptionConfiguration.yaml:                                   │
  │    resources:                                                    │
  │    - resources: [secrets]                                        │
  │      providers:                                                  │
  │      - aescbc:            ← First provider = used for WRITE      │
  │          keys:                                                    │
  │          - name: key1                                            │
  │            secret:                   │
  │      - identity: {}       ← Empty = plain text (for migration)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl create secret → API Server encrypts with AES-CBC → etcd │
  │  kubectl get secret    → API Server decrypts from etcd → user    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  etcd stores: k8s:enc:aescbc:v1:key1:  🟢 ENCRYPTED │
  │  (even if attacker has etcd access, data is unreadable!)         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost in production, the EncryptionConfiguration uses aescbc to encrypt database passwords, API keys, and TLS secrets stored in etcd. The anih kubeadm cluster should have this configured — check with grep encryption-provider-config /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml. KCNA exam: base64 is ENCODING, not ENCRYPTION — anyone can decode it.

Container Orchestration Q110 of 200
Your Django API container image is stored in a private registry (AWS ECR). How do you allow Kubernetes to pull this image?
A) Set DOCKER_USERNAME and DOCKER_PASSWORD environment variables on the pod — Kubernetes reads these automatically
B) Create a Secret of type docker-registry (or kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson) with registry credentials, then reference imagePullSecrets in the pod spec
C) Add the registry URL to the AllowedRegistries field in the kubelet configuration
D) Private registries require a Container Registry Proxy to be installed as a DaemonSet on every node
✅ Correct Answer: B

To pull images from a private registry, you create a Secret of type kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson (or the older kubernetes.io/dockercfg) containing the registry URL, username, and password/token. Then reference it in the pod spec via imagePullSecrets. Kubernetes uses these credentials to authenticate with the registry when pulling the image. You can also attach the secret to a ServiceAccount so all pods using that SA automatically get the pull secret.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ImagePullSecrets — Authenticating to Private Registries
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STEP 1: Create the registry credential secret                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl create secret docker-registry ecr-creds \               │
  │    --docker-server=123456.dkr.ecr.us-east-1.amazonaws.com \      │
  │    --docker-username=AWS \                                       │
  │    --docker-password=$(aws ecr get-login-password) \             │
  │    -n jobpost                                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  Secret: ecr-creds (type: kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson)│    │
  │  │  .dockerconfigjson: {                                    │    │
  │  │    "auths": {                                            │    │
  │  │      "123456.dkr.ecr...": {                              │    │
  │  │        "username": "AWS",                                │    │
  │  │        "password": "eyJ...token...",                     │    │
  │  │        "auth": "base64(AWS:token)"                       │    │
  │  │      }                                                   │    │
  │  │    }                                                     │    │
  │  │  }                                                       │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 2: Reference in Pod spec                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    imagePullSecrets:                                             │
  │    - name: ecr-creds           ← kubelet uses this to pull image │
  │    containers:                                                   │
  │    - name: django-api                                          │
  │      image: 123456.dkr.ecr.../anihpj:latest  ← PRIVATE image    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ALTERNATIVE: Attach to ServiceAccount (auto-adds to all pods)   │
  │  kubectl patch serviceaccount default \                          │
  │    -p '{"imagePullSecrets": [{"name": "ecr-creds"}]}'            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django API image is pushed to AWS ECR (a private registry). The CI/CD pipeline creates/refreshes the ecr-creds secret (ECR tokens expire every 12 hours!), and the pod references it via imagePullSecrets. On the anih kubeadm cluster, images from Docker Hub don't need this — only private registries do. KCNA key: imagePullSecrets = the bridge between your private registry and kubelet's image pull.

🔍 Section 2.3: Troubleshooting & DebuggingQ111-Q122

🟠 Node Conditions, Pod Lifecycle, Probes, Logs & Events

Domain: Container Orchestration (28%) — how to diagnose "why isn't my pod working?" from node-level down to container-level.

Container Orchestration Q111 of 200
What are the five main Node conditions in Kubernetes and what does Node "Ready" actually mean?
A) Ready (node can accept pods), MemoryPressure (low memory), DiskPressure (low disk), PIDPressure (too many PIDs), NetworkUnavailable (CNI not configured) — Ready=True means kubelet is healthy and node can schedule pods
B) Running, Stopped, Pending, Failed, Unknown — same as pod phases applied to nodes
C) CPUReady, MemoryReady, DiskReady, NetworkReady, PodReady — one condition per resource type
D) Up, Down, Degraded, Maintenance, Unknown — based on node lifecycle states
✅ Correct Answer: A

Node conditions are reported by the kubelet (via the node controller) and describe the node's health. The five standard conditions are: Ready (can accept new pods — True/False/Unknown), MemoryPressure (node memory is low — True means eviction may occur), DiskPressure (node disk is low — True means image garbage collection), PIDPressure (too many process IDs running), and NetworkUnavailable (CNI plugin hasn't been set up yet). Ready is the master switch — if Ready=False, no new pods will be scheduled.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Node Conditions — The Five Health Signals
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl describe node anih | grep -A1 "Conditions:"             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Ready                True    (kubelet is posting status) │   │
  │  │  MemoryPressure       False   (enough memory available)   │   │
  │  │  DiskPressure         False   (enough disk space)         │   │
  │  │  PIDPressure          False   (PID count is healthy)      │   │
  │  │  NetworkUnavailable   False   (CNI IS configured)         │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  EVICTION THRESHOLDS (kubelet flags):                            │
  │    --eviction-hard=memory.available<100Mi                        │
  │    --eviction-hard=nodefs.available<10%                          │
  │    --eviction-hard=imagefs.available<15%                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WHEN MemoryPressure=True:                                       │
  │    1. Node tainted: node.kubernetes.io/memory-pressure          │
  │    2. Pods evicted (least QoS → BestEffort first)                │
  │    3. New pods NOT scheduled on this node                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On the anih kubeadm node, kubectl describe node anih shows all five conditions. If MemoryPressure becomes True (common on single-node labs running anihpj/jobpost + PostgreSQL + Redis simultaneously), pods will start getting evicted. KCNA key: Ready=False = NO new pods scheduled. MemoryPressure/DiskPressure True = pods may be evicted.

Container Orchestration Q112 of 200
A node suddenly shows Ready=Unknown. You run `kubectl describe node mynode`. Where should you look first for the root cause?
A) The node's YAML definition — check if someone edited it incorrectly
B) The kube-proxy logs — node status is reported by kube-proxy, not kubelet
C) The Conditions section and Events section at the bottom — Conditions show the current state, Events show the timeline of what happened (e.g., "Node status was updated: NodeReady condition changed to Unknown")
D) The Pods section — if all pods are running, the node is fine regardless of Ready status
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl describe node is the go-to diagnostic command. The Conditions section tells you the current health status. The Events section at the bottom provides the timeline — it shows when conditions changed and often includes error messages. Ready=Unknown typically means the kubelet stopped reporting (node unresponsive, kubelet crashed, or network partition) — the node controller marks it Unknown after the node-monitor-grace-period (default 40s).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl describe node — Anatomy of a Diagnostic Output
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl describe node anih                                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  SECTION 1: Info                                         │   │
  │  │  Name: anih, Roles: control-plane, Version: v1.32.13     │   │
  │  │  OS: Ubuntu 22.04, Kernel: 5.15.0-...                    │   │
  │  │  Container Runtime: containerd://1.7.24                  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  SECTION 2: Conditions  ← CHECK THIS FIRST               │   │
  │  │  Ready              True    Thu, 06 Jun 2026 14:23:01    │   │
  │  │  MemoryPressure     False   Thu, 06 Jun 2026 14:23:01    │   │
  │  │  DiskPressure       False   Thu, 06 Jun 2026 14:23:01    │   │
  │  │  PIDPressure        False   Thu, 06 Jun 2026 14:23:01    │   │
  │  │  NetworkUnavailable False   Thu, 06 Jun 2026 14:23:01    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  SECTION 3: Capacity & Allocatable                       │   │
  │  │  CPU: 4, Memory: 16Gi, Pods: 110                         │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  SECTION 4: Events  ← READ THIS FOR ROOT CAUSE           │   │
  │  │  54m  Normal   NodeReady      Node anih status is now    │   │
  │  │                                NodeReady                 │   │
  │  │  10s  Warning  NodeNotReady   Node status changed to     │   │
  │  │                                NotReady: kubelet stopped │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For the anih node, if Ready goes Unknown, check systemctl status kubelet on the node itself. On a single-node kubeadm cluster, this means ALL workloads become inaccessible. KCNA exam: describe node → Conditions + Events = root cause.

Container Orchestration Q113 of 200
A pod is stuck in CrashLoopBackOff. What does this status mean and what are the three most common causes?
A) The node ran out of resources — CrashLoopBackOff means Kubernetes couldn't schedule the pod
B) The image is too large — CrashLoopBackOff occurs during long image pulls that time out
C) The pod was evicted by the scheduler due to resource pressure
D) The container starts, crashes, and Kubernetes keeps restarting it with exponential backoff (10s, 20s, 40s, up to 5min) — common causes: application bug (exit code 1), OOMKilled (exit code 137), or misconfiguration (wrong env vars, missing files)
✅ Correct Answer: D

CrashLoopBackOff means the container in the pod starts, immediately crashes, gets restarted by the kubelet, crashes again — and Kubernetes applies an exponential backoff delay between restart attempts: 10 seconds → 20 seconds → 40 seconds → 80 seconds, capping at 5 minutes. The three most common causes are: (1) application exits with non-zero code (bug, missing dependency), (2) OOMKilled — container exceeds memory limit, (3) misconfiguration — wrong command, missing file, bad environment variable.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod Lifecycle — CrashLoopBackOff Diagnostic Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  POD LIFECYCLE STATES & TRANSITIONS                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Pending → Running → Succeeded (exit 0, restartPolicy=Never)     │
  │                   → Failed (exit non-zero, restartPolicy=Never)  │
  │                   → CrashLoopBackOff (exit non-zero + restart)   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl get pods                                                │
  │  NAME         READY   STATUS             RESTARTS   AGE          │
  │  django-app   0/1     CrashLoopBackOff   24         12m          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DIAGNOSTIC COMMANDS:                                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 1. kubectl describe pod django-app                       │   │
  │  │    → Look at "Last State" → "Reason: Error" or "OOMKilled│   │
  │  │    → Look at "Events" section for clues                  │   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ 2. kubectl logs django-app --previous                    │   │
  │  │    → Shows logs from the CRASHED container               │   │
  │  │    → Key flag: --previous (CRITICAL for CrashLoopBackOff)│   │
  │  │                                                          │   │
  │  │ 3. kubectl get events --field-selector involvedObject.   │   │
  │  │    name=django-app --sort-by='.lastTimestamp'            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  EXIT CODES:                                                     │
  │    0   = Success (normal exit)                                   │
  │    1   = Application error (bug, panic, missing file)           │
  │    137 = SIGKILL (OOMKilled or external kill) — 128+9=137       │
  │    143 = SIGTERM (graceful shutdown) — 128+15=143               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django container crashes with CrashLoopBackOff, check kubectl logs django-app --previous — it might show "could not connect to database" (PostgreSQL not ready yet), a Python import error (missing dependency in the image), or OOMKilled (memory limit too low for Django+Gunicorn). KCNA key: --previous is the magic flag for CrashLoopBackOff debugging.

Container Orchestration Q114 of 200
You run `kubectl describe pod` on a failing pod. Which section provides the most actionable diagnostic timeline?
A) Containers section — it shows the image and ports used
B) Events section at the bottom — it shows a chronological timeline: "Scheduled → Pulling → Pulled → Created → Started → BackOff" with timestamps
C) Conditions section — it shows True/False for PodScheduled, Initialized, ContainersReady, Ready
D) Labels and Annotations section — it shows metadata about the pod
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Events section at the bottom of kubectl describe pod is the most valuable diagnostic section. It shows a chronological timeline of everything that happened to the pod: scheduling decisions, image pulls (with progress), container creation, startup, and failures with specific error messages and timestamps. The Conditions section gives you current boolean status, but Events tell you the story of what happened.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl describe pod — Events Section Timeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl describe pod django-app-7f8c9d-abc12                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Events:                                                         │
  │  ┌──────┬──────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │ TYPE │ REASON   │      AGE      │ MESSAGE                  │ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Normal│Scheduled │      5m       │Successfully assigned     │ │
  │  │      │          │               │jobpost/django-app to anih│ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Normal│Pulling   │      5m       │Pulling image             │ │
  │  │      │          │               │"123456.dkr.ecr.../django"│ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Normal│Pulled    │      4m50s    │Successfully pulled image │ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Normal│Created   │      4m50s    │Created container django  │ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Normal│Started   │      4m50s    │Started container django  │ │
  │  ├──────┼──────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────────┤ │
  │  │Warning│BackOff  │      30s      │Back-off restarting failed│ │
  │  │      │          │               │container (exit code 137) │ │
  │  └──────┴──────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────────────┘ │
  │                                                                  │
  │  INTERPRETATION:                                                 │
  │  "Scheduled" → Node assignment worked (not a scheduling issue)   │
  │  "Pulling/Pulled" → Image was accessible (not a registry issue)  │
  │  "Created/Started" → Container launched briefly (not a runtime)  │
  │  "BackOff + exit code 137" → OOMKilled! Memory limit too low!    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if you see "BackOff" with exit code 137 in the Events, the Django container is being OOMKilled — increase its memory limit. If you see "Failed to pull image" and "ErrImagePull", the ECR credentials in imagePullSecrets may have expired. KCNA key: Events = the pod's diary. Read it bottom-up for the most recent issues.

Container Orchestration Q115 of 200
What is a Liveness probe in Kubernetes? Choose the most accurate description.
A) A health check that determines if a container is "alive" — if it fails, the kubelet KILLS and RESTARTS the container. Use when your app can get into a broken state that only a restart can fix
B) A health check that determines if a container can receive traffic — if it fails, the pod is removed from the Service's endpoints but NOT restarted
C) A startup check that gives slow-starting applications extra time before liveness kicks in
D) A monitoring probe that sends metrics to Prometheus — it doesn't affect the pod lifecycle
✅ Correct Answer: A

Liveness probe answers: "Is my app alive, or is it stuck in a deadlock?" If the probe fails (after failureThreshold consecutive failures), the kubelet kills the container and restarts it (subject to the pod's restartPolicy). This is useful for applications that can get into unrecoverable states — like a thread deadlock, infinite loop, or corrupted internal state. Three types: exec (run a command), httpGet (HTTP endpoint check), tcpSocket (TCP port check).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Liveness Probe — "Is my app alive?" (failed = RESTART)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LIVENESS PROBE: Check if the container is HEALTHY               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  spec.containers[0].livenessProbe:                        │   │
  │  │    httpGet:                                               │   │
  │  │      path: /healthz            ← Django health endpoint   │   │
  │  │      port: 8000                                          │   │
  │  │    initialDelaySeconds: 30     ← Wait 30s before first    │   │
  │  │    periodSeconds: 10           ← Check every 10 seconds   │   │
  │  │    failureThreshold: 3         ← 3 failures = RESTART     │   │
  │  │    timeoutSeconds: 5           ← 5s timeout per check     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  TIMELINE:                                                       │
  │  t=0s   Container starts                                         │
  │  t=30s  First liveness check (initialDelaySeconds)               │
  │  t=40s  Second check → OK ✅                                     │
  │  t=50s  Third check → FAIL ❌ (1st failure)                      │
  │  t=60s  Fourth check → FAIL ❌ (2nd failure)                     │
  │  t=70s  Fifth check → FAIL ❌ (3rd failure = threshold)          │
  │  t=70s  KUBELET KILLS AND RESTARTS CONTAINER 🔄                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WARNING: Liveness probe with NO initialDelaySeconds + heavy app │
  │  = container killed during startup! Use startupProbe instead.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django container has a liveness probe at /healthz that checks if Django can respond to HTTP requests. If Gunicorn workers all deadlock, the probe fails, and Kubernetes restarts the container — bringing the API back online without human intervention. KCNA key: Liveness = Kill & Restart. Don't use it for dependency checks (e.g., "is PostgreSQL up?") — that causes cascading restarts!

Container Orchestration Q116 of 200
What happens when a Readiness probe fails on a pod that is part of a Service?
A) The pod is immediately restarted by the kubelet — same behavior as a liveness probe failure
B) Nothing — readiness probes are only for informational purposes and don't affect traffic routing
C) The pod is REMOVED from the Service's Endpoints (no more traffic sent to it) but NOT restarted — it continues running. When the probe succeeds again, it's added back to the Service
D) The pod is terminated and a new pod is created by the ReplicaSet controller
✅ Correct Answer: C

Readiness probe answers: "Is my app ready to serve requests?" If it fails, the Endpoints controller removes the pod's IP from the Service's Endpoints list — traffic stops flowing to that pod. But the pod is NOT restarted — it keeps running. This is critical for scenarios like: a pod is overloaded and needs to shed traffic, or a pod's database connection is temporarily down but the app itself is fine.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Readiness vs Liveness — Two Different Purposes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  READINESS PROBE: "Can you serve traffic?" (failed = remove EP)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Service: django-api (selector: app=django)                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐              │
  │  │ Pod A (Ready=True)  │   │ Pod B (Ready=False) │              │
  │  │ IP: 10.244.1.5      │   │ IP: 10.244.1.6      │              │
  │  │ ✅ In Endpoints     │   │ ❌ REMOVED from EP  │              │
  │  │ ✅ Receives traffic │   │ ❌ No traffic       │              │
  │  └─────────────────────┘   └─────────────────────┘              │
  │            │                          │                          │
  │            │                          │                          │
  │  ┌─────────▼──────────────────────────▼──────────────────────┐  │
  │  │              kube-proxy (iptables/IPVS rules)              │  │
  │  │  Traffic → Pod A (10.244.1.5:8000) ONLY                  │  │
  │  │  Pod B is NOT in the list — it's temporarily bypassed     │  │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  KEY DIFFERENCE:                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ PROBE    │ FAILURE ACTION     │ USE CASE                 │   │
  │  ├──────────┼────────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │Liveness  │ KILL + RESTART     │ App deadlocked, restart  │   │
  │  │Readiness │ Remove from Service│ App overloaded, shed load│   │
  │  │Startup   │ Block liveness/    │ Slow-starting app        │   │
  │  │          │ readiness checks   │ (JVM, ML model loading)  │   │
  │  └──────────┴────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django pod has a readiness probe at /ready that checks if the database connection pool is healthy. If PostgreSQL is temporarily restarting, readiness fails → the pod is removed from the Service → no 500 errors for users. When PostgreSQL comes back, readiness succeeds → pod rejoins the Service. KCNA key: Readiness = traffic control, NOT pod lifecycle.

Container Orchestration Q117 of 200
Your Java application takes 90 seconds to start before it can handle requests. Which probe should you configure to prevent premature liveness/readiness failures?
A) Set initialDelaySeconds: 120 on both liveness and readiness probes — this is the recommended approach
B) Use a Startup probe — it runs FIRST, and until it succeeds, liveness and readiness probes are disabled. This gives slow-starting apps dedicated time to boot
C) Set failureThreshold: 30 on the liveness probe — this allows 30 failures before restarting
D) Use a preStop hook that waits 90 seconds before starting the probes
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Startup probe (introduced in Kubernetes 1.16, stable in 1.20) is specifically designed for slow-starting applications (JVM, ML models, legacy apps). When a startup probe is configured, liveness and readiness probes are disabled until the startup probe succeeds. This means you can set aggressive liveness checks (e.g., every 5 seconds) without worrying about killing the container during its long boot. Once startup succeeds, liveness and readiness take over.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Startup Probe — Dedicated Boot Time for Slow Apps
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITHOUT STARTUP PROBE (BAD — premature kill):                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Liveness: initialDelaySeconds=30, periodSeconds=10, failThresh=3│
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=0s   Container starts (JVM loading, Spring Boot initializing) │
  │  t=30s  Liveness check 1 → FAIL (app still booting) 🔴           │
  │  t=40s  Liveness check 2 → FAIL 🔴                               │
  │  t=50s  Liveness check 3 → FAIL 🔴 → KILLED! RESTART!           │
  │  (App was 75% through boot — would have been ready at t=90s!)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WITH STARTUP PROBE (GOOD — patient boot):                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  startupProbe:                                                   │
  │    httpGet:                                                      │
  │      path: /actuator/health     ← Spring Boot health endpoint    │
  │      port: 8080                                                │
  │    failureThreshold: 30         ← Up to 30 failures allowed     │
  │    periodSeconds: 10            ← Check every 10s (30×10=300s)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=0s    Container starts, startup probe active                 │
  │  t=10s   Startup check 1 → FAIL 🔴 (still booting, OK!)         │
  │  ...     (continues failing while app boots)                     │
  │  t=90s   Startup check 9 → SUCCESS ✅ (app ready!)              │
  │  t=90s   Liveness + Readiness probes NOW ACTIVATED               │
  │          (they take over, using their own configs)               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if Django+Gunicorn took 60+ seconds to start (loading ML models or large datasets), a Startup probe would ensure the pod isn't killed prematurely. Modern Django apps boot quickly, so a simple initialDelaySeconds: 15 on the liveness probe is usually sufficient. KCNA key: Startup probe = "give my app time to boot before health checks kick in."

Container Orchestration Q118 of 200
A pod has crashed and restarted 5 times. You need to see the logs from the PREVIOUS (crashed) container instance. Which command do you use?
A) kubectl logs pod-name — Kubernetes automatically stores all previous container logs in the pod's status
B) kubectl describe pod pod-name — the describe output includes the last 50 lines of logs
C) kubectl logs pod-name --all — the --all flag shows logs from all instances including crashed ones
D) kubectl logs pod-name --previous — the --previous flag retrieves logs from the PREVIOUSLY terminated container instance (invaluable for CrashLoopBackOff debugging)
✅ Correct Answer: D

kubectl logs pod-name --previous (or -p) is the most critical debugging flag for CrashLoopBackOff scenarios. When a container crashes and is restarted, the kubelet keeps the last terminated container's logs available for a short time. Without --previous, you only see logs from the CURRENT (newly started) container — which probably hasn't produced the error yet.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl logs — Critical Flags for Debugging
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl logs COMMAND REFERENCE                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  FLAG          │  PURPOSE                                │   │
  │  ├────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  --previous,-p │  Logs from PREVIOUS (crashed) container │   │
  │  │  --tail=N      │  Show only last N lines                 │   │
  │  │  --since=5m    │  Show logs from last 5 minutes          │   │
  │  │  --timestamps  │  Show timestamps on each log line       │   │
  │  │  --follow,-f   │  Stream logs in real-time (like tail -f)│   │
  │  │  --all-containers│ Logs from ALL containers in the pod   │   │
  │  │  -c container  │  Specify which container (multi-container│   │
  │  │                │  pods like pod with sidecar)            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  CRASHLoopBackOff DEBUGGING SEQUENCE:                            │
  │  1. kubectl describe pod django-app → Check "Last State: Reason" │
  │  2. kubectl logs django-app --previous → See crash error        │
  │  3. kubectl logs django-app --previous --tail=50 → Last 50 lines│
  │                                                                  │
  │  MULTI-CONTAINER POD:                                            │
  │  kubectl logs django-app -c django  → Django container logs      │
  │  kubectl logs django-app -c nginx   → Nginx sidecar logs         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django container crashes, run kubectl logs django-app-xxx --previous to see the Python traceback. Without --previous, you'd see the new container's startup logs ("Watching for file changes...") — not the actual error. KCNA exam: --previous is the #1 flag for CrashLoopBackOff.

Container Orchestration Q119 of 200
You need to run a diagnostic command inside a running container (e.g., check if the database is reachable from within the pod). Which command do you use?
A) kubectl attach pod-name — this attaches to the container's main process and lets you send signals
B) kubectl exec pod-name -- command — this runs a command inside a running container, like kubectl exec django-app -- python manage.py check --deploy
C) kubectl run diagnostic --image=busybox --rm -it -- restartPolicy=Never — you must create a new pod for diagnostics
D) kubectl cp local-file pod-name:/path — copy a diagnostic script into the container and wait for it to run via cron
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl exec runs a command inside an already running container. It's like SSH'ing into the container. Common uses: kubectl exec -it pod-name -- /bin/bash (interactive shell), kubectl exec pod-name -- ls /app (check files), kubectl exec pod-name -- curl localhost:8080/healthz (test internal endpoints). Use -c container-name for multi-container pods (e.g., -c django vs -c nginx).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl exec — Running Commands Inside Containers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  COMMON kubectl exec PATTERNS                                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ # Interactive shell (most common)                         │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec -it django-app-xxx -- /bin/bash              │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ # Run a specific command                                  │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- python manage.py check     │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ # Test connectivity from inside the container             │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- curl postgres-svc:5432     │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- nslookup redis-svc         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ # Check environment variables                             │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- env | grep DATABASE        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ # Check mounted files                                     │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- cat /etc/config/app.yaml   │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │ # Multi-container pod: specify which container            │   │
  │  │ kubectl exec -it django-app-xxx -c nginx -- /bin/sh       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  exec vs attach vs run:                                          │
  │  exec  → Run a NEW command in an existing container              │
  │  attach → Connect to the MAIN process (PID 1) of a container    │
  │  run   → Create a COMPLETELY NEW pod (for one-off tasks)         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if the Django API returns 500 errors, you can kubectl exec -it django-app-xxx -- python manage.py dbshell to check if PostgreSQL is reachable, or kubectl exec django-app-xxx -- env | grep DATABASE_URL to verify the database connection string. KCNA key: exec = run command in existing container. run = create NEW pod.

Container Orchestration Q120 of 200
You want to temporarily access a pod's port from your local machine for debugging (e.g., access the Django admin panel at port 8000). Which command do you use?
A) kubectl expose pod pod-name --port=8000 — creates a Service to expose the pod externally
B) kubectl proxy — starts a local proxy to the API server that exposes all pods automatically
C) kubectl port-forward pod/pod-name 8000:8000 — creates a secure tunnel from your local machine to the pod through the API server, no Service needed
D) kubectl ingress pod-name --local-port=8000 — creates a temporary Ingress rule for local access
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl port-forward creates a secure TCP tunnel from your local machine to a pod (or service/deployment) through the Kubernetes API server. No Service, Ingress, or NodePort is needed. It's perfect for temporary debugging — access a database, admin panel, or metrics endpoint without exposing anything to the network. The tunnel stays open until you Ctrl+C.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl port-forward — Secure Tunnel for Local Debugging
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl port-forward pod/django-app-xxx 8000:8000               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────┐          ┌──────────────┐          ┌─────────┐│
  │  │ Your Laptop  │  HTTPS   │  API Server  │  HTTP    │ Pod     ││
  │  │ localhost:   │─────────▶│  (kube-apiserver)│──────▶│ :8000   ││
  │  │   8000       │  tunnel  │              │  tunnel  │(Django) ││
  │  └──────────────┘          └──────────────┘          └─────────┘│
  │                                                                  │
  │  ACCESS PATTERNS:                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ # Forward local port to pod                               │   │
  │  │ kubectl port-forward pod/django-app-xxx 8000:8000         │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Forward to a Service (load-balanced across pods)        │   │
  │  │ kubectl port-forward svc/django-api 8000:8000              │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Forward to a Deployment (picks one pod)                 │   │
  │  │ kubectl port-forward deploy/django 8000:8000               │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Bind to all interfaces (accessible from network)        │   │
  │  │ kubectl port-forward --address 0.0.0.0 pod/xxx 8000:8000  │   │
  │  │ ⚠️  SECURITY: Exposes pod to network!                     │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Access PostgreSQL from local DBeaver/pgAdmin            │   │
  │  │ kubectl port-forward svc/postgres 5432:5432                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use kubectl port-forward svc/postgres 5432:5432 to connect pgAdmin on your laptop directly to the cluster's PostgreSQL, or kubectl port-forward pod/django-app-xxx 8000:8000 to access the Django REST Framework browsable API. KCNA key: port-forward = temporary local tunnel, NOT a production solution.

Container Orchestration Q121 of 200
You run `kubectl top pod` and see resource usage. What component must be installed in the cluster for this command to work?
A) Prometheus — kubectl top reads metrics directly from Prometheus
B) Metrics Server — it collects resource metrics from the kubelet's Summary API and exposes them via the metrics.k8s.io API; kubectl top queries this API
C) Nothing — kubectl top is built into kubectl and works out of the box on any cluster
D) cAdvisor — kubectl top reads directly from cAdvisor running on each node
✅ Correct Answer: B

Metrics Server is a cluster addon that collects CPU and memory metrics from each node's kubelet Summary API (which reads from cAdvisor), aggregates them, and exposes them through the metrics.k8s.io API. kubectl top pod/node queries this API. Without Metrics Server installed, kubectl top returns "error: Metrics API not available." Metrics Server is also used by the HPA (Horizontal Pod Autoscaler) for CPU/memory-based autoscaling.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Metrics Pipeline — cAdvisor → kubelet → Metrics Server → kubectl top

  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────┐              │
  │  │ cAdvisor│────▶│ kubelet   │────▶│Metrics Server│              │
  │  │(per node│     │Summary API│     │(cluster addon)│              │
  │  │runtime) │     │:10255     │     │              │              │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────┘     └──────┬───────┘              │
  │                                          │                       │
  │                                          ▼                       │
  │                                   ┌──────────────┐              │
  │                                   │metrics.k8s.io│              │
  │                                   │    API       │              │
  │                                   └──────┬───────┘              │
  │                                          │                       │
  │                          ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐      │
  │                          │               │               │      │
  │                          ▼               ▼               ▼      │
  │                   ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌─────────┐  │
  │                   │kubectl   │  │     HPA      │  │  VPA    │  │
  │                   │top pod   │  │(autoscaling) │  │(vertical)│  │

  │                                                                  │

  │    metrics-server/releases/latest/download/components.yaml       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  OUTPUT:                                                         │

  │  NAME          CPU(cores)   MEMORY(bytes)                        │
  │  django-app    150m         256Mi                                │

On the anih kubeadm cluster, Metrics Server may not be installed by default. For anihpj/jobpost, install it to enable HPA-based autoscaling of Django pods when CPU exceeds 80%. KCNA key: no Metrics Server = kubectl top fails AND HPA can't scale on CPU/memory.

Container Orchestration Q122 of 200
How do you view cluster-wide events to find out what happened across all namespaces in the last hour?
A) kubectl describe cluster — this shows all events at the cluster level
B) kubectl logs kube-apiserver — all events are logged by the API server
C) kubectl get events --all-namespaces --sort-by='.lastTimestamp' — events are first-class API resources that show what happened (scheduling, pulls, starts, kills, warnings) with timestamps
D) kubectl get audit-log — events are stored in the audit log by default
✅ Correct Answer: C

Events are a first-class Kubernetes API resource (v1/events). They record what happened in the cluster: pods being scheduled, images being pulled, containers starting/failing, nodes becoming NotReady, etc. Events are namespaced but can be queried cluster-wide with --all-namespaces (or -A). They are retained for 1 hour by default (controlled by --event-ttl in kube-apiserver). Use --sort-by='.lastTimestamp' to see the most recent events first.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl get events — The Cluster's Diary
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl get events -A --sort-by='.lastTimestamp'                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  NAMESPACE  LAST SEEN  TYPE     REASON     OBJECT         MESSAGE│
  │  ─────────  ─────────  ────     ──────     ──────         ───────│
  │  jobpost    2s         Warning  BackOff    pod/django     Back-  │
  │                                           -app-xxx        off    │
  │  kube-system 10s       Normal   Scheduled  pod/coredns-   Success│
  │                                           abc123          fully  │
  │  jobpost    30s        Normal   Pulled     pod/django-    Success│
  │                                           app-yyy        fully   │
  │  default    1m         Warning  Failed     pod/bad-pod    Error: │
  │                                                       ImagePull  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  USEFUL FILTERS:                                                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ # Only warnings (focus on problems)                       │   │
  │  │ kubectl get events -A --field-selector type=Warning       │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Events for a specific pod                               │   │
  │  │ kubectl get events --field-selector involvedObject.name=  │   │
  │  │   django-app-xxx                                          │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Watch events in real-time                               │   │
  │  │ kubectl get events -A --watch                             │   │
  │  │                                                            │   │
  │  │ # Events sorted newest first                              │   │
  │  │ kubectl get events -A --sort-by='.lastTimestamp'          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RETENTION: Default 1 hour (--event-ttl in kube-apiserver)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

When debugging anihpj/jobpost, kubectl get events -n jobpost --sort-by='.lastTimestamp' is often the first command to run — it shows if PostgreSQL crashed, if the Django image failed to pull, or if a ConfigMap update triggered a rolling restart. KCNA key: events are the cluster's diary — always check them first when something goes wrong.

💾 Section 2.4: Storage in KubernetesQ123-Q132

🟣 Volumes, PersistentVolumes, StorageClasses, CSI & StatefulSets

Domain: Container Orchestration (28%) — from ephemeral emptyDir to dynamically provisioned cloud volumes, how Kubernetes manages data.

Container Orchestration Q123 of 200
What is the relationship between Volumes and VolumeMounts in a pod specification?
A) Volumes and VolumeMounts are the same thing — the terms are used interchangeably
B) VolumeMounts define what storage exists at the pod level, and Volumes define where containers mount them
C) Volumes are for persistent storage and VolumeMounts are for ephemeral storage only
D) Volumes define WHAT storage exists (the "plug" — emptyDir, hostPath, PVC), VolumeMounts define WHERE each container mounts it (the "socket" — mountPath). One Volume can be mounted by multiple containers
✅ Correct Answer: D

In a pod spec, spec.volumes is an array that declares available storage — like plugs on a power strip. spec.containers[].volumeMounts is an array that connects a container to a volume at a specific mountPath — like plugging a device into a socket. This separation is powerful: one volume can be mounted by ALL containers in the pod (sidecar pattern for log sharing), and each container can mount it at a different path.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Volumes vs VolumeMounts — The Plug and Socket Analogy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Pod: django-app                                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    volumes:                        ← "AVAILABLE PLUGS"           │
  │    - name: app-code                                              │
  │      emptyDir: {}                 ← Shared temp space            │
  │    - name: db-data                                               │
  │      persistentVolumeClaim:                                      │
  │        claimName: postgres-pvc   ← Persistent storage           │
  │    - name: config                                                │
  │      configMap:                                                  │
  │        name: django-config       ← ConfigMap as files            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Container: django                                        │   │
  │  │  volumeMounts:              ← "WHERE TO PLUG IN"          │   │
  │  │  - name: app-code                                         │   │
  │  │    mountPath: /app                                       │   │
  │  │  - name: config                                           │   │
  │  │    mountPath: /etc/django/settings.py                     │   │
  │  │    subPath: settings.py     ← Mount specific file         │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Container: nginx (sidecar)                               │   │
  │  │  volumeMounts:                                            │   │
  │  │  - name: app-code                                         │   │
  │  │    mountPath: /usr/share/nginx/html  ← SAME volume!       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  KEY: volumes = what, volumeMounts = where. One volume,          │
  │       multiple mounts, multiple containers, multiple paths.      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, a shared emptyDir volume allows the Nginx sidecar to serve static files that Django collects with collectstatic. The PostgreSQL container mounts a PVC-backed volume at /var/lib/postgresql/data for persistent database storage. KCNA key: volumes = pod-level declaration, volumeMounts = container-level attachment.

Container Orchestration Q124 of 200
What happens to data in an emptyDir volume when the pod using it is deleted?
A) The data is DELETED — emptyDir is tied to the pod's lifecycle. It's created when the pod is scheduled and destroyed when the pod is deleted (container restarts preserve the data, but pod deletion wipes it)
B) The data persists as an orphaned volume on the node and must be manually cleaned up
C) The data is automatically backed up to the default StorageClass before the pod is deleted
D) The data is retained for 24 hours and then garbage collected by the kubelet
✅ Correct Answer: A

emptyDir is the simplest volume type. It's an empty directory created on the node's filesystem (typically /var/lib/kubelet/pods/.../volumes/...) when the pod is scheduled. The data survives container restarts within the same pod, but is permanently deleted when the pod is deleted. Use emptyDir for temporary scratch space, caching, or sharing data between containers in the same pod (sidecar pattern).

📝 Detailed Explanation
emptyDir — Ephemeral Storage Tied to Pod Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  emptyDir LIFECYCLE                                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=0: Pod created → emptyDir created on node's disk              │
  │        Node: /var/lib/kubelet/pods//volumes/            │
  │              kubernetes.io~empty-dir//              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=1: Container writes data to emptyDir                          │
  │        Container: echo "important" > /cache/data.txt             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=2: Container CRASHES and RESTARTS                             │
  │        ✅ Data SURVIVES! (same pod, same emptyDir)               │
  │        Container: cat /cache/data.txt → "important"              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  t=3: Pod DELETED                                                │
  │        ❌ Data DELETED! (emptyDir cleaned up with pod)           │
  │        ALL data in /cache is GONE permanently                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  OPTIONAL: medium: "Memory"                                      │
  │    emptyDir backed by tmpfs (RAM) — faster but uses pod memory   │
  │    (counts against the container's memory limit!)                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, an emptyDir can be used to share static files between the Django container (which runs collectstatic) and the Nginx sidecar (which serves them). If the pod is deleted, the collected static files are lost — but they get regenerated on the next deployment. KCNA key: emptyDir = temporary, tied to pod, deleted on pod deletion.

Container Orchestration Q125 of 200
What is a hostPath volume and why should you avoid it in production multi-node clusters?
A) hostPath is a network-attached volume that provides high availability across nodes
B) hostPath is a cloud provider volume type that is automatically replicated
C) hostPath mounts a file or directory from the NODE's filesystem into the pod — it ties the pod to a specific node (can't reschedule) and grants access to the node's filesystem (security risk); use only for DaemonSet monitoring/logging agents
D) hostPath is the recommended way to share files between pods running on different nodes
✅ Correct Answer: C

hostPath mounts a file or directory from the host node's filesystem directly into a container. It creates two problems: (1) Node affinity — the pod can only run on the node with that data, so if the node dies, the pod's data is lost, (2) Security — the container can read/write to the node's filesystem, potentially escaping the container. hostPath is appropriate only for node-level agents (DaemonSets like Fluentd, Prometheus Node Exporter, or CSI node plugins).

📝 Detailed Explanation
hostPath — When to Use and When to Avoid
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  hostPath: Mounts node filesystem INTO container                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Node "worker-1"                        Node "worker-2"          │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐               ┌─────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ /var/log/containers │               │ /var/log/containers │  │
  │  │ /var/lib/docker     │               │ /var/lib/docker     │  │
  │  │ /sys/fs/cgroup       │               │ /sys/fs/cgroup       │  │
  │  └─────────┬───────────┘               └─────────┬───────────┘  │
  │            │ hostPath                             │ hostPath      │
  │            ▼                                      ▼              │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐                   ┌─────────────────┐      │
  │  │ Pod: fluentd    │                   │ Pod: fluentd    │      │
  │  │ (DaemonSet)     │                   │ (DaemonSet)     │      │
  │  │ Reads host logs │                   │ Reads host logs │      │
  │  └─────────────────┘                   └─────────────────┘      │
  │  ✅ GOOD: DaemonSet logging agent (one per node)                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐                                           │
  │  │ Pod: django-app │                                           │
  │  │ hostPath:        │                                           │
  │  │   /data/uploads  │  ← Mounted from node filesystem           │
  │  │ Schedule on      │                                           │
  │  │ worker-1 ✅      │                                           │
  │  │ Schedule on      │  ❌ CAN'T! /data/uploads doesn't exist   │
  │  │ worker-2         │     on worker-2!                          │
  │  └─────────────────┘                                           │
  │  ❌ BAD: Application data — not portable!                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RULE: hostPath = DaemonSet agents ONLY. Apps = PVC/CSI.        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, do NOT use hostPath for the PostgreSQL data directory — if the pod reschedules to a different node, the database is gone. Use a PVC instead. hostPath is fine for node-level monitoring agents on the anih kubeadm single-node cluster. KCNA key: hostPath = node-locked, use PVC for portable storage.

Container Orchestration Q126 of 200
What is a PersistentVolume (PV) in Kubernetes? Use the "parking lot" analogy.
A) A PV is a cluster-wide storage resource (like a parking spot) provisioned by an admin or dynamically — it exists independently of any pod and has its own lifecycle
B) A PV is a storage request made by a pod — it's deleted when the pod is deleted
C) A PV is a cloud-specific volume type (EBS, Azure Disk) that can't be used with on-premises storage
D) A PV is a temporary volume that gets created when a pod starts and deleted when it stops
✅ Correct Answer: A

A PersistentVolume (PV) is a piece of storage in the cluster that has been provisioned by an administrator or dynamically via StorageClass. It has a lifecycle independent of any individual pod that uses it. Think of PVs as parking spots in a garage — they exist whether or not a car (pod) is parked in them. PVs are cluster-scoped resources (not namespaced) with attributes like capacity, accessMode, and reclaimPolicy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PersistentVolume — The "Parking Spot" of Kubernetes Storage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PARKING LOT ANALOGY                                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  PV "spot-1"     PV "spot-2"     PV "spot-3"            │   │
  │  │  [10Gi, RWO]     [50Gi, RWO]     [100Gi, RWX]           │   │
  │  │  (AWS EBS)       (NFS share)      (Azure Disk)           │   │
  │  │   🚗 Occupied      🅿️ Empty         🚗 Occupied           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PV PROPERTIES:                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  capacity.storage: 10Gi    ← How big?                    │   │
  │  │  accessModes: [RWO]        ← How can it be accessed?     │   │
  │  │  persistentVolumeReclaimPolicy: Retain/Delete/Recycle    │   │
  │  │  storageClassName: fast-ssd ← Which "menu" category?     │   │
  │  │  CSI/volume driver config  ← What backs this PV?         │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LIFECYCLE:                                                      │
  │    Provisioned → Available → Bound (to PVC) → Released → Retained│
  │                        ↑                                         │
  │                   (PVC claims it)                                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the PostgreSQL database needs a 10Gi PV. It can be statically provisioned (admin creates it manually on the anih node) or dynamically provisioned (StorageClass creates it automatically when the PVC is created). KCNA key: PV = cluster resource, PVC = user request. They bind like a parking spot and a reservation.

Container Orchestration Q127 of 200
What is a PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) and how does it bind to a PV?
A) A PVC is a volume type like emptyDir — it creates storage directly on the node
B) A PVC is a user's storage request (like a parking reservation) specifying size and access mode — Kubernetes finds a matching PV (or dynamically provisions one via StorageClass) and binds them 1:1
C) A PVC is an alias for a ConfigMap that stores persistent configuration data
D) A PVC automatically creates a new disk on the cloud provider every time a pod restarts
✅ Correct Answer: B

A PersistentVolumeClaim (PVC) is a namespaced request for storage by a user. It specifies requirements like size ("I need 5Gi"), access mode ("ReadWriteOnce"), and optionally a StorageClass name. Kubernetes matches the PVC to an available PV with sufficient capacity and matching access modes. Once bound, that PV is dedicated to that PVC — it's a 1:1 binding. If no PV matches and a StorageClass with a provisioner exists, dynamic provisioning creates a new PV automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
PVC → PV Binding — Request Meets Resource
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PVC: "I need 5Gi of ReadWriteOnce storage"                      │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐                                         │
  │  │ PVC: postgres-data  │  ← Namespaced (jobpost namespace)      │
  │  │   storage: 5Gi      │                                         │
  │  │   accessModes: [RWO]│                                         │
  │  │   storageClassName: │  ← If set, ONLY uses PVs with this SC  │
  │  │     fast-ssd        │                                         │
  │  └──────────┬──────────┘                                         │
  │             │ "Find me a matching PV!"                           │
  │             ▼                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  AVAILABLE PVs IN CLUSTER:                               │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  PV "pv-001": 3Gi, RWO, standard  → ❌ Too small          │   │
  │  │  PV "pv-002": 5Gi, RWO, fast-ssd  → ✅ PERFECT MATCH!    │   │
  │  │  PV "pv-003": 5Gi, RWX, fast-ssd  → ❌ Wrong access mode │   │
  │  │  PV "pv-004": 10Gi, RWO, fast-ssd → ✅ Also matches      │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Kubernetes picks the BEST match (pv-002: exact size)     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  BINDING IS 1:1: Once PV "pv-002" is bound to PVC "postgres-    │
  │  data", no other PVC can use pv-002 until it's released.         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the PostgreSQL StatefulSet uses a PVC template: each replica gets its own PVC (e.g., postgres-data-postgres-0) bound to its own PV. This ensures each database instance has dedicated, persistent storage. KCNA key: PVC = request (namespaced), PV = resource (cluster-wide), 1:1 binding.

Container Orchestration Q128 of 200
What is a StorageClass and what problem does it solve?
A) StorageClass is a Kubernetes API group that defines how pods access the filesystem
B) StorageClass is a label on PVs that categorizes them as "fast" or "slow" for informational purposes only
C) StorageClass is a DaemonSet that runs storage drivers on each node
D) StorageClass is the "menu" of storage options — it defines classes of storage (fast-ssd, slow-hdd, aws-ebs-gp3) and enables DYNAMIC PROVISIONING: when a PVC requests a StorageClass, the provisioner automatically creates the PV, no admin intervention needed
✅ Correct Answer: D

A StorageClass is the bridge between user storage requests and the underlying storage infrastructure. It defines classes of storage (e.g., "fast-ssd" backed by AWS EBS gp3, "slow-hdd" backed by NFS) and includes a provisioner (CSI driver) that can dynamically create PVs on demand. Without StorageClasses, admins must manually create every PV — with them, users just create PVCs and the storage auto-provisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation
StorageClass — The "Menu" of Storage with Auto-Provisioning
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  STORAGECLASS: THE STORAGE MENU                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  MENU ITEM: fast-ssd                                     │   │
  │  │  provisioner: ebs.csi.aws.com    ← AWS EBS CSI driver    │   │
  │  │  parameters:                                              │   │
  │  │    type: gp3                   ← EBS volume type          │   │
  │  │    iops: "3000"                ← Performance spec        │   │
  │  │  reclaimPolicy: Delete         ← Delete PV when PVC goes │   │
  │  │  💰 $0.08/GB/month                                       │   │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  MENU ITEM: slow-hdd                                     │   │
  │  │  provisioner: nfs.csi.k8s.io    ← NFS CSI driver         │   │
  │  │  parameters:                                              │   │
  │  │    server: nfs-server.default   ← NFS server address      │   │
  │  │    path: /exports/data                                   │   │
  │  │  💰 $0.02/GB/month                                       │   │
  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │  MENU ITEM: default (default StorageClass)               │   │
  │  │  provisioner: rancher.io/local-path  ← kind/k3s default  │   │
  │  │  💰 Free (local node storage)                             │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DYNAMIC PROVISIONING FLOW:                                      │
  │  User creates PVC with storageClassName: "fast-ssd"             │
  │  → StorageClass "fast-ssd" activates                             │
  │  → Provisioner (ebs.csi.aws.com) creates EBS volume              │
  │  → PV created and bound to PVC                                   │
  │  → Pod mounts PVC → data persists!                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

On the anih kubeadm cluster, the default StorageClass is likely local-path (rancher.io/local-path) which creates hostPath-based PVs on the node. For anihpj/jobpost in production (EKS), use ebs.csi.aws.com with gp3 volumes. KCNA key: StorageClass = auto-provision PVs, no admin needed.

Container Orchestration Q129 of 200
What are the three accessModes for PersistentVolumes and what does each mean?
A) ReadOnly, WriteOnly, ReadWrite — they control file permissions inside the volume
B) SingleNode, MultiNode, ClusterWide — they control which nodes can mount the volume
C) RWO (ReadWriteOnce — one node can read/write), ROX (ReadOnlyMany — many nodes can read), RWX (ReadWriteMany — many nodes can read/write, requires NFS/Ceph/EFS)
D) local, regional, global — they control the geographic replication of the storage
✅ Correct Answer: C

The three access modes are: RWO (ReadWriteOnce) — the volume can be mounted as read-write by a single node (multiple pods on the SAME node can use it), typical for block storage (EBS, Azure Disk). ROX (ReadOnlyMany) — the volume can be mounted as read-only by many nodes simultaneously, common for shared datasets. RWX (ReadWriteMany) — many nodes can read-write simultaneously, requires shared filesystem like NFS, CephFS, GlusterFS, or AWS EFS.

📝 Detailed Explanation
accessModes — How Many Nodes Can Use This Volume?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ACCESS MODES VISUALIZED                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  RWO: ReadWriteOnce (MOST COMMON)                        │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐                                            │   │
  │  │  │ Node-1   │  ✅ Read/Write                             │   │
  │  │  │ ┌──────┐ │  ┌──────┐                                  │   │
  │  │  │ │ Pod A│ │  │  PV  │ ← Block storage (EBS, Azure Disk)│   │
  │  │  │ │ Pod B│ │  │      │   Both pods on SAME node = OK!   │   │
  │  │  │ └──────┘ │  └──────┘                                  │   │
  │  │  └──────────┘                                            │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐                                            │   │
  │  │  │ Node-2   │  ❌ Cannot mount (already on Node-1)       │   │
  │  │  └──────────┘                                            │   │
  │  │  USE: Databases (PostgreSQL, MySQL), single-replica apps │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  RWX: ReadWriteMany (MULTI-NODE READ/WRITE)              │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐               │   │
  │  │  │ Node-1   │  │ Node-2   │  │ Node-3   │               │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ R/W   │  │ ✅ R/W   │  │ ✅ R/W   │               │   │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘               │   │
  │  │       └──────────────┼──────────────┘                    │   │
  │  │                      │                                    │   │
  │  │               ┌──────▼──────┐                             │   │
  │  │               │ Shared NFS  │ ← NFS, CephFS, AWS EFS     │   │
  │  │               └─────────────┘                             │   │
  │  │  USE: Shared file storage, multiple web servers writing   │   │
  │  │       to the same uploads directory                       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
