  │  │          │────────────────────▶│   ┌──────────────┐   │       │
  │  │          │   changes to Git   │   │ArgoCD / Flux │   │       │
  │  └──────────┘                    │   │  "I PULL      │   │       │
  │                                  │   │   desired     │   │       │
  │  ┌──────────┐                    │   │   state"      │   │       │
  │  │ Git Repo │◀───────────────────│   │ ──syncs──▶   │   │       │
  │  │(k8s YAML)│  Pull every 3 min  │   │   Resources  │   │       │
  │  └──────────┘                    │   └──────────────┘   │       │
  │                                  └──────────────────────┘       │
  │  ✅ Cluster holds credentials (no external CI needs them)        │
  │  ✅ Agent runs INSIDE cluster (more secure)                      │
  │  ✅ Drift detection: manual changes get AUTO-REVERTED! 🔄        │
  │  ✅ Git becomes audit trail: git log = history of all changes    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ArgoCD runs inside the kubeadm cluster, watching the Git repo containing Kubernetes manifests. When a developer updates the image tag in the deployment YAML and pushes to Git, ArgoCD detects the change and syncs it. KCNA key: GitOps = Git is truth, agent pulls, drift auto-corrected.

App Delivery Q154 of 200
What is Argo CD and how does it implement GitOps for Kubernetes?
A) Argo CD is a CNCF-graduated GitOps tool that continuously monitors Git repositories and automatically syncs Kubernetes manifests — it provides a web UI, CLI, auto-sync, drift detection, and multi-cluster management via Application CRDs
B) Argo CD is a Kubernetes Ingress controller that routes traffic based on Git repository contents
C) Argo CD is a CI tool that replaces Jenkins and GitHub Actions for building container images
D) Argo CD is a monitoring dashboard that shows Kubernetes cluster health metrics from Git repositories
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD (CNCF Graduated) is a declarative GitOps continuous delivery tool for Kubernetes. It's implemented as a Kubernetes controller with an Application CRD. Key features: (1) Web UI showing sync status, resource tree, and diff, (2) Auto-sync — automatically apply changes when Git changes, (3) Drift detection — highlights when live state ≠ desired state, (4) Multi-cluster — one ArgoCD instance can manage multiple clusters, (5) Sync waves & hooks — control deployment ordering (PreSync → Sync → PostSync).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Architecture — Application Controller + Web UI
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ARGO CD ARCHITECTURE                                            │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Argo CD Components (in cluster):                         │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐      │   │
  │  │  │ API Server  │  │  Repo Server│  │ Application │      │   │
  │  │  │ (gRPC/REST) │  │(clone+render│  │ Controller  │      │   │
  │  │  │             │  │   manifests)│  │(sync+reconcile│     │   │
  │  │  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘      │   │
  │  │         │                │                │              │   │
  │  │         ▼                ▼                ▼              │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │   │
  │  │  │          Application CRD                          │   │   │
  │  │  │  spec:                                            │   │   │
  │  │  │    source:                                        │   │   │
  │  │  │      repoURL: github.com/Anihs-Canvas/anihpj-k8s │   │   │
  │  │  │      path: overlays/production                    │   │   │
  │  │  │    destination:                                   │   │   │
  │  │  │      server: https://kubernetes.default.svc       │   │   │
  │  │  │      namespace: jobpost                           │   │   │
  │  │  │    syncPolicy:                                    │   │   │
  │  │  │      automated:                                   │   │   │
  │  │  │        prune: true     ← Delete old resources     │   │   │
  │  │  │        selfHeal: true  ← Auto-revert drift        │   │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  WEB UI STATUS INDICATORS:                                       │
  │    🟢 Synced + Healthy   → Desired = Live, pods running          │
  │    🟡 OutOfSync          → Git changed, needs sync               │
  │    🔴 Degraded           → Live state unhealthy (pod crash)      │
  │    ⚪ Unknown            → Can't determine status                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ArgoCD manages the Django Deployment, PostgreSQL StatefulSet, and Redis Deployment across staging and production clusters. The web UI shows real-time sync status. KCNA key: Argo CD = Application CRD + auto-sync + drift detection + web UI.

App Delivery Q155 of 200
How does Flux differ from Argo CD in implementing GitOps, and what unique feature does Flux have?
A) Flux is identical to Argo CD — they implement the same features with different names
B) Flux is for VMs only while Argo CD is for Kubernetes
C) Flux replaces kubectl — it's a CLI tool, not a controller
D) Flux (CNCF Graduated) uses a multi-controller architecture (source, kustomize, helm, notification, image automation) with a unique feature: IMAGE AUTOMATION — Flux can automatically update image tags in Git when new images are pushed to the registry, closing the CI→CD loop
✅ Correct Answer: D

Flux (CNCF Graduated, same as ArgoCD) takes a modular controller approach: separate controllers for source (GitRepository, HelmRepository), Kustomize (Kustomization), Helm (HelmRelease), notification, and most uniquely — image automation. The Image Reflector + Image Automation controllers watch container registries for new image tags and automatically update Git with the new version. This creates a complete CI→CD loop: CI pushes image → Flux detects new tag → Flux commits update to Git → Flux syncs to cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Flux vs Argo CD — Feature Comparison
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FLUX (Modular Controllers):                                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────────┐    │
  │  │Source      │ │Kustomize │ │Helm      │ │Image Auto-   │    │
  │  │Controller  │ │Controller│ │Controller│ │mation Control │    │
  │  │            │ │          │ │          │ │              │    │
  │  │GitRepository│ │Kustomiza-│ │HelmRelease│ │ImageRepository│   │
  │  │OCIRepository│ │tion CRD  │ │CRD       │ │ImagePolicy CRD│   │
  │  │HelmRepository│ │          │ │          │ │ImageUpdateAuto│   │
  │  │            │ │          │ │          │ │mation CRD     │   │
  │  └────────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────────┘    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  UNIQUE FLUX FEATURE: Image Automation                           │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  CI builds image                                      │   │
  │  │  docker push anihpj/django:v1.2.3                     │   │
  │  │         │                                              │   │
  │  │         ▼                                              │   │
  │  │  Flux Image Reflector watches ECR                      │   │
  │  │  → Detects new tag: v1.2.3                             │   │
  │  │         │                                              │   │
  │  │         ▼                                              │   │
  │  │  Flux Image Automation:                                │   │
  │  │  → Edits deployment.yaml in Git                        │   │
  │  │  → git commit: "Update django to v1.2.3"               │   │
  │  │  → git push                                            │   │
  │  │         │                                              │   │
  │  │         ▼                                              │   │
  │  │  Flux Kustomize Controller detects Git change          │   │
  │  │  → Syncs to cluster                                    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ARGO CD vs FLUX:                                                │
  │  ┌────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │            │ ARGO CD           │ FLUX                     │  │
  │  ├────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Web UI     │ ✅ Yes            │ ❌ No (CLI + dashboards) │  │
  │  │ Image Auto │ ❌ No             │ ✅ Yes (unique!)         │  │
  │  │ Multi-Clstr│ ✅ Yes            │ ✅ Yes                   │  │
  │  │ Helm       │ ✅ Native         │ ✅ Helm Controller       │  │
  │  │ Drift Det  │ ✅ Auto-heal      │ ✅ Auto-remediate        │  │
  │  └────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Argo CD is preferred for its web UI (great for team visibility). But Flux's image automation could automatically update the image tag in Git whenever the CI pipeline pushes a new version, fully automating the CI→CD handoff. KCNA key: Flux = modular controllers + image automation (auto-update Git). ArgoCD = web UI + Application CRD.

App Delivery Q156 of 200
What is the complete GitOps reconciliation loop and what happens when someone manually deletes a Deployment that Argo CD manages?
A) Nothing — manual deletions are permanent because they're performed by a human
B) Git push → agent detects change → syncs to cluster → if someone manually deletes the Deployment (DRIFT) → agent detects live state ≠ desired state → AUTO-REMEDIATES by recreating the Deployment from Git
C) The agent sends an email to the administrator asking whether to recreate the Deployment
D) The agent deletes the Git repository entry because it considers manual actions as the new desired state
✅ Correct Answer: B

The GitOps reconciliation loop is: (1) Developer pushes to Git (desired state), (2) GitOps agent detects change, (3) Agent compares desired state (Git) with live state (cluster), (4) Agent applies diff to converge live → desired. Drift detection is the killer feature: if someone manually runs kubectl delete deployment django, the GitOps agent detects that live state ≠ desired state and automatically recreates the deployment within the next sync interval (typically 3 minutes). This makes manual changes effectively impossible in a GitOps-managed cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GitOps Reconciliation Loop — Drift Detection & Auto-Remediation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GITOPS RECONCILIATION LOOP (ArgoCD / Flux)                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  LOOP (runs every 3 minutes by default):                 │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  1. FETCH: Clone/pull Git repo                            │   │
  │  │            → Get desired state (k8s YAML)                 │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  2. RENDER: Apply Kustomize/Helm to generate manifests   │   │
  │  │            → Full list of all resources                   │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  3. COMPARE: kubectl diff desired vs live                 │   │
  │  │            → Are there differences?                       │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  4. SYNC: If differences found                            │   │
  │  │            → Apply changes                                │   │
  │  │            → OR auto-remediate (recreate deleted resources)│   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DRIFT EXAMPLE:                                                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ GIT (desired)    │          │ CLUSTER (live)            │     │
  │  │                  │          │                           │     │
  │  │ Deployment:      │          │ Deployment:               │     │
  │  │   name: django   │          │   (MANUALLY DELETED!) ❌  │     │
  │  │   replicas: 3    │          │                           │     │
  │  │                  │          │                           │     │
  │  │ ArgoCD compares: │          │                           │     │
  │  │ "Git says 3      │          │ Cluster says 0            │     │
  │  │  replicas exist" │──────────│ → DRIFT DETECTED!         │     │
  │  │                  │          │ → AUTO-REMEDIATE 🔄       │     │
  │  │                  │          │ → Recreates Deployment    │     │
  │  └──────────────────┘          └──────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RESULT: Manual change lasted < 3 minutes! Git wins.             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if a team member accidentally runs kubectl delete deployment django-api, ArgoCD's selfHeal: true setting detects the drift and recreates the deployment within 3 minutes. This is why GitOps is described as "self-healing infrastructure." KCNA key: GitOps reconciliation loop = fetch → render → compare → sync. Drift = auto-corrected.

App Delivery Q157 of 200
How does Argo CD manage deployments across multiple Kubernetes clusters (dev, staging, production)?
A) One Argo CD instance can manage multiple clusters by adding them as "destinations" — each Application CR specifies which cluster (destination) to sync to, enabling dev/staging/prod promotion from a single control plane
B) You must install a separate Argo CD instance in each cluster — one instance can only manage its own cluster
C) Argo CD uses Kubernetes Federation (KubeFed) to synchronize resources across clusters
D) Multi-cluster management requires a commercial Argo CD Enterprise license
✅ Correct Answer: A

Argo CD supports multi-cluster management natively. A single Argo CD instance (typically running in a "hub" cluster) can manage deployments to multiple "spoke" clusters by adding their API server URLs/credentials as cluster secrets. Each Application CR specifies a destination.server — which cluster to sync to. This enables promotion workflows: sync to dev (auto), verify, sync to staging (auto), verify, promote to production (manual or automated).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Argo CD Multi-Cluster — Hub & Spoke Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ARGO CD MULTI-CLUSTER MANAGEMENT                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  HUB CLUSTER (runs Argo CD)                              │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  Argo CD manages 3 clusters:                              │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │   │
  │  │  │  Application: anihpj-dev                         │    │   │
  │  │  │    destination: dev-cluster (auto-sync)           │    │   │
  │  │  │    path: overlays/development                     │    │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │   │
  │  │  │  Application: anihpj-staging                     │    │   │
  │  │  │    destination: staging-cluster (auto-sync)       │    │   │
  │  │  │    path: overlays/staging                         │    │   │
  │  │  ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────┤    │   │
  │  │  │  Application: anihpj-production                  │    │   │
  │  │  │    destination: prod-cluster (MANUAL sync)        │    │   │
  │  │  │    path: overlays/production                      │    │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PROMOTION FLOW:                                                 │
  │  Git change → DEV (auto) ──verify──▶ STAGING (auto)             │
  │                                         │                        │
  │                                    verify + approve              │
  │                                         │                        │
  │                                         ▼                        │
  │                                   PRODUCTION (manual sync)       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  Each cluster gets the SAME manifests with DIFFERENT Kustomize   │
  │  overlays (dev=1 replica, staging=3, prod=5, different DB URLs) │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, ArgoCD running on the anih kubeadm "hub" cluster could manage deployments to kind-cka-lab (dev), kind-cl1 (staging), and a separate production cluster. Each uses a different Kustomize overlay. KCNA key: one ArgoCD → multiple clusters via destination.server in Application CR.

App Delivery Q158 of 200
What is the fundamental difference between GitOps (pull-based) and traditional CI/CD (push-based) deployment?
A) There is no difference — they both deploy code to production
B) GitOps is faster because it uses WebSockets instead of HTTP
C) Traditional CI/CD: CI server PUSHES changes to cluster (requires cluster credentials outside cluster). GitOps: agent PULLS from Git (agent runs INSIDE cluster, more secure). Plus GitOps provides automatic drift detection and correction — manual changes are reverted
D) GitOps requires Kubernetes while traditional CI/CD works with any platform
✅ Correct Answer: C

The core architectural difference is push vs pull. Traditional CI/CD: an external system (Jenkins, GitHub Actions runner) has credentials to the cluster and pushes changes via kubectl/helm. GitOps: the agent runs inside the cluster (no external credentials needed) and pulls desired state from Git. Additional GitOps advantages: (1) Drift detection — any manual change is auto-reverted, (2) Audit trail — git log = full history of who changed what and when, (3) Disaster recovery — point ArgoCD/Flux at a new cluster and it recreates everything from Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Push (Traditional CI/CD) vs Pull (GitOps)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PUSH MODEL (Traditional CI/CD):                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐  kubectl apply -f k8s/  ┌────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  CI/CD   │ ──────────────────────▶ │ Kubernetes Cluster │    │
  │  │  (Jenkins│    "Here, run this!"    │                    │    │
  │  │   Actions)│    🔴 External creds    │                    │    │
  │  └──────────┘                         └────────────────────┘    │
  │  ❌ Cluster credentials outside cluster                          │
  │  ❌ No drift detection                                           │
  │  ❌ Push failures need manual retry                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PULL MODEL (GitOps — ArgoCD/Flux):                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐  git push           ┌────────────────────┐        │
  │  │ Developer│ ──────────────────▶ │ Kubernetes Cluster │        │
  │  └──────────┘                     │ ┌────────────────┐ │        │
  │                                   │ │ GitOps Agent   │ │        │
  │  ┌──────────┐   Pull every 3min   │ │ "Is Git still  │ │        │
  │  │ Git Repo │◀────────────────────│ │ the same?"     │ │        │
  │  └──────────┘                     │ │ ──syncs if──▶  │ │        │
  │                                   │ │   needed       │ │        │
  │                                   │ └────────────────┘ │        │
  │                                   └────────────────────┘        │
  │  ✅ Agent INSIDE cluster (no external creds)                     │
  │  ✅ Auto drift detection + remediation                           │
  │  ✅ Git = disaster recovery source                               │
  │  ✅ git log = complete audit trail                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, using GitOps means: no CI system has cluster credentials (more secure), every deployment is in git history (auditable), and if the cluster is destroyed, ArgoCD can recreate everything from Git in minutes. KCNA key: Push = CI pushes to cluster. Pull = agent pulls from Git. GitOps = Pull + Drift Detection.

Section 3.3: Helm & Kustomize (Q159-Q164)

App Delivery Q159 of 200
What is Helm and why is it called the "package manager for Kubernetes"?
A) Helm is a Kubernetes controller that manages resource limits for all pods in a namespace
B) Helm packages Kubernetes resources into "Charts" (like .deb or .rpm packages) — you can helm install a full application stack (Django + PostgreSQL + Redis + Ingress) with one command, parameterize it with values.yaml, and share it via Helm repositories
C) Helm is a monitoring tool that collects metrics from all pods and displays them on a dashboard
D) Helm is a Kubernetes distribution that replaces kubeadm for cluster installation
✅ Correct Answer: B

Helm is the de facto package manager for Kubernetes, analogous to apt (Debian), yum (RHEL), or brew (macOS). It packages all Kubernetes YAML manifests (Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Ingress, PVC) into a single redistributable unit called a Chart. Charts are templated with Go templates — the same chart can deploy to dev, staging, and production with different values. Charts are shared via Helm repositories (like Docker Hub but for Helm charts) or OCI registries.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Helm — Package Manager for Kubernetes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM: Package Manager Analogy                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┬────────────────────┬────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Domain  │  Package Manager   │  Package / Command          │  │
  │  ├──────────┼────────────────────┼────────────────────────────┤  │
  │  │ Linux    │ apt / yum          │ .deb / .rpm                 │  │
  │  │ Python   │ pip                │ .whl / requirements.txt     │  │
  │  │ Node.js  │ npm / yarn         │ package.json                │  │
  │  │ K8s      │ Helm               │ Chart (templates+values)    │  │
  │  └──────────┴────────────────────┴────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  HELM WORKFLOW:                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  1. DEVELOPER creates a Chart:                            │   │
  │  │     helm create anihpj-chart                              │   │
  │  │     → Generates: Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/      │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  2. PACKAGE and SHARE:                                    │   │
  │  │     helm package ./anihpj-chart                           │   │
  │  │     helm push anihpj-chart-1.0.0.tgz oci://ghcr.io       │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  3. USER installs:                                        │   │
  │  │     helm install anihpj oci://ghcr.io/anihpj-chart \      │   │
  │  │       --set django.replicas=3 \                           │   │
  │  │       --set postgres.password=secret123                   │   │
  │  │     → Deploys Django+PostgreSQL+Redis+Ingress in ONE CMD! │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, a single Helm chart packages Django API, PostgreSQL StatefulSet, Redis, and Ingress into one installable unit. Values like replica count, image tag, and DB password are parameterized via values.yaml. KCNA key: Helm = package manager for K8s. Chart = template + values.

App Delivery Q160 of 200
What are the key files in a Helm chart directory and what is each used for?
A) Dockerfile, docker-compose.yaml, .dockerignore — Helm charts use Docker's file format
B) main.go, go.mod, go.sum — Helm charts are written in Go
C) Chart.yaml (metadata: name, version, appVersion), values.yaml (default configuration), templates/ (Go-templated Kubernetes YAML files), charts/ (sub-chart dependencies) — Helm renders templates with values to produce final Kubernetes manifests
D) package.json, node_modules/, index.js — Helm uses the Node.js package format
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Helm chart has a standard directory structure: Chart.yaml (metadata: chart name, version, description, appVersion), values.yaml (default configuration values — what users customize), templates/ (directory containing Go-templated Kubernetes YAML files — use {{ .Values.replicaCount }} for parameterization), charts/ (directory for sub-charts/dependencies), and optionally README.md, LICENSE, .helmignore. Helm merges the templates with values to render final Kubernetes manifests.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Helm Chart Structure — Template Rendering
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM CHART STRUCTURE: anihpj-chart/                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ├── Chart.yaml              ← Metadata                         │
  │  │   apiVersion: v2                                               │
  │  │   name: anihpj                                               │
  │  │   version: 1.0.0                                              │
  │  │   appVersion: "1.5.0"      ← Application version             │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ├── values.yaml             ← Default configuration            │
  │  │   django:                                                     │
  │  │     replicas: 3                                               │
  │  │     image: anihpj/django                                      │
  │  │     tag: "1.5.0"                                              │
  │  │   postgres:                                                   │
  │  │     storage: 10Gi                                             │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  ├── templates/              ← Go-templated K8s manifests        │
  │  │   ├── deployment.yaml     ← Template for Django Deployment    │
  │  │   │   replicas: {{ .Values.django.replicas }}                 │
  │  │   │   image: {{ .Values.django.image }}:{{ .Values.django.tag│
  │  │   ├── service.yaml        ← Template for Service              │
  │  │   ├── configmap.yaml                                          │
  │  │   ├── ingress.yaml                                            │
  │  │   └── _helpers.tpl        ← Reusable template functions       │
  │  │                                                               │
  │  └── charts/                ← Sub-chart dependencies             │
  │      ├── postgresql-12.1.0.tgz                                    │
  │      └── redis-18.0.0.tgz                                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  RENDERING: helm template ./anihpj-chart --values prod-values.yaml│
  │  → Templates + Values = Final Kubernetes YAML manifests          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Helm chart's values.yaml defaults to 3 Django replicas. For development, override with --set django.replicas=1. For production, use a production-values.yaml file with 5 replicas and larger PostgreSQL storage. KCNA key: Chart.yaml=metadata, values.yaml=config, templates/=parameterized YAML.

App Delivery Q161 of 200
What are the key Helm release lifecycle commands and what does each do?
A) helm create, helm delete, helm restart — similar to Docker commands
B) helm push, helm pull, helm tag — similar to Docker registry commands
C) helm init, helm deploy, helm stop — Helm v2 Tiller commands
D) helm install (deploy a new release), helm upgrade (update an existing release), helm rollback (revert to a previous revision), helm uninstall (remove the release), helm list (show installed releases) — each release has a revision number
✅ Correct Answer: D

Helm manages the release lifecycle: helm install myapp ./chart creates release revision 1, helm upgrade myapp ./chart --set replicas=5 creates revision 2, helm rollback myapp 1 reverts to revision 1, helm uninstall myapp removes everything. Each upgrade creates a new revision (stored as Secrets in the release namespace), enabling easy rollback. helm history myapp shows all revisions with status (deployed, superseded, failed).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Helm Release Lifecycle — Revisions & Rollback
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM RELEASE LIFECYCLE                                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  helm install anihpj ./anihpj-chart \                            │
  │    --set django.replicas=3                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  REVISION 1 (deployed)                                    │   │
  │  │  Secret: sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v1                     │   │
  │  │  → Deploys Django (3 replicas) + PostgreSQL + Redis       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         │ helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj-chart \                   │
  │         │   --set django.replicas=5                              │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  REVISION 2 (deployed)                                    │   │
  │  │  Secret: sh.helm.release.v1.anihpj.v2                     │   │
  │  │  → Updates Django to 5 replicas                           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         │ OOPS! 5 replicas crashed. Rollback!                    │
  │         │ helm rollback anihpj 1                                 │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  REVISION 3 (deployed) = REVISION 1 config!               │   │
  │  │  → Django back to 3 replicas ✅                            │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  HELM COMMANDS:                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ helm list        │ Show all installed releases            │   │
  │  │ helm status NAME │ Show release status + resources        │   │
  │  │ helm history NAME│ Show all revisions                     │   │
  │  │ helm get values  │ Show values used for a release         │   │
  │  │ helm get manifest│ Show the rendered Kubernetes YAML      │   │
  │  └─────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if a Helm upgrade sets too many replicas and pods crash, simply run helm rollback anihpj 1 to instantly revert. KCNA key: install → upgrade → rollback → uninstall. Each upgrade = new revision.

App Delivery Q162 of 200
What is the key architectural difference between Helm and Kustomize?
A) Helm is for production, Kustomize is for development only
B) Helm uses Go TEMPLATES ({{ .Values.x }}) with a values.yaml — the same chart renders DIFFERENT YAML for different inputs. Kustomize uses OVERLAYS — you start with BASE YAML and PATCH it, producing new YAML by merging overlays. No template syntax
C) Kustomize is a Helm plugin — it requires Helm to function
D) There is no difference — they both produce Kubernetes YAML from parameters
✅ Correct Answer: B

Helm uses Go templates — YAML files contain {{ .Values.replicaCount }} placeholders that get replaced when you run helm install. Kustomize uses a patch/overlay approach — you write pure YAML (no template syntax) and Kustomize applies strategic merge patches or JSON patches to produce environment-specific configurations. Helm is better for packaging and sharing, Kustomize is better for managing environment variations (dev/staging/prod) of the same application.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Helm (Templates) vs Kustomize (Patches/Overlays)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  HELM: TEMPLATE APPROACH                                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  deployment.yaml (TEMPLATE):                                     │
  │    replicas: {{ .Values.replicas }}                              │
  │    image: {{ .Values.image }}:{{ .Values.tag }}                  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  + values.yaml: replicas: 3                                      │
  │  + values-prod.yaml: replicas: 5                                 │
  │                                                                  │
  │  → Renders DIFFERENT YAML depending on values input              │
  │  → Must learn Go template syntax (if/range/with/pipes)           │
  │  → Great for: packaging + sharing + parameterization             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUSTOMIZE: PATCH/OVERLAY APPROACH                               │
  │                                                                  │
  │  base/deployment.yaml (PURE YAML — no templates!):               │
  │    replicas: 1                                                   │
  │    image: anihpj/django:latest                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  overlays/production/patch.yaml (PURE YAML):                     │
  │    spec:                                                         │
  │      replicas: 5          ← Patches base from 1 to 5             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  → Merges base + overlay = FINAL YAML                            │
  │  → No template syntax — 100% YAML                                │
  │  → Great for: environment variations (dev/staging/prod)          │
  │  → Built into kubectl: kubectl apply -k overlays/production      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Helm is used to package and share the full stack, while Kustomize overlays handle differences between dev (1 replica, debug=True) and production (5 replicas, debug=False). KCNA key: Helm = templates + values. Kustomize = base + patches. Both produce K8s YAML.

App Delivery Q163 of 200
How do Kustomize overlays work for managing dev, staging, and production environments?
A) A base/ directory contains common configuration, and overlays/{dev,staging,prod}/ each contain a kustomization.yaml that references the base and applies patches — kubectl apply -k overlays/production generates the final config by merging base + production patches
B) You create three completely separate sets of YAML files for each environment
C) Kustomize uses environment variables to switch between configurations at runtime
D) Overlays are Helm-specific — Kustomize doesn't support this pattern
✅ Correct Answer: A

Kustomize's overlay model is its core strength. You define a base directory with the common Kubernetes resources (deployment, service, configmap). Then each environment (overlays/development, overlays/staging, overlays/production) has its own kustomization.yaml that references the base and applies strategic merge patches to change specific fields like replica count, image tag, resource limits, or environment variables. No YAML duplication — all environments share the same base.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kustomize Overlays — One Base, Many Environments
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUSTOMIZE DIRECTORY STRUCTURE                                   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  k8s/                                                            │
  │  ├── base/                       ← Common configuration          │
  │  │   ├── kustomization.yaml                                       │
  │  │   │   resources:                                              │
  │  │   │   - deployment.yaml                                       │
  │  │   │   - service.yaml                                          │
  │  │   │   - configmap.yaml                                        │
  │  │   ├── deployment.yaml         ← replicas: 1 (default)         │
  │  │   ├── service.yaml                                             │
  │  │   └── configmap.yaml                                           │
  │  │                                                                │
  │  └── overlays/                   ← Environment-specific          │
  │      ├── development/                                             │
  │      │   ├── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │      │   │   bases:                                               │
  │      │   │   - ../../base                                        │
  │      │   │   patchesStrategicMerge:                               │
  │      │   │   - replica-count.yaml                                │
  │      │   │   - debug-env.yaml                                     │
  │      │   └── replica-count.yaml  ← replicas: 1                    │
  │      │                                                             │
  │      ├── staging/                                                 │
  │      │   ├── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │      │   └── replica-count.yaml  ← replicas: 3                    │
  │      │                                                             │
  │      └── production/                                              │
  │          ├── kustomization.yaml                                   │
  │          └── replica-count.yaml  ← replicas: 5                    │
  │                                                                  │
  │  DEPLOY:                                                          │
  │  kubectl apply -k overlays/development   ← 1 replica              │
  │  kubectl apply -k overlays/production    ← 5 replicas             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the base defines the Django Deployment (1 replica, debug=False). The development overlay adds debug=True, staging sets 3 replicas, and production sets 5 replicas with higher resource limits. KCNA key: base = common config. overlays = env-specific patches. kubectl apply -k.

App Delivery Q164 of 200
What are the key fields in a kustomization.yaml file?
A) apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec — same as any Kubernetes resource
B) FROM, RUN, COPY, CMD — Kustomize uses Dockerfile syntax
C) chart, version, appVersion, dependencies — Kustomize uses Helm chart syntax
D) resources (list of YAML files to include), patchesStrategicMerge (overlay patches), images (override image tags), configMapGenerator (auto-create ConfigMaps), secretGenerator (auto-create Secrets), namePrefix/nameSuffix, commonLabels/commonAnnotations
✅ Correct Answer: D

A kustomization.yaml file is Kustomize's configuration file. Key fields: resources (list of base YAML files to include), patchesStrategicMerge (overlay patches that modify specific fields), images (override image name/tag/digest), configMapGenerator (auto-create ConfigMaps from files/literals with content hashes for automatic rollouts), secretGenerator (same for Secrets), namePrefix/nameSuffix (add prefix/suffix to all resource names), commonLabels/commonAnnotations (apply labels/annotations to all resources).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kustomization.yaml — Key Fields Explained
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  overlays/production/kustomization.yaml                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  resources:                  ← Include these YAML files    │   │
  │  │  - ../../base               ← Reference the base          │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  patchesStrategicMerge:      ← Patch specific fields      │   │
  │  │  - replica-count.yaml       ← replicas: 5                 │   │
  │  │  - resource-limits.yaml     ← cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi    │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  images:                     ← Override image tags        │   │
  │  │  - name: anihpj/django                                   │   │
  │  │    newTag: v1.5.0-prod                                   │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  configMapGenerator:         ← Auto-generate ConfigMaps   │   │
  │  │  - name: django-config                                   │   │
  │  │    files:                                                 │   │
  │  │    - application.properties                               │   │
  │  │    → Creates ConfigMap with hash suffix for auto-rollout │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  commonLabels:               ← Applied to ALL resources   │   │
  │  │    environment: production                                │   │
  │  │    team: backend                                          │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  namePrefix: prod-           ← Prefix all resource names  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, configMapGenerator auto-creates Django ConfigMaps with content hashes — when the config changes, the hash changes → Deployment triggers a rolling restart. KCNA key: resources=what to include, patches=what to change, generators=auto-create ConfigMaps/Secrets.

Section 3.4: Deployment Strategies (Q165-Q170)

App Delivery Q165 of 200
What is a Rolling Update and why is it the default Deployment strategy in Kubernetes?
A) Rolling update replaces ALL pods at once with the new version — it's the fastest strategy
B) Rolling update creates a completely separate stack and switches traffic all at once
C) Rolling update gradually replaces old pods with new pods one at a time (controlled by maxSurge and maxUnavailable), achieving ZERO DOWNTIME — it's the default because it balances safety, resource usage, and speed
D) Rolling update requires a service mesh — it doesn't work with standard Kubernetes Services
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Rolling Update strategy (default in Kubernetes Deployments) replaces pods incrementally. Controlled by two parameters: maxSurge (how many EXTRA pods can be created above desired count — default 25%) and maxUnavailable (how many pods can be UNAVAILABLE during update — default 25%). The Deployment creates a new ReplicaSet, gradually scales it up while scaling down the old one, ensuring no downtime. This is the "slow and safe" default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rolling Update — Gradual, Zero-Downtime Replacement
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROLLING UPDATE: replicas=4, maxSurge=1, maxUnavailable=1        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 1 (initial):                                               │
  │  [v1] [v1] [v1] [v1]   ← Old ReplicaSet, 4 pods running         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 2:                                                        │
  │  [v1] [v1] [v1] [v1] [v2]  ← Create 1 new pod (maxSurge=1)     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 3:                                                        │
  │  [v1] [v1] [v1] [v2]       ← Delete 1 old pod (maxUnavailable=1)│
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 4 (repeat):                                               │
  │  [v1] [v1] [v2] [v2]       ← Gradually shift toward v2          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  FINAL:                                                          │
  │  [v2] [v2] [v2] [v2]       ← All pods updated, zero downtime!   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  KEY PARAMETERS:                                                 │
  │  maxSurge: 1         → At most 1 EXTRA pod during update        │
  │  maxUnavailable: 1   → At most 1 pod can be down during update  │
  │  (Can be absolute numbers or percentages: 25%)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, a rolling update of the Django API with maxUnavailable=1 and maxSurge=1 ensures at least 2 of 3 pods are always serving requests during deployment. KCNA key: rolling update = gradual replacement, zero downtime, controlled by maxSurge + maxUnavailable.

App Delivery Q166 of 200
What is a Blue/Green deployment and what is its main advantage over rolling updates?
A) Blue/Green runs TWO complete stacks simultaneously (Blue=old, Green=new) and switches ALL traffic at once by changing the Service selector — the main advantage is INSTANT rollback (switch the Service back to Blue)
B) Blue/Green is the same as a rolling update but uses different colored labels
C) Blue/Green deploys to half the pods (blue) and the other half (green) simultaneously
D) Blue/Green is a networking concept that requires a CNI plugin with color support
✅ Correct Answer: A

In a Blue/Green deployment, you maintain two identical environments: Blue (current production) and Green (new version). You deploy the new version to Green, test it thoroughly, then switch the Service selector from Blue to Green — ALL traffic instantly moves to Green. The killer advantage: if something goes wrong, you switch the Service back to Blue — instant rollback, no waiting for rolling update reversal. The trade-off: requires 2x resources during deployment.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Blue/Green Deployment — Two Stacks, Instant Switch
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BLUE/GREEN DEPLOYMENT                                           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  BEFORE SWITCH:                                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ BLUE (v1, LIVE)    │     │ GREEN (v2, STANDBY) │            │
  │  │ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ │     │ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ │            │
  │  │ │Pod│ │Pod│ │Pod│ │     │ │Pod│ │Pod│ │Pod│ │            │
  │  │ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ │     │ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ │            │
  │  └──────────┬──────────┘     └──────────┬──────────┘            │
  │             │                           │                        │
  │  Service selector: version=blue ──▶ BLUE pods only              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  AFTER SWITCH (instant!):                                        │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ BLUE (v1, STANDBY)│     │ GREEN (v2, LIVE!)   │            │
  │  │ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ │     │ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐ │            │
  │  │ │Pod│ │Pod│ │Pod│ │     │ │Pod│ │Pod│ │Pod│ │            │
  │  │ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ │     │ └───┘ └───┘ └───┘ │            │
  │  └─────────────────────┘     └──────────┬──────────┘            │
  │                                         │                        │
  │  Service selector: version=green ──▶ GREEN pods only           │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ROLLBACK (also instant!):                                       │
  │  Service selector: version=blue ──▶ Back to v1!                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, Blue/Green is useful for major version bumps (Django 4.x → 5.x) where you want instant rollback capability. On the anih single-node lab, it's harder due to resource constraints — use in multi-node clusters. KCNA key: Blue/Green = two stacks, Service switch, instant rollback, 2x resources.

App Delivery Q167 of 200
How does a Canary deployment differ from Blue/Green and Rolling Update?
A) Canary is the same as Rolling Update — both replace pods gradually
B) Canary sends a SMALL PERCENTAGE of real user traffic to the new version (e.g., 10% v2, 90% v1) and gradually increases it while monitoring metrics — unlike Blue/Green (all-at-once switch) and Rolling Update (pod-level replacement, not traffic-level)
C) Canary deploys only to nodes named "canary" — it's a node-level strategy
D) Canary requires a bird-themed Kubernetes controller — it's a specialized operator
✅ Correct Answer: B

A Canary deployment is a traffic-splitting strategy: you deploy the new version alongside the old one and route a small percentage of actual user traffic to the new version (the "canary in the coal mine"). You monitor error rates, latency, and user metrics on the canary. If everything looks good, gradually increase: 10% → 25% → 50% → 100%. If problems appear, shift traffic back to 0% for the new version. Requires a Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd) or Ingress controller with traffic splitting — not built into standard Kubernetes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Canary Deployment — Gradual Traffic Shifting
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CANARY DEPLOYMENT: Traffic-Based Gradual Rollout                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PHASE 1: 10% canary (monitor for 30 minutes)                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Users ──▶ Service Mesh ──┬── 90% ──▶ v1 (stable)       │   │
  │  │                           └── 10% ──▶ v2 (canary)       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PHASE 2: 25% canary (error rates look good → increase)          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Users ──▶ Service Mesh ──┬── 75% ──▶ v1                │   │
  │  │                           └── 25% ──▶ v2                │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  PHASE 3: 50% → 100% (full rollout)                              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  EMERGENCY: If v2 error rate spikes → shift v2 to 0% instantly!  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ STRATEGY       │ WHAT IT DOES │ ROLLBACK                 │   │
  │  ├────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Rolling Update │ Replace pods │ Slow (reverse rollout)   │   │
  │  │ Blue/Green     │ Switch all   │ Instant (switch back)    │   │
  │  │ Canary         │ Traffic %    │ Instant (shift to 0%)    │   │
  │  └────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, a canary deployment of the Django API routes 10% of users to the new version. If error rates stay low on the canary, increase to 100%. KCNA key: Canary = traffic % based. Needs Service Mesh or Ingress. Rollback = shift traffic to 0%.

App Delivery Q168 of 200
What is A/B testing and how does it differ from a canary deployment?
A) A/B testing is the same as canary — the terms are interchangeable
B) A/B testing tests two different infrastructure configurations, not application features
C) A/B testing is a Kubernetes feature that randomly routes traffic between two Services
D) A/B testing routes users by SPECIFIC CRITERIA (header, cookie, user ID) to compare FEATURES (not versions) — e.g., "show new checkout to users with cookie 'beta=true'" — while canary is about safely rolling out a NEW VERSION to all users gradually
✅ Correct Answer: D

A/B testing (or split testing) is about comparing features, not versions. You route specific users to different versions based on rules (HTTP headers, cookies, user agent, geolocation). For example: "users with cookie beta=true see the new checkout flow." The goal is to measure which feature performs better (conversion rate, engagement). Canary is about safely rolling out a new version to all users, with the goal of catching bugs before full rollout. Both use traffic splitting but serve different purposes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
A/B Testing vs Canary — Different Goals
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CANARY DEPLOYMENT (Risk mitigation):                            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  "Is v2 stable?"                                          │   │
  │  │  10% of ALL users → v2                                    │   │
  │  │  Monitor: error rate, latency, CPU                        │   │
  │  │  Goal: Safe rollout, catch bugs early                     │   │
  │  │  Eventually: 100% of users → v2                           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  A/B TESTING (Feature comparison):                               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  "Does the new checkout flow increase conversions?"        │   │
  │  │  Users with header X-Canary: beta → v2 (new checkout)    │   │
  │  │  Users WITHOUT header → v1 (old checkout)                 │   │
  │  │  Monitor: conversion rate, revenue, engagement            │   │
  │  │  Goal: Data-driven feature decisions                      │   │
  │  │  May run for DAYS/WEEKS with both versions side-by-side  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ROUTING RULES (Istio VirtualService example for A/B):           │
  │  - match:                                                        │
  │    - headers:                                                    │
  │        cookie:                                                    │
  │          regex: "^(.*;)?beta=true(;.*)?$"                        │
  │    route:                                                        │
  │    - destination: v2 (new feature)                               │
  │  - route:                                                        │
  │    - destination: v1 (default — everyone else)                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, A/B testing could compare a new job listing UI (v2) vs the current one (v1), routing internal beta testers (cookie: beta=true) to v2 while all external users stay on v1. KCNA key: Canary = safe version rollout. A/B = feature comparison by user criteria.

App Delivery Q169 of 200
What are the kubectl rollout commands used to manage a Deployment update?
A) kubectl start-rollout, kubectl stop-rollout, kubectl delete-rollout
B) kubectl upgrade, kubectl downgrade, kubectl revert — similar to Helm commands
C) kubectl rollout status (monitor progress), kubectl rollout history (view revision history), kubectl rollout undo (rollback to previous revision), kubectl rollout pause/resume (pause mid-rollout for canary-style verification)
D) kubectl rollout is deprecated — use kubectl apply instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

kubectl rollout provides deployment lifecycle management: rollout status deployment/django watches the rollout progress in real-time, rollout history deployment/django lists all revisions with change-cause (if using --record or kubernetes.io/change-cause annotation), rollout undo deployment/django instantly rolls back to the previous revision, rollout undo deployment/django --to-revision=3 rolls back to a specific revision, rollout pause/resume pauses a mid-flight rollout (useful for canary testing with the native approach).

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl rollout — Deployment Lifecycle Commands
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl rollout COMMANDS                                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # Start a rolling update                                 │   │
  │  │  kubectl set image deploy/django django=anihpj/django:v2 │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Watch rollout progress (blocks until done)             │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout status deploy/django                     │   │
  │  │  Waiting for rollout to finish: 2 of 3 updated...        │   │
  │  │  deployment "django" successfully rolled out              │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # View revision history                                  │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout history deploy/django                    │   │
  │  │  REVISION  CHANGE-CAUSE                                   │   │
  │  │  1         initial deployment                             │   │
  │  │  2         set image to v1.1.0                            │   │
  │  │  3         set image to v2.0.0                            │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Rollback (undo last deployment)                        │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout undo deploy/django                       │   │
  │  │  → Creates REVISION 4 = REVISION 2 config                 │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Rollback to specific revision                          │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout undo deploy/django --to-revision=1       │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Pause/resume (canary-like control)                     │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout pause deploy/django                      │   │
  │  │  kubectl rollout resume deploy/django                     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if a rolling update of Django v1.5 causes 500 errors, immediately run kubectl rollout undo deploy/django-api to roll back. The Deployment controller handles the rollback as another rolling update in reverse. KCNA key: rollout status/history/undo/pause/resume — 5 essential Deployment lifecycle commands.

App Delivery Q170 of 200
When should you use each deployment strategy for anihpj/jobpost releases?
A) Rolling Update for routine patch releases (v1.4.1 → v1.4.2), Blue/Green for major database migrations (v1.x → v2.0 with instant rollback), Canary for risky changes where you want to test on 10% traffic first, A/B for UI/UX experiments
B) Always use Rolling Update — it works for everything
C) Canary for everything — it's the safest strategy in all cases
D) Blue/Green for all deployments because instant rollback is always better
✅ Correct Answer: A

There's no "one size fits all" — each strategy has a sweet spot. Rolling Update: best for routine patches, minor version bumps, non-breaking changes — simple, resource-efficient, default. Blue/Green: best for major releases with database migrations where instant rollback is critical — costs 2x resources during deployment. Canary: best for risky changes where you want to test on a small % of real users before full rollout — requires Service Mesh or Ingress. A/B Testing: best for comparing features, not versions — UX/product decisions, not deployment safety.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Deployment Strategy Decision Matrix for anihpj/jobpost
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DECISION MATRIX: Which strategy for which scenario?              │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ SCENARIO           │ STRATEGY      │ WHY                  │   │
  │  ├────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Bug fix, no schema │ Rolling       │ Simple, resource-    │   │
  │  │ change (v1.4.1→    │ Update        │ efficient, default   │   │
  │  │ v1.4.2)            │               │                      │   │
  │  ├────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Django 4→5 upgrade │ Blue/Green    │ Instant rollback if  │   │
  │  │ + DB migration     │               │ migration fails      │   │
  │  ├────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ New caching layer  │ Canary        │ Test on 10% users,   │   │
  │  │ (risky perf change)│               │ monitor latency      │   │
  │  ├────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ New search UI      │ A/B Testing   │ Compare old vs new   │   │
  │  │ (UX experiment)    │               │ UI conversion rates  │   │
  │  ├────────────────────┼───────────────┼──────────────────────┤   │
  │  │ Emergency hotfix   │ Rolling       │ Fastest safe rollback│   │
  │  │ (security patch)   │ Update        │ via kubectl undo     │   │
  │  └────────────────────┴───────────────┴──────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  GOLDEN RULE: Start simple (Rolling Update), add complexity      │
  │  (Blue/Green, Canary) only when the risk justifies it.           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost on the 6-cluster lab: use Rolling Update on the kubeadm cluster for daily development, Canary on the kind-cl1 cluster when testing risky changes, and Blue/Green on the production cluster for major releases with database migrations. KCNA key: match strategy to risk — routine=rolling, risky=canary, major=blue/green.

Section 3.5: Application Debugging & Troubleshooting (Q171-Q176)

App Delivery Q171 of 200
A running container lacks the tools you need for debugging (no curl, no netcat). How can you debug it without modifying the container image?
A) Stop the container, modify the Dockerfile to add tools, rebuild, and redeploy
B) Use kubectl debug with an ephemeral container — it attaches a temporary debug container (with all your tools) to a running pod without restarting it. The ephemeral container shares the pod's network and process namespace
C) SSH into the node and use nsenter to attach to the container's namespace
D) Run kubectl exec with --debug flag — it automatically downloads and installs debug tools
✅ Correct Answer: B

kubectl debug with ephemeral containers (beta since 1.23, stable 1.25) is the modern way to debug distroless or minimal containers. It attaches a temporary debug container (with tools like curl, netcat, tcpdump, strace) to a running pod without restarting it. The ephemeral container shares the target pod's process namespace (see processes in target container) and network namespace (use same IP to test connectivity). This is a game-changer for distroless images that have no shell or tools.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl debug — Ephemeral Debug Containers
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBECTL DEBUG WITH EPHEMERAL CONTAINERS                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  SCENARIO: Django pod running distroless image (no shell/tools)  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Pod: django-app-xxx                                     │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────────────┐  │   │
  │  │  │ django (distroless)│  │ debug-container (EPHEMERAL) │  │   │
  │  │  │ PID: 1             │  │ PID: 42                     │  │   │
  │  │  │ NO shell! NO curl! │  │ HAS: curl, netcat, strace, │  │   │
  │  │  │ NO debug tools!    │  │       tcpdump, python, etc. │  │   │
  │  │  │                    │  │                             │  │   │
  │  │  │ Network: 10.244.1.5│  │ Network: 10.244.1.5 (SAME!)│  │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────────────────┘  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  COMMANDS:                                                       │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # Attach ephemeral container with networking tools       │   │
  │  │  kubectl debug -it django-app-xxx \                       │   │
  │  │    --image=nicolaka/netshoot \    ← Debug toolbox image  │   │
  │  │    --target=django                ← Share process NS     │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Now inside debug container:                            │   │
  │  │  curl localhost:8000/healthz   ← Same IP as django!      │   │
  │  │  netstat -tlnp                  ← See django's ports      │   │
  │  │  strace -p 1                    ← Trace django process!   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django container uses a distroless image (no shell). Use kubectl debug -it django-app --image=nicolaka/netshoot --target=django to attach a debug container for network/pod connectivity testing. KCNA key: kubectl debug = ephemeral containers, no pod restart, shares network & process namespace.

App Delivery Q172 of 200
You need to copy a configuration file FROM a running container to your local machine for inspection. What command do you use?
A) kubectl cp pod-name:/path/to/file ./local-file — copies files bidirectionally between a pod and local filesystem (pod→local or local→pod), similar to docker cp
B) kubectl scp — uses SSH protocol to copy files
C) kubectl download — streams the file over HTTP
D) You must mount a PersistentVolume to transfer files — kubectl has no file copy command
✅ Correct Answer: A

kubectl cp copies files and directories to and from containers. Syntax: kubectl cp <source> <destination> where either source or destination can be <pod-name>:<path>. For multi-container pods, use -c <container-name>. It's a wrapper around kubectl exec tar — the source is tar'd, copied, and untar'd at the destination. Useful for: copying out config files for inspection, copying in diagnostic scripts, extracting log files.

📝 Detailed Explanation
kubectl cp — Bidirectional File Transfer
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBECTL CP USAGE PATTERNS                                       │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # FROM pod TO local                                      │   │
  │  │  kubectl cp django-app-xxx:/etc/django/settings.py \      │   │
  │  │    ./settings.py                                          │   │
  │  │  → Copies Django settings from container to laptop        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # FROM local TO pod                                      │   │
  │  │  kubectl cp ./debug-script.py \                           │   │
  │  │    django-app-xxx:/tmp/debug-script.py                    │   │
  │  │  → Copies diagnostic script into the container            │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Multi-container pod                                    │   │
  │  │  kubectl cp django-app-xxx:/var/log/nginx/access.log \    │   │
  │  │    ./access.log -c nginx                                  │   │
  │  │  → Copies from nginx sidecar container                    │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Copy entire directory                                  │   │
  │  │  kubectl cp django-app-xxx:/app/static ./static-files     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  INTERNALS: kubectl cp = kubectl exec tar cf - ... | tar xf -   │
  │  (tars files in container, pipes to local tar extraction)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, use kubectl cp django-app:/etc/django/settings.py ./settings.py to inspect the runtime configuration without exec'ing into the container. KCNA key: kubectl cp = tar-based file copy between pod and local. Use -c for multi-container.

App Delivery Q173 of 200
Your Django pod has an Nginx sidecar for serving static files. How do you view logs specifically from the Nginx container?
A) kubectl logs pod-name — this automatically streams logs from all containers
B) kubectl exec pod-name -- cat /var/log/nginx/access.log — exec into the pod and read the log file
C) kubectl logs pod-name -c nginx — the -c flag specifies which container in a multi-container pod; use --all-containers to see logs from ALL containers at once
D) kubectl logs pod-name --sidecar — the --sidecar flag automatically selects sidecar containers
✅ Correct Answer: C

In a multi-container pod, kubectl logs requires the -c <container-name> flag to specify which container's logs to retrieve. Without it, kubectl asks "which container?" if there are multiple. Use --all-containers=true to stream logs from ALL containers simultaneously (useful for correlating sidecar logs). The pod's spec.containers[].name values are the container names you pass to -c.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Container Pod Logging — Specifying Which Container
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MULTI-CONTAINER POD: django-app                                │
  │                                                                  │
  │  spec:                                                           │
  │    containers:                                                   │
  │    - name: django             ← python manage.py runserver      │
  │      image: anihpj/django                                       │
  │    - name: nginx              ← nginx -g "daemon off;"          │
  │      image: nginx:alpine                                        │
  │                                                                  │
  │  LOG COMMANDS:                                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  # Django logs only                                       │   │
  │  │  kubectl logs django-app-xxx -c django                    │   │
  │  │  → [INFO] Starting Django development server...           │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Nginx logs only                                        │   │
  │  │  kubectl logs django-app-xxx -c nginx                     │   │
  │  │  → 10.244.1.5 - - [12/Jun/2026] "GET /static/..."        │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # ALL containers at once                                 │   │
  │  │  kubectl logs django-app-xxx --all-containers=true        │   │
  │  │  → Shows django logs + nginx logs interleaved             │   │
  │  │                                                           │   │
  │  │  # Follow + previous + container in one command           │   │
  │  │  kubectl logs -f django-app-xxx -c django --previous      │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, the Django+Nginx sidecar pod requires kubectl logs django-app -c nginx to see Nginx access logs and kubectl logs django-app -c django for Django application logs. KCNA key: -c = which container, --all-containers = all at once.

App Delivery Q174 of 200
Your pod is in CrashLoopBackOff. What is the complete debugging sequence to find the root cause?
A) Just restart the pod — CrashLoopBackOff is usually a transient issue
B) kubectl delete pod and hope the new one doesn't crash
C) Check the node logs — pod crashes are always caused by node resource issues
D) kubectl describe pod (check Events + Last State reason) → kubectl logs --previous (see crash output) → check exit code (137=OOMKilled, 1=app error, 143=SIGTERM) → kubectl get events (wider context) → fix root cause (increase memory, fix config, etc.)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The complete CrashLoopBackOff debugging sequence: (1) kubectl describe pod → check "Last State" for the exit reason (Error, OOMKilled, Completed) and "Events" for timeline, (2) kubectl logs --previous → see the crashed container's output (the error message!), (3) interpret exit codes: 0=success, 1=app error, 137=SIGKILL (OOMKilled=128+9), 143=SIGTERM, (4) kubectl get events for wider cluster context (was the node under pressure?), (5) fix root cause and verify with kubectl logs -f.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CrashLoopBackOff — Complete Diagnostic Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEBUGGING SEQUENCE: CrashLoopBackOff                             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 1: kubectl describe pod django-app-xxx                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Last State:     Terminated                               │   │
  │  │    Reason:       Error                                    │   │
  │  │    Exit Code:    1          ← Application error           │   │
  │  │    Started:      Thu, 12 Jun 2026 10:30:00               │   │
  │  │    Finished:     Thu, 12 Jun 2026 10:30:05  (5s!)        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 2: kubectl logs django-app-xxx --previous                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Traceback (most recent call last):                        │   │
  │  │    File "manage.py", line 22, in                   │   │
  │  │  django.core.exceptions.ImproperlyConfigured:             │   │
  │  │    The SECRET_KEY setting must not be empty.              │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 3: Interpret exit code                                     │
  │  Exit 1 = Python exception (missing SECRET_KEY env var!)        │
  │  Exit 137 = OOMKilled (increase memory limit)                   │
  │  Exit 143 = SIGTERM (probe timeout or preStop hook)             │
  │                                                                  │
  │  STEP 4: Fix (add SECRET_KEY to ConfigMap/Secret)                │
  │  STEP 5: Verify: kubectl logs django-app-xxx -f → "Starting..." │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost, if Django crashes with exit code 1, use kubectl logs django-app --previous to see the Python traceback. Exit code 137 means PostgreSQL OOMKilled — increase its memory limit. KCNA key: describe → logs --previous → exit code → fix. 137=OOMKilled, 1=app error.

App Delivery Q175 of 200
A Service should route traffic to your Django pods but connections fail. What is the systematic debugging approach for Service connectivity?
A) Restart all pods — connectivity issues are always transient
B) kubectl get endpoints (check if Service has pod IPs) → verify Service selector matches pod labels → kubectl exec into another pod to test DNS (nslookup service-name) and TCP connectivity (curl service:port) → check NetworkPolicy if Endpoints exist but no connection
C) Delete and recreate the Service — Kubernetes automatically fixes routing
D) Check the CNI plugin logs — Service failures are always caused by network plugin bugs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Service debugging follows a systematic path: (1) kubectl get endpoints <service> — if empty, the Service selector doesn't match any pod labels, (2) kubectl describe service → verify the selector and ports, (3) kubectl get pods --show-labels → confirm pod labels match the Service selector, (4) kubectl exec -it <test-pod> -- nslookup <service-name> → test DNS resolution (CoreDNS), (5) kubectl exec -it <test-pod> -- curl http://<service>:<port> → test actual TCP/HTTP connectivity, (6) kubectl get networkpolicies → if Endpoints exist but connection fails, a NetworkPolicy might be blocking traffic.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Service Connectivity Debugging — Systematic Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SERVICE DEBUGGING FLOWCHART                                     │
  │                                                                  │
  │  kubectl get svc django-api                                      │
  │         │                                                        │
  │         ▼                                                        │
  │  kubectl get endpoints django-api                                │
  │         │                                                        │
  │  ┌──────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ ENDPOINTS EMPTY?                                │            │
  │  │                                                 │            │
  │  │ YES → Check selector matches pod labels:        │            │
